On a decomposition of 𝒑 \boldsymbol{p} -adic Coxeter orbits
Alexander B. Ivanov

Abstract




Abstract.
We analyze the geometry of some
p
-adic Deligne–Lusztig spaces
X_{w}(b)
, introduced by the author in a paper in Crelle, attached to an unramified reductive group
{\mathbf{G}}
over a non-archimedean local field. We prove that when
{\mathbf{G}}
is classical,
b
basic and
w
Coxeter,
X_{w}(b)
decomposes as a disjoint union of translates of a certain integral
p
-adic Deligne–Lusztig space. Along the way we extend some observations of DeBacker and Reeder on rational conjugacy classes of unramified tori to the case of extended pure inner forms and prove a loop version of the Frobenius-twisted Steinberg cross section.

Keywords.
Deligne–Lusztig theory, p-adic reductive groups, Coxeter tori

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification.
20G25, 14M15 (primary), 14F20 (secondary)

Received by the Editors on October 8, 2021, and in final form on April 23, 2023.

Accepted on June 9, 2023.

Mathematisches Institut, Universität Bonn, Endenicher Allee 60, 53115 Bonn, Germany

(new address) Ruhr-Universität Bochum, Fakultät für Mathematik, Universitätsstrasse 150, 44780 Bochum, Germany

e-mail:
a.ivanov@rub.de

The author acknowledges support by the DFG via the Leibniz prize of Peter Scholze.

http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/

1. Introduction

Let
{\mathbf{G}}
be an unramified reductive group over a non-archimedean local field
k
. In
[
Iva23
]

p
-adic Deligne–Lusztig spaces
X_{w}(b)
attached to
{\mathbf{G}}
were constructed. This allows one to apply the rich variety of methods from Deligne–Lusztig theory
[
DL76
]
to the study of representations of the
p
-adic group
{\mathbf{G}}(k)
. Indeed, many results in this direction were obtained recently;
cf.

[
CI23
,
CS17
,
CO21
,
DI20
]
and references therein.

The classical Deligne–Lusztig varieties are smooth schemes of finite type over a finite field. In contrast, the
X_{w}(b)
are sheaves for the arc-topology,
cf.

[
BM21
]
, on perfect algebras over the residue field of
k
. Concerning their representability, the situation is slightly delicate: In many cases it is known that the
X_{w}(b)
are ind-representable;
cf.

[
Iva23
, Theorem C]
.
Also, there are examples where
X_{w}(b)
is known to be an ind-scheme which is not a scheme;
cf.

[
Iva23
, Corollary 10.2]
. On the other hand, for
{\mathbf{G}}={\bf GL}_{n}
,
c
Coxeter and
b
basic,
X_{c}(b)
is a scheme;
cf.

[
CI23
, Proposition 2.6]
. Moreover, the proof of this last fact has led to a quite explicit description of (a cover of)
X_{c}(b)
in this case, eventually allowing one to compute large parts of its cohomology and relate it to the local Langlands correspondences, which was the main task of
[
CI23
,
CI21b
]
.

Motivated by the last sentence, we analyze here the geometry of the spaces
X_{c}(b)
and their natural covers
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
when
{\mathbf{G}}
is a classical group,
(1)
(1)

(1)

We call an unramified
k
-group
classical
if each connected component of its Dynkin diagram is of type
A_{n-1},B_{m},C_{m},D_{m},{}^{2}A_{n-1}
or
{}^{2}D_{m}
; see Definition
5.1
for more details.

c
is a (twisted) Coxeter element and
b
is basic. Under these assumptions our main result, Theorem
1.1
, shows that
X_{c}(b)
and
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
admit natural decompositions as disjoint unions of translates of certain integral level Deligne–Lusztig spaces of Coxeter type. Those integral level spaces are easier to handle. In particular, they are affine schemes, and it follows that
X_{c}(b)
and
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
are schemes, proving
[
Iva23
, Conjecture 1.1]
in this case. Our decomposition result, along with the Mackey-type formula shown in
[
DI20
]
(as well as techniques from
[
Lus76
,
CO21
]
), can be applied—as
in
[
CI23
]
—to study the cohomology of
X_{c}(b)
and
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
and smooth
{\mathbf{G}}(k)
-representations therein.
Beside our main result, we prove some related results on rational conjugacy classes of tori and a version of Steinberg’s cross section; see Section
1.4

below for a summary. Now, we introduce the relevant notation and state our main result, Theorem
1.1
.

1.1. Setup

Let
\breve{k}
denote the completion of a maximal unramified extension of
k
, and let
{\mathcal{O}}_{k},{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}
be the rings of integers of
k,\breve{k}
. Let
\sigma
be the Frobenius of
\breve{k}/k
. Let
{\mathbf{T}}\subseteq{\mathbf{B}}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}
be a centralizer of a maximal split
k
-torus and a
k
-rational Borel subgroup of the unramified group
{\mathbf{G}}
. (Note that
{\mathbf{T}}
is a
k
-rational maximal torus of
{\mathbf{G}}
.) Let
{\mathbf{U}}
, resp.
{\mathbf{U}}^{-}
, be the unipotent radical of
{\mathbf{B}}
, resp. the opposite Borel subgroup. Let
W
be the Weyl group of
{\mathbf{T}}
and
S\subseteq W
the set of simple reflections determined by
{\mathbf{B}}
. Denote by
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbf{G}},\breve{k}}
the Bruhat–Tits building of the adjoint group
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
of
{\mathbf{G}}
over
\breve{k}
and by
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},\breve{k}}
the apartment of
{\mathbf{T}}
therein. Denote by
\sigma
the action induced by Frobenius on
W,S,{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbf{G}},\breve{k}},{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},\breve{k}}
and
{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
(as well as its subgroups).

An element
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
is called
basic
if its Newton point
\nu_{b}
factors through the center of
{\mathbf{G}}
;
cf.

[
Kot85
]
. Twisting
\sigma
by
\Ad(b)
, one gets an inner
k
-form
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
of
{\mathbf{G}}
, see Section
2.1.3
. Similarly, for
w\in W
, twisting the Frobenius on
{\mathbf{T}}(\breve{k})
by
\Ad(w)
, one gets an (outer)
k
-form
{\mathbf{T}}_{w}
of
{\mathbf{T}}
.

The space
X_{w}(b)
, introduced in
[
Iva23
]
, is the intersection of
L{\mathcal{O}}(w)\subseteq L({\mathbf{G}}/{\mathbf{B}})^{2}
with the graph of
b\sigma\colon L({\mathbf{G}}/{\mathbf{B}})\rightarrow L({\mathbf{G}}/{\mathbf{B}})
, where
L
denotes the loop functor (see Section
2.1.1
). Similarly, we have its coverings
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)
, where
\bar{w}
lies in
\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
, a certain discrete quotient of the set
F_{w}(\breve{k})\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
of all lifts of
w
;
cf.
Sections
3.4
and
4
. For more details on
X_{w}(b)
and
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)
, see Section
2.2
and Definition
4.1
. The group
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
acts on
X_{w}(b)
, and
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)\times{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(k)
acts on
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)
. The natural map
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)\rightarrow X_{w}(b)
is
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
-equivariant.

An element
c\in W
is called
twisted Coxeter
, or simply
Coxeter
, if any of its reduced expressions contains precisely one element from each
\sigma
-orbit on
S
;
cf.

[
Spr74
, Section 7]
. Usually, we denote by
w
an arbitrary element of
W
, whereas the letter
c
is reserved for Coxeter elements.

We refer to Section
2
for more notation and setup.

1.2. Main result

Let
c\in W
be Coxeter,
\bar{c}\in\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
a lift of
c
and
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
basic. We prove a decomposition result for
X_{c}(b)
and
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
. The space
X_{c}(b)
is always non-empty, and—after an equivariant isomorphism—one can assume that
c
is a special Coxeter element (see Definition
5.2
) and
b
is a lift of
c
. Moreover, in Proposition
6.1
, we will see that either
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)=\varnothing
, or—after an equivariant isomorphism—we may even assume that
b
lies over
\bar{c}
. In the rest of Section
1.2
we work under these simplifying assumptions (which do not result in any loss of generality).

As
b
lies over
c
, the affine transformation
b\sigma
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},\breve{k}}
has precisely one fixed point
{\mathbf{x}}
(this follows from
[
Spr74
, Lemma 7.4]
as
c
is Coxeter).
Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{x}}
be the corresponding parahoric
{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}
-model of
{\mathbf{G}}
. It descends to an
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
-model
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}
of
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
. In particular, the Frobenius
\sigma_{b}:=\Ad(b)\circ\sigma
of
L{\mathbf{G}}
fixes the subgroup
L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
. Here
L
and
L^{+}
denote the functors of (positive) loops; see Section
2.1.1
. We also have the corresponding parahoric model
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}
of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
. Consider the scheme
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
defined by the cartesian diagram

(1.1)

\textstyle{\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

where the lower map is
g\mapsto g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)
and where
({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}
is the closure of
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-}
in
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
. We also consider the quotient for the pro-étale (equivalently, arc-)topology,

(1.2)

X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}=\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}/{\mathbf{T}}_{c}^{\ad}(k),

where
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
is (
1.1
) applied to the parahoric model
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}
of the adjoint group
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
. Then
X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
and
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
are infinite-dimensional affine schemes;
cf.
Proposition
2.2
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{G}}
be an unramified group of classical type. Let
c,\bar{c}
and
b
be as above. Then there exist a
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
-equivariant isomorphism

(1.3)

X_{c}(b)=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}\gamma X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}

and a
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)\times{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)
-equivariant isomorphism

(1.4)

\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)\cong\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}\gamma\dot{X}_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}.

After necessary preparations, Theorem
1.1
will be proven in Sections
6
and
7
. Let us outline the proof. By a
v
-descent argument, (
1.3
) follows from a particular case of (
1.4
) for absolutely almost simple adjoint groups. Showing (
1.4
) in this case presents the main technical difficulty, which is solved in Section
7
by using a special property of the Newton polygons of isocrystals and analyzing each irreducible Dynkin type separately (here the assumption that
c
is Coxeter becomes crucial). Finally, (
1.4
) can be deduced in general from (
1.3
). We expect Theorem
1.1
to hold for all unramified reductive groups
{\mathbf{G}}
.

At this point, let us stress that
p
-adic Deligne–Lusztig spaces are quite different in nature from affine Deligne–Lusztig varieties (say, at Iwahori level). The latter were introduced by Rapoport in
[
Rap05
]
. They are important because their unions appear as special fibers of Rapoport–Zink spaces. Note, for example, that whereas affine Deligne–Lusztig varieties are (perfect) schemes locally of finite type over
\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}
, the
p
-adic Deligne–Lusztig spaces are essentially never of finite type over
\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}
. On the other hand, there is, remarkably, some relation between these two types of objects. In
[
CI21a
]
it was shown that the inverse limit of a certain system of affine Deligne–Lusztig varieties (of increasing level) for the group
{\bf GL}_{n}
is isomorphic to a
p
-adic Deligne–Lusztig space
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
for the same group and a Coxeter element
c
. We expect this result to generalize to other groups, especially in light of the similarity of the decomposition of Coxeter-type spaces
X_{c}(b)
provided by Theorem
1.1
and a very similar-looking decomposition result of He–Nie–Yu
[
HNY22
, Theorem 1.1(3)]
for affine Deligne–Lusztig varieties attached to elements of the affine Weyl group with finite Coxeter part.

Let us also note that Theorem
1.1
implies
[
Iva23
, Conjecture 1.1]
in the following cases.

Corollary 1.2
.

If
{\mathbf{G}}
is an unramified group of classical type,
c\in W
Coxeter,
\bar{c}\in\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
arbitrary and
b
basic, then
X_{c}(b)
and
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
are disjoint unions of affine schemes.

1.3. Quasi-split case

If
b
is
\sigma
-conjugate to
1
, Theorem
1.1
admits a clearer reformulation. Let
c\in W
be Coxeter and
\bar{c}\in\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
. If
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(1)\neq\varnothing
, then a (
i.e.
, any) lift
\dot{c}\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
of
\bar{c}
is
\sigma
-conjugate to
1
(by Corollary
4.6
). Moreover, choosing
\dot{c}
appropriately, we may additionally assume that the (unique and automatically hyperspecial) fixed point
{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{x}}_{\dot{c}}
of
\dot{c}\sigma
on
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},\breve{k}}
lies in
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},k}={\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},\breve{k}}^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
(
cf.
Section
6.4
). In this situation we have the integral level analogues
X_{c}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}(1)
and
\dot{X}_{\dot{c}}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}(1)
of
X_{c}(1)
and
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(1)
, which were introduced in
[
DI20
, Definition 4.1.1]
; see Section
2.2
. In this situation we have the following corollary, proven in Section
6.4
.

Corollary 1.3
.

With notation as above, there are
{\mathbf{G}}(k)
-
(
resp.
{\mathbf{G}}(k)\times{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)
-
)
equivariant isomorphisms

X_{c}(1)\cong\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}^{\ad}_{{\mathbf{x}},1}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}\gamma X_{c}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{x}}}(1)\quad\text{ and }\quad\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(1)\cong\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in{\mathbf{G}}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},1}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}\gamma\dot{X}_{\dot{c}}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{x}}}(1).

1.4. Two related results

In the rest of the introduction we briefly discuss two further themes of this article. First, we review in Section
3
, following DeBacker and Reeder
[
DeB06
,
DR09
,
Ree11
]
, the parametrization of rational conjugacy classes of unramified maximal
k
-tori in pure inner forms of
{\mathbf{G}}
. Moreover, in Sections
3.3
–
3.4
we extend their discussion to all extended pure inner forms and give a description of rational conjugacy classes in terms of
\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
, the discrete parameter space for the spaces
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)
over
X_{w}(b)
. In Section
3.5
we investigate in detail the Coxeter case , where (under some mild assumptions on
{\mathbf{G}}
) it turns out that the set of different covers
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
of
X_{c}(b)
for
b
basic is in canonical bijection with the set of rational conjugacy classes of unramified Coxeter tori in the inner form
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
of
{\mathbf{G}}
; see Corollary
4.7
. Thus the
p
-adic Deligne–Lusztig space
X_{c}(b)
“can see” the difference between the various rational conjugacy classes of tori whose stable class is given by
c
.

Secondly, in Section
5
we show a loop version of the twisted Steinberg cross section. Steinberg’s original result
[
Ste65
, Proposition 8.9]
states roughly that if
\dot{c}\in F_{c}(\breve{k})
is any lift of a Coxeter element
c\in W
, any
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}
-orbit on
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}
for the action given by
g(u)=g^{-1}u\Ad(\dot{c})(g)
contains precisely one point in
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-}
(
k
can be replaced by any field here). In
[
HL12
, Section 3.14(c)]
this is generalized in various respects, in particular to the case where
k
is replaced by a finite field
{\mathbb F}_{q}
and the action is Frobenius-twisted,
i.e.
, given by
g(u)=g^{-1}u\sigma_{\dot{c}}(g)
, where
\sigma
is the (geometric) Frobenius over
{\mathbb F}_{q}
and
\sigma_{\dot{c}}=\Ad(\dot{c})\circ\sigma
. This can be used to prove affineness of classical Deligne–Lusztig varieties attached to Coxeter elements (for example, by combining
[
DL76
, Corollary 1.12]
and
[
HL12
, Section 4.3]
). In our setup, with
k
non-archimedean local, (to perform a reduction step in the proof of Theorem
1.1
) we need the same result for the action of
L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}})
on
L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})
. A part of the proof in
[
HL12
]
can be adapted, but at some point in
[
HL12
]
the Ax--Grothendieck theorem
(2)
(2)

(2)

It states, for an endomorphism
X\rightarrow X
of a scheme of finite type over an algebraically closed field
\Omega
, that if
X(\Omega)\rightarrow X(\Omega)
is injective, then it is also surjective.
is used, which is impossible in our setting (as
L{\mathbf{U}}
is not even a scheme). Therefore, we carry through a different argument, at least for classical groups and special Coxeter elements.

Outline

In Section
2
we introduce more notation, prove a fact about integral
p
-adic Deligne–Lusztig spaces and review the Borovoi fundamental group and
\sigma
-conjugacy classes. In Section
3
we review (and extend) some results on rational/stable conjugacy classes of unramified maximal tori of
{\mathbf{G}}
. In Section
4
we review and extend some results from
[
Iva23
]
about the maps
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)\rightarrow X_{w}(b)
. In Section
5
we prove loop (and integral loop) versions of the twisted Steinberg cross section. This is used in Section
6
to replace
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
(and its integral analogue) by a more tractable object. The main part of the article consists of Sections
6
and
7
, where Theorem
1.1
is proven.

2. Setup and preliminaries

Recall that
{\mathbf{G}}
is an unramified group over the local field
k
. We use the notation from Section
1.1
.

2.1. More notation

Given a group
G
,
g\in G
and any subset
H\subseteq G
, we write
{}^{g}H=gHg^{-1}
. For an abelian group
A
, we write
A_{{\rm tors}}
for its torsion subgroup.

Let
p
be the residue characteristic of
k
. For a ring
R
of characteristic
p
, denote by
\Perf_{R}
the category of perfect algebras over
R
. Denote by
\varpi
a uniformizer of
k
and by
{\mathbb F}_{q}
(resp.
\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}
) the residue field of
k
(resp.
\breve{k}
).

For
R
in
\Perf_{{\mathbb F}_{q}}
, let
{\mathbb W}(R)
be the unique (up to unique isomorphism)
\varpi
-adically complete and separated
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
-algebra in which
\varpi
is not a zero-divisor and which satisfies
{\mathbb W}(R)\,/\varpi{\mathbb W}(R)=R
. In other words, if
W(R)
denotes the
p
-typical Witt-vectors of
k
, then
{\mathbb W}(R)=W(R)\otimes_{W({\mathbb F}_{q})}{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
if
\charac k=0
and
{\mathbb W}(R)=R[\![\varpi]\!]
if
\charac k=p
. Denote by
\phi=\phi_{R}
the Frobenius automorphisms of
{\mathbb W}(R)
,
{\mathbb W}(R)[1/\varpi]
, induced by
x\mapsto x^{q}
on
R
(this notation is only used in the proof of Proposition
5.3
and Section
7
). We have
{\mathbb W}({\mathbb F}_{q})[1/\varpi]=k
and
{\mathbb W}(\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q})[1/\varpi]=\breve{k}
(so that
\phi_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}=\sigma
).

Denote by
k^{\alg}
a fixed algebraic closure of
k
containing the maximal algebraic subextension of
\breve{k}/k
. Denote by
\langle\sigma\rangle\cong\hat{\mathbb Z}
the group of continuous automorphisms of
\breve{k}/k
.

2.1.1. Loop functors

For a
\breve{k}
-scheme
X
, let

LX\colon\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}\longrightarrow\Sets,\quad R\longmapsto X\left({\mathbb W}(R)\left[1/\varpi^{-1}\right]\right)

be the loop space of
X
. If
X
is affine of finite type over
\breve{k}
, then
LX
is representable by an ind-scheme. For an
{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}
-scheme
{\mathcal{X}}
, let

L^{+}{\mathcal{X}}\colon\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}\longrightarrow\Sets,\quad R\longmapsto{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb W}(R))

be the space of positive loops of
{\mathcal{X}}
. Moreover, if
n\geq 1
, let

L^{+}_{n}{\mathcal{X}}\colon\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}\longrightarrow\Sets,\quad R\longmapsto{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb W}(R)/\varpi^{n}{\mathbb W}(R))

be the truncated version of
L^{+}{\mathcal{X}}
. By
[
BGA18
, Proposition-Definition 6.2]
,
L^{+}_{n}{\mathcal{X}}
is a scheme (resp. affine scheme) for arbitrary (resp. affine)
{\mathcal{X}}
, and if
{\mathcal{X}}
is of finite type, then
L^{+}{\mathcal{X}}
is too. Moreover,
L^{+}{\mathcal{X}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}L^{+}_{n}{\mathcal{X}}
, along affine transition maps (
cf.

[
BGA18
, Proposition 8.5]
). Thus, if
{\mathcal{X}}
is a scheme (resp. affine scheme), then
L^{+}{\mathcal{X}}
is too.

If
X
is the base change to
\breve{k}
of a
k
-scheme
X_{0}
, then the functor
LX
carries a natural automorphism, the geometric Frobenius, denoted by
\sigma_{X_{0}}
(or simply by
\sigma
if
X_{0}
is clear from context). Similarly, we have the geometric Frobenius
\sigma=\sigma_{{\mathcal{X}}_{0}}
on
L^{+}{\mathcal{X}}
if the
{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}
-scheme
{\mathcal{X}}
is the base change of an
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
-scheme
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
.

2.1.2. Groups

We write
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
, resp.
{\mathbf{G}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}}
, for the adjoint group, resp. the simply connected cover of the derived group, of
{\mathbf{G}}
. If
{\mathbf{H}}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}
is a closed subgroup of
{\mathbf{G}}
, denote by
{\mathbf{H}}^{\ad}
, resp.
{\mathbf{H}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}}
, its image in
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
, resp. its preimage in
{\mathbf{G}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}}
. We identify the Weyl groups of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
and
{\mathbf{G}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}}
with
W
. We denote by
{\mathbf{Z}}
the center of
{\mathbf{G}}
.

Write
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
, resp.
X^{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
, for the free
{\mathbb Z}
-module of cocharacters, resp. characters, of
{\mathbf{T}}
. Denote by
\Phi\subseteq X^{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
, resp.
\Phi^{\vee}\subseteq X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
, the set of roots, resp. coroots, of
{\mathbf{G}}
; denote by
\Phi^{+}
, resp.
\Delta
, the set of positive, resp. simple, roots, determined by
{\mathbf{B}}
; denote by
s_{\alpha}\in W
the reflection attached to
\alpha\in\Phi
. For
\xi\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
, denote by
\varpi^{\xi}
the image in
{\mathbf{T}}(\breve{k})
of
\varpi
under
\xi
.

Write
{\mathbf{N}}
for the normalizer of
{\mathbf{T}}
. We have the reductive
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
-model
{\mathcal{T}}
of
{\mathbf{T}}
, and
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})
is the maximal compact subgroup of
{\mathbf{T}}(\breve{k})
. The quotient
\widetilde{W}={\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})/{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})
is the
extended affine Weyl group
of
{\mathbf{T}}
in
{\mathbf{G}}
. It is naturally an extension
\widetilde{W}=W\ltimes X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
,
whose splittings (resp.
\sigma
-equivariant splittings)
W\rightarrow\widetilde{W}
correspond to hyperspecial points of
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{G}},\breve{k}}
(resp. of
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{G}},\breve{k}}^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
).

2.1.3. Twisting Frobenius

For
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
, we have the
k
-group
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
, which is defined as the functorial
\sigma
-centralizer of
b
;
cf.

[
RZ96
, 1.12]
or
[
Kot97
, 3.3, Appendix A]
. Then
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)=\{g\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})\colon g^{-1}b\sigma(g)=b\}
. If
b
is basic (
cf.
Section
2.4
), then
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
is an inner form of
{\mathbf{G}}
, we may identify
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(\breve{k})={\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
and we denote by
\sigma_{b}:=\Ad(b)\circ\sigma
the twisted Frobenius on
{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
. We also write
\sigma_{b}
for the corresponding geometric Frobenius on
L{\mathbf{G}}_{b}=L{\mathbf{G}}
.

Suppose that the basic element
b
lies in
{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
and that
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},\breve{k}}
is fixed by the action of
b\sigma
. Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
be the corresponding parahoric model of
{\mathbf{G}}
over
{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}
. Then
\sigma_{b}
fixes
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
, and
L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\subseteq L{\mathbf{G}}
. Thus
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
descends to an
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
-model of
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
, which we denote by
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}
. We have
L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}\otimes_{{\mathbb F}_{q}}\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}=L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
.

Similarly, for
w\in W
, we have the (outer)
\breve{k}/k
-form
{\mathbf{T}}_{w}
of
{\mathbf{T}}
, satisfying
{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(k)=\{t\in{\mathbf{T}}(\breve{k})\colon\Ad\,w(\sigma(t))=t\}
. We may identify
{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(\breve{k})={\mathbf{T}}(\breve{k})
, and we denote by
\sigma_{w}:=\Ad(w)\circ\sigma
the twisted Frobenius on this group, as well as on
L{\mathbf{T}}
.

2.1.4. Topologies

We work with two topologies on
\Perf_{{\mathbb F}_{p}}
: the pro-étale topology,
cf.

[
BS15
]
, and the arc-topology,
cf.

[
BM21
]
. Recall that a map
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
of qcqs schemes is an arc-cover if any immediate specialization in
S
lifts to
S^{\prime}
. By
[
BM21
, Theorem 5.16]
, the arc-topology on perfect qcqs
{\mathbb F}_{p}
-schemes coincides with the canonical topology. Thus any perfect qcqs scheme is an arc-sheaf. In particular, for any qcqs scheme
{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
,
L^{+}{\mathcal{X}}
is an arc-sheaf. For any quasi-projective scheme
X/k
,
LX
is an arc-sheaf by
[
Iva23
, Theorem A]
.

2.1.5. Locally profinite groups

As in
[
Iva23
, Section 4]
, we regard a locally profinite group
H
as a (representable) functor

S\longmapsto C^{0}(|S|,H)

on
\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
. In particular,
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
and
{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(k)
are such functors. If
H
is profinite, this functor is even represented by an affine scheme.

2.1.6. Galois cohomology

For the definition and standard facts about non-abelian cohomology, we refer to
[
Ser94
]
. If
M
is a discrete
\langle\sigma\rangle
-group, where
\langle\sigma\rangle
is as in Section
2.1
, we write
H^{1}(\sigma,M)
for the corresponding first cohomology set. We identify a continuous
1
-cocycle
a\colon\langle\sigma\rangle\rightarrow M
with
a(\sigma)\in M
and write
[a(\sigma)]\in H^{1}(\sigma,M)
for the corresponding cohomology class.

We will write
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})
, resp.
H^{1}(\sigma_{w},{\mathbf{T}})
, instead of
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k}))
, resp.
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(\breve{k}))
. By a theorem of Steinberg
[
Ser94
, Theorem III.1’]
, resp. its extension
[
BS68
, Section 8.6]
if
\charac k>0
, we have
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})=H^{1}(k,{\mathbf{G}})
and
H^{1}(\sigma_{w},{\mathbf{T}})=H^{1}(k,{\mathbf{T}}_{w})
via inflation along
\Gal_{k}\twoheadrightarrow\langle\sigma\rangle
. We will always use the notation
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})
and
H^{1}(\sigma_{w},{\mathbf{T}})
without further comment.

2.2. Review of some
\boldsymbol{p}
-adic Deligne–Lusztig spaces

2.2.1. Spaces
\boldsymbol{X_{w}(b)}
and
\boldsymbol{\dot{X}_{\dot{w}}(b)}

Let
w\in W
,
\dot{w}\in{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
be a lift of
w
and
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
. Denote by
{\mathcal{O}}(w)\subseteq({\mathbf{G}}/{\mathbf{B}})^{2}
(resp.
{\mathcal{O}}(\dot{w})\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}/{\mathbf{U}}
) the
{\mathbf{G}}
-orbit corresponding to
w
(resp.
\dot{w}
) under the Bruhat decomposition. The
p
-adic Deligne–Lusztig spaces

X_{w}(b)
and
\dot{X}_{\dot{w}}(b)
were defined in
[
Iva23
, Definition 8.3]
by the cartesian diagrams of functors on
\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}

\textstyle{X_{w}(b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L{\mathcal{O}}(w)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L(G/B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\id,b\sigma)}
\textstyle{L(G/B)\times L(G/B)}

and

\textstyle{\dot{X}_{\dot{w}}(b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\dot{\mathcal{O}}(\dot{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L(G/U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\id,b\sigma)}
\textstyle{L(G/U)\times L(G/U)\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

where
b\sigma\colon L(G/B)\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,L(G/B)
and
b
acts by left multiplication. Then
X_{w}(b)
and
\dot{X}_{\dot{w}}(b)
are sheaves for the arc-topology (see
[
BM21
]
) on
\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
;
cf.

[
Iva23
, Corollary 8.4]
. There is a natural action of
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
on
X_{w}(b)
and of
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)\times{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(k)
on
\dot{X}_{w}(b)
.

2.2.2. Spaces
\boldsymbol{\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}}
and
\boldsymbol{X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}}

These spaces were introduced in (
1.1
) and (
1.2
). Here we prove their representability.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\{H_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
be an inverse system of finite groups, acting compatibly and freely on an inverse system
\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
of pro-étale
(
resp. arc-
)
sheaves on
\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
. Write
H=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}H_{n}
and
X=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}X_{n}
. The natural map
X/H\rightarrow\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}X_{n}/H_{n}
is an isomorphism, where the quotients are formed for the pro-étale
(
resp. arc-
)
topology.

Proof.

Consider the case of pro-étale topology. The existence of the map is easy. For the injectivity, it is enough to show that the map from the presheaf quotient
R\mapsto X(R)/H(R)
to
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}X_{n}/H_{n}
is injective. Let
R\in\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
. Suppose
s_{1},s_{2}\in X(R)
map to the same element in
(\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}X_{n}/H_{n})(R)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}(X_{n}/H_{n})(R)
. Then for each
n
, the images
s_{1,n}
,
s_{2,n}
of
s_{1},s_{2}
in
X_{n}(R)/H_{n}(R)
coincide (as any quotient presheaf is automatically separated). Thus for each
n\geq 1
,
S_{n}=\{h_{n}\in H_{n}(R)\colon h_{n}s_{1,n}=s_{2,n}\}\neq\varnothing
. This set forms an inverse system. By the freeness of the action, we have
\#S_{n}=1
, and it follows that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}S_{n}\neq\varnothing
. Then
h\in\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}S_{n}\subseteq H(R)
satisfies
hs_{1}=s_{2}
. This proves the injectivity. For the surjectivity, let
s_{n}\in X_{n}(R)/H_{n}(R)
be a compatible collection of elements. For each
n
, find some pro-étale cover
R\rightarrow R_{n}
such that
s_{n}|_{R_{n}}
lifts to
\tilde{s}_{n}\in X_{n}(R_{n})
. As
R\rightarrow R^{\prime}=\mathop{\,\underrightarrow{\lim\,}}_{n}R_{n}
is still a pro-étale cover,
cf.

[
BS15
, proof of Lemma 4.1.8]
, we may replace each
R_{n}
with
R^{\prime}
and
\tilde{s}_{n}
with
\tilde{s}_{n}|_{R^{\prime}}
. Now, we construct an element
\tilde{s}\in X(R^{\prime})
mapping to
(s_{n})_{n\geq 1}
. Starting with
\tilde{s}_{1}
if
n=1
, and working by induction on
n
, suppose that
(\tilde{s}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
is compatible. As the system
(s_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is compatible, we can find some
h_{n+1}\in H_{n+1}(R^{\prime})
such that
h_{n+1}\tilde{s}_{n+1}
maps to
\tilde{s}_{n}
under
X_{n+1}(R^{\prime})\rightarrow X_{n}(R^{\prime})
. Then replace
\tilde{s}_{n+1}
by
h_{n+1}\tilde{s}_{n+1}
, so that
(\tilde{s}_{i})_{i=1}^{n+1}
becomes compatible. We conclude by induction. The argument for the arc-topology is the same, using
[
BM21
, Corollary 2.18]
instead of
[
BS15
, Lemma 4.1.8]

∎

For
n\geq 1
, let
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
be defined by the diagram (
1.1
) with
L^{+}
replaced by
L^{+}_{n}
everywhere. Then all
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
are affine schemes and
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
. We also have the truncated version of (
1.2
): let
{\mathcal{T}}
be the closure of
{\mathbf{T}}
in
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{c}
the
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
-torus, arising by twisting the Frobenius on
{\mathcal{T}}
by
c
. Then put
X_{c,b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}:=\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}/T_{n}
, where
T_{n}:={\mathcal{T}}_{c}^{\ad}({\mathcal{O}}_{k}/\varpi^{n})
.

Proposition 2.2
.

The schemes
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
and
X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}
are affine and perfect. Also,
X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}=\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}/{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)
remains unchanged if one takes the quotient with respect to the arc-topology instead of the pro-étale topology. Moreover,
X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}X_{c,b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}
.

Proof.

The claim for
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
follows from the definition and the affineness of
L^{+}{\mathcal{X}}
for an affine scheme
{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}
;
cf.
Section
2.1.1
. Suppose that
{\mathbf{G}}
is adjoint. For
n\geq 1
, let
T_{n}={\mathcal{T}}_{c}^{\ad}({\mathcal{O}}_{k}/\varpi^{n})
be as above. Note that the finite group
T_{n}
acts freely on the affine scheme
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}
and we have
X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}}=(\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}}/T)_{\proet}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{n}(\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}/T_{n})_{\proet}
by Lemma
2.1
. By
[
Sta14
, Tag 07S7]
, the fppf-quotient
(\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}/T_{n})_{\fppf}
is a scheme, which is even affine (since
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}
is), and
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}\rightarrow(\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}/T_{n})_{\fppf}
is an fppf-torsor. As
T_{n}
is finite and acts freely on
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}
, this is even an étale torsor. From this, it follows that
(\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}/T_{n})_{\fppf}=(\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}}/T_{n})_{\text{\rm\'{e}t}}
, the quotient for the étale topology. In particular, the latter is an affine perfect scheme, hence already a pro-étale and even an arc-sheaf. This implies that the sheafification of the quotient for the arc- and pro-étale topologies agree, as well as the fact that
X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}}
, being the inverse limit of affine perfect schemes, is itself one.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

The map
g\mapsto g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)\colon L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{\bf x}\rightarrow L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{\bf x}
is pro-étale and—by Lang’s theorem applied to all truncations—also surjective. Thus
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
and
X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}
and their truncations are always non-empty. Also, it follows that
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
and
X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}
are infinite-dimensional. The truncations are perfections of finite-dimensional schemes. More precisely, as the Lang map is étale, we have

\dim\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}=\dim X_{c,b,n}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}=\dim L^{+}_{n}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{\bf x}=\ell(c)n,

where
\ell(c)
denotes the length of the Coxeter element
c\in W
.

Conjecture 2.4
.

The scheme
X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}
is an
{\mathbb A}^{\infty}
-bundle over a classical Deligne–Lusztig variety attached to the reductive quotient of the special fiber of
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
.

This is easily verified by hand for
{\mathbf{G}}={\bf GL}_{n}
, using the explicit description in
[
CI21a
]
.

2.2.3. Spaces
\boldsymbol{X_{w}^{{\mathcal{G}}}(1)}
,
\boldsymbol{\dot{X}_{\dot{w}}^{{\mathcal{G}}}(1)}

These spaces only appear in Sections
1.3
and
6.4
. Suppose
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},\breve{k}}^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
is a hyperspecial point, and let
{\mathcal{G}}={\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
be the corresponding reductive
{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}
-model of
{\mathbf{G}}
. The reductive
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
-group
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},1}
is quasi-split, and the closure
{\mathbf{B}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
of
{\mathbf{B}}
in
{\mathcal{G}}
is an
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
-rational Borel subgroup. Moreover,
{\mathcal{G}}
has a Bruhat decomposition, in the sense that
({\mathcal{G}}/{\mathbf{B}}_{{\mathbf{x}}})^{2}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{k}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}
contains locally closed subschemes
{\mathcal{O}}(w)
(
w\in W
), flat over
{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}
, which fiberwise induce the usual Bruhat decomposition. If
{\mathbf{N}}_{{\mathbf{x}}},{\mathbf{U}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
are the closures of
{\mathbf{N}},{\mathbf{U}}
in
{\mathcal{G}}
, one similarly has
{\mathcal{O}}(\dot{w})\subseteq({\mathcal{G}}/{\mathbf{U}}_{{\mathbf{x}}})^{2}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{k}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}
(
\dot{w}\in{\mathbf{N}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})
). Taking
b=1
,
\dot{w}\in{\mathbf{N}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})
and replacing
{\mathbf{G}},{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{U}}
with
{\mathcal{G}},{\mathbf{B}}_{{\mathbf{x}}},{\mathbf{U}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
and
L
with
L^{+}
everywhere in the diagrams in Section
2.2.1
, we obtain integral
p
-adic Deligne–Lusztig spaces
X_{w}^{{\mathcal{G}}}(1)
and
\dot{X}_{\dot{w}}^{{\mathcal{G}}}(1)
, as introduced in
[
DI20
, Definition 4.1.1]
. They are acted on by
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},1}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
and
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},1}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})\times{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
, respectively.

2.3. Fundamental group and the Kottwitz map

The natural map
{\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{T}}
induces an injection
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})\rightarrow X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
, and we identify
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})
with its image. The fundamental group of
{\mathbf{G}}
in the sense of Borovoi
[
Bor98
]
is the group

\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})=X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})/X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}}).

It is independent of the choice of
{\mathbf{T}}
and functorial in
{\mathbf{G}}
, the induced
W
-action on it is trivial, and it admits a Frobenius action, which we denote by
\sigma
. Kottwitz constructed a homomorphism
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}\colon{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
; see
[
Kot97
, Section 7]
and
[
PR08
, Section 2.a.2]
. Reformulated, a classical result of Kottwitz
[
Kot84
, Section 6]
states that
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}
induces an isomorphism
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})\cong\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle,{{\rm tors}}}
. For tori, we have
\pi_{1}(-)=X_{\ast}(-)
. For any
w\in W
, any
k
-rational embedding
{\mathbf{T}}_{w}\rightarrow{\mathbf{G}}
induces the map

X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle,{{\rm tors}}}=H^{1}(\sigma_{w},{\mathbf{T}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})=\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle,{{\rm tors}}},

which is obtained from
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
by taking
\sigma_{w}
- (resp.
\sigma
-)invariants and passing to the torsion subgroup. From the definition of
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
we deduce an exact sequence

(2.1)

X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta_{w}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}\longrightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\longrightarrow 0.

In particular,

(2.2)

\ker\left(H^{1}(\sigma_{w},{\mathbf{T}})\longrightarrow H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})\right)=\im\left(\beta_{w}\right)_{{\rm tors}}

Remark 2.5
.

If
{\mathbf{G}}
is adjoint and
w
Coxeter, then
\beta_{w}=0
and (equivalently)
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}\cong\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
. For split groups, this follows from
[
Spr74
, Corollary 8.3]
. In the non-split case, note that there is some
n\geq 1
such that
\sigma_{w}^{n}=\sigma_{w_{\spl}}
as automorphisms of
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
and
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\Sc})
, where
w_{\spl}
is some (non-twisted) Coxeter element in
W
and
\sigma_{w_{\spl}}:=\Ad(w_{\spl})\circ\sigma^{n}
is formed with respect to the base change of
{\mathbf{G}}
to the unramified extension of
k
of degree
n
. Then, as automorphisms of
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
and of
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\Sc})
,
\sigma_{w_{\spl}}-1
factors through
\sigma_{w}-1
, so that we have a surjection
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}\twoheadrightarrow X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w_{\spl}}\rangle}
and a similar surjection for
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\Sc})
. These surjections form a commutative square with the maps
\beta_{w}
and
\beta_{w_{\spl}}
(as both are induced by the inclusion
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\Sc})\rightarrow X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
). As
\beta_{w_{\spl}}=0
by the split case, it follows that also
\beta_{w}=0
.

By
[
PR08
, Section 5]
we may reinterpret
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}
as a map of ind-schemes
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}\colon L{\mathbf{G}}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
, where we regard
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
as a discrete perfect scheme via Section
2.1.5
. Passing to
\sigma
-coinvariants gives a surjective map

(2.3)

\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}\colon L{\mathbf{G}}\longrightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}.

Finally, let us note that for
w\in W
, we have the exact sequence

(2.4)

1\longrightarrow{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(k)\longrightarrow L{\mathbf{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma_{w}-1}}{{\longrightarrow}}L{\mathbf{T}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\kappa_{w}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}\longrightarrow 1,

where we write
\kappa_{w}
instead of
\kappa_{{\mathbf{T}}_{w}}
. Moreover,
\kappa_{w}
factors through the natural projection
\bar{\kappa}_{w}\colon X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\rightarrow X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}
.

2.4. Newton map and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
-conjugacy classes

Let
{\mathfrak f}\in\Perf_{{\mathbb F}_{q}}
be an algebraically closed field, so that
L={\mathbb W}({\mathfrak f})[1/\varpi]
is an extension of
\breve{k}
. Then
L/k
is equipped with the Frobenius
\sigma
lifting that of
{\mathfrak f}/{\mathbb F}_{q}
and
L^{\sigma}=k
. In
[
Kot85
]
the set

B({\mathbf{G}})=H^{1}(\sigma^{\mathbb Z},{\mathbf{G}}(L))\cong{\mathbf{G}}(L)/\Ad\nolimits_{\sigma}{\mathbf{G}}(L)

of
\sigma
-conjugacy classes of
{\mathbf{G}}(L)
in considered, where
x,y
are
\sigma
-conjugate if there is some
g\in{\mathbf{G}}(L)
with
g^{-1}x\sigma(g)=y
. Denote the
\sigma
-conjugacy class of
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(L)
by
[b]
(or
[b]_{\mathbf{G}}
if
ambiguity is possible). The set
B({\mathbf{G}})
is independent of the choice of
{\mathfrak f}
. To a
\sigma
-conjugacy class
[b]
, one can attach the
Newton point

\nu_{b}\in(W\backslash X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{{\mathbb Q}})^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
. Moreover, the Kottwitz map
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}({\mathfrak f})
from (
2.3
) factors through a map
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}\colon B({\mathbf{G}})\twoheadrightarrow\pi({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
. Then the map

(2.5)

(\nu,\kappa_{\mathbf{G}})\colon B({\mathbf{G}})\longrightarrow(W\backslash X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{{\mathbb Q}})^{\langle\sigma\rangle}\times\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}

is injective. An element
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(L)
is called
basic
if
\nu_{b}
is central in
{\mathbf{G}}
. We denote by
B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}\subseteq B({\mathbf{G}})
the subset of basic classes. There is a canonical injection
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})\hookrightarrow B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}
. In
[
Kot85
]
, the isomorphism
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})\cong\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle,{{\rm tors}}}
was extended to the natural commutative diagram

(2.6)

\textstyle{H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle,{\rm tors}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle},}

where the lower map is the restriction of
\kappa_{{\mathbf{G}}}
to
B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}
.

We recall how to compute the Newton point
\nu_{b}
of
b\in{\mathbf{N}}(L)
; see
e.g.

[
RV14
, Remark 2.3(iii)]
. Let
\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}
be the image of
b
under
{\mathbf{N}}(L)\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{W}
. There is an integer
d>0
such that
\sigma^{d}
acts trivially on
\widetilde{W}
and such that
\mu=\tilde{w}\sigma(\tilde{w})\dots\sigma^{d-1}(\tilde{w})\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
. Then
\nu_{b}
is the image of
d^{-1}\mu
in
W\backslash X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\mathbb Q}
. In particular,
\nu_{b}
only depends on
\tilde{w}
, and it makes sense to define
\nu_{\tilde{w}}=\nu_{b}
. Moreover, we call
\tilde{w}
basic if
\nu_{\tilde{w}}
is central.

Finally, we recall that any
\sigma
-conjugacy class has a representative in
{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
and that the induced surjection
{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})\twoheadrightarrow B({\mathbf{G}})
factors through
\widetilde{W}\twoheadrightarrow B({\mathbf{G}})
;
cf.

[
GHK+10
, Corollary 7.2.2]
for split groups and
[
He14
, Theorem 3.7]
in general. In particular, the Kottwitz homomorphism induces a map
\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}\colon\widetilde{W}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
.

3. Rational and stable conjugacy classes of unramified tori

Recall that two maximal
k
-tori
{\mathbf{T}}_{1},{\mathbf{T}}_{2}
in
{\mathbf{G}}
are
stably conjugate
if there exists a
g\in{\mathbf{G}}(k^{\alg})
such that
{}^{g}({\mathbf{T}}_{1}(k))={\mathbf{T}}_{2}(k)
. If two maximal
k
-tori are rationally conjugate, they are also stably conjugate. Thus any stable conjugacy class breaks up into a (finite) union of rational conjugacy classes. We review from
[
DeB06
,
DR09
,
Ree11
]
how this happens for unramified tori of pure inner forms of
{\mathbf{G}}
. Then we investigate the case of
extended
pure inner forms of
{\mathbf{G}}
. We wish to point out that the description from
[
DeB06
]
in terms of the Bruhat–Tits building covers this case, but we are interested in a slightly different description, which is closely related to the spaces
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)
.

3.1. Stable conjugacy classes

For
b\in{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
, the twisted Frobenius
\sigma_{b}=\Ad\,b\circ\sigma
of
{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
induces one on
W
. Now suppose
b\in{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
is basic. By
[
DeB06
, Lemma 4.3.1]
, there is a natural injection from the set of stable conjugacy classes of unramified maximal
k
-tori in
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
into
H^{1}(\sigma_{b},W)
, which is even a bijection when
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
is quasi-split (
e.g.
,
[b]=[1]
). Identifying
H^{1}(\sigma_{b},W)\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,H^{1}(\sigma,W)
via the natural twisting map
x\mapsto xb
on cocycles (using
[
Ser94
, Proposition I.35 bis]
), we get

(3.1)

\{\text{stable conjugacy classes of $k$-tori in ${\mathbf{G}}_{b}$}\}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow H^{1}(\sigma,W),

mapping the class of
{\mathbf{T}}(\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}_{b})
to the image of
b
in
H^{1}(\sigma,W)
.

Definition 3.1
.

For
b\in{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
basic and
w\in W
, denote by
{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},w)
the
stable conjugacy class
of all unramified maximal
k
-tori in
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
, which corresponds to
[w]\in H^{1}(\sigma,W)
under (
3.1
).

The elements of
{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},w)
are precisely the images of
k
-embeddings
{\mathbf{T}}_{w}\rightarrow{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
.

Remark 3.2
.

We recall the construction from the proof of
[
DeB06
, Lemma 4.3.1]
. Any unramified maximal
k
-torus of
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
is of the form
{}^{g}{\mathbf{T}}
for some
g\in{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(\breve{k})
. Moreover,
{}^{g}{\mathbf{T}}
is
k
-rational if and only if
\sigma_{b}({}^{g}{\mathbf{T}})={}^{g}{\mathbf{T}}
, which is equivalent to
g^{-1}b\sigma(g)\in{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
. In that case, if
w\in W
is the image of
g^{-1}b\sigma(g)
, then
{}^{g}{\mathbf{T}}\in{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},w)
. Replacing
g
by
g\dot{v}
for any lift
\dot{v}\in{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
of some
v\in W
has the effect of replacing
w
by
v^{-1}w\sigma(v)
.

3.2. Rational conjugacy classes for pure inner forms

It is easy to see that the surjection
{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{W}
induces an isomorphism
(3)
(3)

(3)

Indeed, one can use
[
Ser94
, Corollary 2 to Proposition I.39 and Corollary to Proposition I.41]
along with the pro-version of Lang’s theorem for the group
{\mathcal{T}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})=\ker({\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{W})
with varying rational structures given by
\sigma_{b}
(
b\in{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
).

H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k}))\cong H^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
.

The following is a consequence of
[
Ree11
, Proposition 6.1]
;
(4)
(4)

(4)

In fact,
[
Ree11
]
works with
\Gal_{k}
-cohomology and
all
maximal
k
-tori. To deduce our statements, one can run literally the same arguments with
\langle\sigma\rangle
replacing
\Gal_{k}
and unramified
k
-tori replacing all
k
-tori.
see also
[
DR09
, Section 2]
and
[
DeB06
, Section 4.3]
. Suppose that
[b]\in B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}
comes from a class
\xi\in H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})
(
cf.
(
2.6
)),
i.e.
,
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
is a pure inner form of
{\mathbf{G}}
. Then rational conjugacy classes of unramified maximal
k
-tori in
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
are in bijection with preimages of
\xi
under the natural map
H^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k}))\rightarrow H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})
;
cf.

[
Ree11
, Lemma 6.2]
(and use twisting). There are maps

(3.2)

H^{1}(\sigma,W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\;q_{0}}}{{\longleftarrow}}H^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}}).

By Section
3.1
, given
[w]\in H^{1}(\sigma,W)
, represented by
w\in W
, and
\xi\in H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})
, represented by
b\in{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
, the set
{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},w)/\Ad{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
of rational conjugacy classes in
{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},w)
is in bijection with
\pi_{0}^{-1}(\xi)\cap q_{0}^{-1}([w])
. In particular,
[w]
is in the image of (
3.1
) if and only if
\pi_{0}^{-1}(\xi)\cap q_{0}^{-1}([w])\neq\varnothing
.

3.3. Rational conjugacy classes for extended pure inner forms

We wish to extend (
3.2
) to all extended pure inner forms. We have
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})\subseteq X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\subseteq\widetilde{W}
. Define

\widetilde{Z}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})=\left\{\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{W}\colon\nu_{\tilde{w}}\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{{\mathbb Q}}\right\},

where
\nu_{\tilde{w}}
is as in Section
2.4
. In other words,
\widetilde{Z}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
is precisely the set of all basic elements in
\widetilde{W}
in the sense of Section
2.4
. Define an equivalence relation on
\widetilde{Z}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
by
x\sim y
if and only if there exists a
g\in\widetilde{W}
such that
y=g^{-1}x\sigma(g)
. Put

\widetilde{H}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})=\widetilde{Z}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})/\sim.

Note that we have
H^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})\subseteq\widetilde{H}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
and
Z^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})\subseteq\widetilde{Z}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
is precisely the set of those
\tilde{w}
with
\nu_{\tilde{w}}=0
. Recalling from (
2.6
) that
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})\subseteq B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

There is a natural surjection
\pi\colon\widetilde{H}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})\rightarrow B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}
extending the surjection
\pi_{0}\colon H^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})\rightarrow H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}})
.

Proof.

Suppose
\tilde{w}_{1}\sim\tilde{w}_{2}\in\widetilde{Z}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
,
i.e.
,
\tilde{w}_{2}=g^{-1}\tilde{w}_{1}\sigma(g)
. Then obviously
\bar{\kappa}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(\tilde{w}_{1})=\bar{\kappa}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(\tilde{w}_{2})\in\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
, and an easy computation shows that
\nu_{\tilde{w}_{1}}=\nu_{\tilde{w}_{2}}
. Thus some (
i.e.
, any) lifts to
{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
of
\tilde{w}_{1}
,
\tilde{w}_{2}
are
\sigma
-conjugate. This gives a map
\widetilde{H}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})\rightarrow B({\mathbf{G}})
whose image is contained in
B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}
as each
\tilde{w}\in\widetilde{Z}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
is basic. The surjectivity is clear.
∎

The projection
\widetilde{W}\twoheadrightarrow W
induces a map
q\colon\widetilde{H}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})\rightarrow H^{1}(\sigma,W)
. Altogether, we have the diagram

(3.3)

H^{1}(\sigma,W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\longleftarrow}}\widetilde{H}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}

extending (
3.2
). DeBacker and Reeder’s parametrization from Section
3.2
admits the following completely analogous generalization to all extended pure inner forms.

Proposition 3.4
.

Given
[w]\in H^{1}(\sigma,W)
and
[b]\in B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}
, we have a natural bijection
{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},w)/\Ad{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)\cong\pi^{-1}([b])\cap q^{-1}([w])
. In particular,
[w]
is in the image of (
3.1
) if and only if
\pi^{-1}([b])\cap q^{-1}([w])\neq\varnothing
.

Proof.

Any unramified maximal
k
-torus in
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
is of the form
{}^{g}{\mathbf{T}}
with
g^{-1}b\sigma(g)\in{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
; see Remark
3.2
. Let
\tilde{w}_{g}\in\widetilde{W}
be the image of
g^{-1}b\sigma(g)
. Then
\nu_{\tilde{w}_{g}}=\nu_{b}
, and hence
\tilde{w}_{g}\in\widetilde{Z}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
and
\pi([\tilde{w}_{g}])=[b]
. Let
{}^{g_{i}}{\mathbf{T}}
(
i=1,2
) be two such tori with corresponding elements
\tilde{w}_{i}\in\widetilde{Z}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
. Suppose that the
{}^{g_{i}}{\mathbf{T}}
are rationally conjugate in
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
,
i.e.
, there is some
h\in{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
with
{}^{g_{1}}{\mathbf{T}}={}^{hg_{2}}{\mathbf{T}}
. Then
g_{1}^{-1}hg_{2}\in{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
, and letting
\tilde{v}\in\widetilde{W}
be its image, we compute

(3.4)

\tilde{v}\tilde{w}_{2}\sigma(\tilde{v})^{-1}=g_{1}^{-1}hg_{2}g_{2}^{-1}b\sigma(g_{2})\sigma(g_{2}^{-1}h^{-1}g_{1})=g_{1}^{-1}hb\sigma(h^{-1})\sigma(g_{1})=g_{1}^{-1}b\sigma(g_{1})=\tilde{w}_{1};

i.e.
,
\tilde{w}_{1}
and
\tilde{w}_{2}
define the same class in
\widetilde{H}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
. Conversely, given
[\tilde{w}]\in\tilde{\pi}^{-1}([b])
, we can lift
\tilde{w}
to some
\dot{w}\in{\mathbf{N}}(\breve{k})
, which necessarily belongs to
[b]
. If
\dot{w}=g^{-1}b\sigma(g)
, one immediately checks that
\tilde{w}\mapsto{}^{g}{\mathbf{T}}
induces an inverse to the above map. This shows that rational conjugacy classes of unramified tori in
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
are in bijection with
\pi^{-1}([b])
, establishing the analogue of
[
Ree11
, Lemma 6.2]
. The rest of the proof (the analogue of
[
Ree11
, Lemma 6.4]
) is done analogously.∎

3.4. A description of
\boldsymbol{\pi^{-1}([b])\cap q^{-1}([w])}

Fix
w\in W
and
[b]\in B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}
. Put

F_{w}\mathop{\overset{\,\mathrm{def}\,}{\scalebox{2.2}[1]{=}}}\text{fiber of ${\mathbf{N}}\rightarrow W$ over $w$}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{F}_{w}\mathop{\overset{\,\mathrm{def}\,}{\scalebox{2.2}[1]{=}}}\text{fiber of $\widetilde{W}\rightarrow W$ over $w$}.

We regard
F_{w}
as a
\breve{k}
-scheme and
\overline{F}_{w}
as a discrete
\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}
-scheme (or, sometimes, as a discrete set). Then
F_{w}
is a (trivial)
{\mathbf{T}}_{\breve{k}}
-torsor and
\overline{F}_{w}
is a (trivial)
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
-torsor. The action of
{\mathbf{T}}_{\breve{k}}
on
F_{w}
is given by
t,\tilde{w}\mapsto t\tilde{w}
, and the action of
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
on
\overline{F}_{w}
is induced by this. Recall the maps
\kappa_{w}\colon L{\mathbf{T}}\rightarrow X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}
and
\bar{\kappa}_{w}\colon X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\rightarrow X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}
from Section
2.3
.

Lemma 3.5
.

The natural surjection
F_{w}(\breve{k})\twoheadrightarrow\overline{F}_{w}
induces an isomorphism
LF_{w}\,/\ker\kappa_{w}\cong\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\overline{\kappa}_{w}
. This discrete set is a
(
trivial
)

X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}
-torsor.

Proof.

As
LF_{w}
(resp.
\overline{F}_{w}
) is a trivial
L{\mathbf{T}}
-torsor (resp.
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
-torsor), everything follows from
L{\mathbf{T}}/\ker\kappa_{w}=X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}=X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}
. ∎

Observe that for
\tilde{w}\in\overline{F}_{w}
, the values of
\nu_{\tilde{w}}
and
\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}(\tilde{w})
remain constant if
\tilde{w}
is multiplied by an element in
\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
. Indeed, for
\bar{\kappa}_{w}
this is clear, and for
\nu
this follows from the description in Section
2.4
. In particular,
\bar{\kappa}_{{\mathbf{G}}}|_{\overline{F}_{w}}
induces a map

(3.5)

\bar{\kappa}^{w}\colon\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}\longrightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}.

Moreover, from Section
2.4
we immediately deduce the following lemma.

Lemma 3.6
.

The map (
2.5
) factors through an injection
(\nu,\bar{\kappa}^{w})\colon\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}\rightarrow(W\backslash X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{{\mathbb Q}})^{\langle\sigma\rangle}\times\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
.

Fix
w\in W
and
[b]\in B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}
. Put

[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\mathop{\overset{\,\mathrm{def}\,}{\scalebox{2.2}[1]{=}}}{\im}\left([b]\cap F_{w}(\breve{k})\hookrightarrow F_{w}(\breve{k})\twoheadrightarrow\overline{F}_{w}\right).

Then
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\subseteq[b]\cap\widetilde{Z}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
, inducing a map
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\rightarrow\pi^{-1}([b])\subseteq\widetilde{H}^{1}(\sigma,\widetilde{W})
. This obviously factors through a map
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\rightarrow\pi^{-1}([b])\cap q^{-1}([w])
. By Lemma
3.6
,
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}
is stable under the action of
\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
, and we have the (finite) quotient set
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
, consisting of all
\bar{w}\in\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
satisfying
\nu_{\bar{w}}=\nu_{b}
and
\bar{\kappa}^{w}(\bar{w})=\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b)
.

Proposition 3.7
.

(i)

The quotient
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
is either empty or a
\ker(H^{1}(\sigma_{w},{\mathbf{T}})\rightarrow H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}}))=\im(\beta_{w})_{{\rm tors}}
-torsor
(
cf. (
2.2
)
)
. It is empty if and only if
{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},w)=\varnothing
.

(ii)

The centralizer
C_{W}(w\sigma)\subseteq W
of
w\sigma\in W\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
acts on
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
in a natural way, and there are canonical isomorphisms

\left([b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}\right)/\penalty C_{W}(w\sigma)\,\xlongrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,\pi^{-1}([b])\cap q^{-1}([w])\cong{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},w)\,/\Ad{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k).

Proof.

We have
{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},w)\neq\varnothing\,\Leftrightarrow\,\exists g\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})\text{ such that }g^{-1}b\sigma(g)\in F_{w}(\breve{k})\,\Leftrightarrow\,[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\neq\varnothing
,
where the first equivalence follows from Remark
3.2
. To show the first claim of (
i
), we may assume that there is an element
\tilde{w}_{1}\in[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\neq\varnothing
. Recall that
\overline{F}_{w}
is a
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
-torsor.

Lemma 3.8
.

For
\tau\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
, we have
\tau\tilde{w}_{1}\in[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\Leftrightarrow\bar{\kappa}_{w}(\tau)\in\ker(H^{1}(\sigma_{w},{\mathbf{T}})\rightarrow H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}}))
.

Proof.

We have
\tau\tilde{w}_{1}\in[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}
if and only if
\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)=0
(as
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}
is a group homomorphism) and
\nu_{\tau\tilde{w}_{1}}=\nu_{\tilde{w}_{1}}
. Now,
\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}(\tau)=0
means that the image
\bar{\kappa}_{w}(\tau)\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}
maps to
0\in\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
, which is equivalent to
\bar{\kappa}_{w}(\tau)\in\im(\beta_{w})
, with
\beta_{w}
as in (
2.1
). Now we use the algorithm to compute
\nu
from Section
2.4
. Note that the integer
d
can be chosen the same for
\tilde{w}_{1}
and
\tau\tilde{w}_{1}
. Then let
\mu_{\tilde{w}_{1}}
and
\mu_{\tau\tilde{w}_{1}}
be as described in Section
2.4
. Then the equality
\nu_{\tau\tilde{w}_{1}}=\nu_{\tilde{w}_{1}}
is equivalent to the existence of some
v\in W
such that the equality

\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d-1}(w\sigma)^{i}(\tau)=v(\mu_{\tilde{w}_{1}})-\mu_{\tilde{w}_{1}}

holds in
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
. But
\tilde{w}_{1}
is basic, so
\mu_{\tilde{w}_{1}}
is central;
i.e.
, the right-hand side is zero. Thus
\tau\tilde{w}_{1}\in[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}
if and only if
\bar{\kappa}_{w}(\tau)\in\im(\beta_{w})
and
\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d-1}(w\sigma)^{i}(\tau)=0
. By
[
DR09
, Lemma 2.4.1]
, the latter condition is equivalent to
\tau\in\bar{\kappa}_{w}^{-1}(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle,{\rm tors}})=\bar{\kappa}_{w}^{-1}(H^{1}(\sigma_{w},{\mathbf{T}}))
.
∎

By Lemma
3.8
,
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}
(being non-empty) is a torsor under the preimage in
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
of
\ker(H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{T}}_{w})\rightarrow H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{G}}))\subseteq X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}
. This preimage contains
\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
, and factoring it out, we arrive at the claim. This finishes the proof of (
i
).

In (
ii
), it is clear that we have a map
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\rightarrow\pi^{-1}([b])\cap q^{-1}([w])
, and one checks that it factors through
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
. Moreover, surjectivity of this map amounts to the tautological fact that any element in
[w]\subseteq W
is
\sigma
-conjugate to
w
. Next, let us describe the natural action of
C_{W}(w\sigma)
on
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
. Let
\bar{w}\in[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
with preimage
\tilde{w}\in[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}
. Let
v\in C_{W}(w\sigma)
, and let
\tilde{v}\in\widetilde{W}
be any preimage. Then the action of
v
sends
\bar{w}
to the image of
\tilde{v}^{-1}\tilde{w}\sigma(\tilde{v})
in
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
. This is independent of the choice of
\tilde{v}
, as if
\tilde{v}^{\prime}
is another choice, putting
\tau=\tilde{v}^{-1}\tilde{v}^{\prime}\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
gives the element
\tilde{v}^{\prime-1}\tilde{w}\sigma(\tilde{v}^{\prime})=\tau^{-1}\tilde{v}^{-1}\tilde{w}\sigma(\tilde{v})\sigma(\tau)
, which has the same image in
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
. Similarly, one shows that it is independent of the choice of
\tilde{w}
. Now,
\bar{w}^{\prime}\in[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
has the same image as
\bar{w}
in
\pi^{-1}([b])\cap q^{-1}([w])
if and only if there is some
\tilde{v}\in\widetilde{W}
with
\tilde{v}^{-1}\tilde{w}\sigma(\tilde{v})=\tilde{w}^{\prime}
for some lift
\tilde{w}^{\prime}\in\overline{F}_{w}
of
\bar{w}^{\prime}
. By the definition of the
C_{W}(w\sigma)
-action, this is equivalent to the fact that
\bar{w}
and
\bar{w}^{\prime}
lie in the same
C_{W}(w\sigma)
-orbit. Thus
C_{W}(w\sigma)
-orbits in
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
coincide with fibers of the surjection onto
\pi^{-1}([b])\cap q^{-1}([w])
, and we are done.∎

Remark 3.9
.

Let us explicate the correspondence in Proposition
3.7
(
ii
). If
\bar{w}\in[b]\cap\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
represents a class on the left side,
\dot{w}\in[b]\cap F_{w}(\breve{k})
is a (
i.e.
, any) lift of
\bar{w}
, and
g\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
satisfies
g^{-1}b\sigma(g)=\dot{w}
, then the map
{\Ad(g)}\circ\iota\colon{\mathbf{T}}_{w}\rightarrow{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
is
k
-rational, its image
{}^{g}{\mathbf{T}}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
lies in
{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},w)
and the
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
-conjugacy class of
{}^{g}{\mathbf{T}}
is the image of
\bar{w}
under the above map.

3.5. The case of Coxeter tori

We keep the setup of Sections
3.3
and
3.4
. Furthermore, throughout Section
3.5
we assume that
w=c\in W

is a Coxeter element
. Recall
\beta_{c}\colon X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})_{\langle\sigma_{c}\rangle}\rightarrow X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{c}\rangle}
from (
2.1
).

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
[b]\in B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}
and
c\in W
be Coxeter. Then the following hold:

(i)

The image
\im(\beta_{c})
is finite.

(ii)

We have

[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}=(\bar{\kappa}^{c})^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b)).

This is an
\im(\beta_{c})
-torsor. In particular,
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\neq\varnothing
.

Proof.

(
i
) As
c
Coxeter,
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})^{\langle\sigma_{c}\rangle}=0
; hence
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})_{\langle\sigma_{c}\rangle}
is finite. Hence
\im(\beta_{c})
is finite. For (
ii
) we first record the following easy and well-known lemma.

Lemma 3.11
.

If
c
is Coxeter, all elements in
\overline{F}_{c}
are basic.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{c}\in\overline{F}_{c}
. If
\mu
is as in the algorithm to compute
\nu_{b}
from Section
2.4
, we see that
\mu\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})^{\langle\sigma_{c}\rangle}_{{\mathbb Q}}\subseteq X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{{\mathbb Q}}
, the last inclusion being true as
c
is Coxeter.
∎

Now note that
\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}|_{\overline{F}_{c}}\colon\overline{F}_{c}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
is surjective. This holds as
\overline{F}_{c}
is a
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
-torsor,
\kappa_{{\mathbf{G}}}
is a group homomorphism and
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
is surjective. Taking any preimage
b^{\prime}
of
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b)
in
\overline{F}_{c}
, Lemma
3.11
shows that
b^{\prime}
is basic. Moreover, we know that
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}
induces a bijection
B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
. It follows that
b^{\prime}\in[b]
;
i.e.
,
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\neq\varnothing
. Moreover, as this holds for any
b^{\prime}
in the preimage, we even get the equality claimed in (
ii
). Now,
(\bar{\kappa}^{c})^{-1}(\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}(b))
is a
\ker(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}_{\langle\sigma_{c}\rangle}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle})=\im(\beta_{c})
-torsor.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

It follows from Proposition
3.10
(
ii
) that for any
[b]\in B({\mathbf{G}})_{\bas}
and any Coxeter element
c
, there exists a representative of
[b]
in
F_{c}(\breve{k})
. This is a converse to Lemma
3.11
.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
c\in W
be Coxeter. Suppose one of the following holds:

(i)

We have
[b]=1
.

(ii)

We have
{\mathbf{G}}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{s}{\mathbf{G}}_{i}
, where for each
i
,
{\mathbf{G}}_{i}={\Res}_{k_{i}/k}{\mathbf{G}}_{i}^{\prime}
for a finite unramified extension
k_{i}/k
and an absolutely almost simple
k_{i}
-group
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}_{i}
.

Then the
C_{W}(c\sigma)
-action on
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
is trivial, and there is a natural bijection

[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}\,\xlongrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},c)\,/\Ad{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k).

Proof.

Choose some
\sigma
-equivariant splitting
\iota\colon W\rightarrow\widetilde{W}
(which exists, as
{\mathbf{G}}
is unramified), and denote its image by
\dot{W}
. Let
\dot{c}\in\overline{F}_{c}\cap\dot{W}
. Let
\tau\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
be such that
\tau\dot{c}\in[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}
. Let
v\in C_{W}(c\sigma)
, and let
\dot{v}=\iota(v)
. The action of
v
sends the image of
\tau\dot{c}
in
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
to the image in
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
of the element
\dot{v}^{-1}\tau\dot{c}\sigma(\dot{v})
. As
\iota
is
\sigma
-equivariant,
\dot{v}\in C_{\dot{W}}(\dot{c}\sigma)
, so
\dot{v}^{-1}\tau\dot{c}\sigma(\dot{v})=\dot{v}^{-1}\tau\dot{v}\dot{c}=\Ad(v)^{-1}(\tau)\dot{c}
;
i.e.
, we must show (replacing
v
with
v^{-1}
) that
(\Ad\,v-\id)(\tau)=\Ad\,v(\tau)-\tau\in\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
.

In case (
i
), we have
[1]\cap\overline{F}_{c}=X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})\cdot\dot{c}
. Thus, as
\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}^{\mathop{\rm sc}}\subseteq\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
, it suffices to show that we have
(\Ad\,v-\id)(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}}))\subseteq\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}^{\mathop{\rm sc}}
. In other words, we are reduced to the case that
{\mathbf{G}}
is simply connected, which is covered by (
ii
).

In case (
ii
), one is immediately reduced to the case that
{\mathbf{G}}=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
with
k^{\prime}/k
a finite subextension of
\breve{k}/k
and
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
an absolutely almost simple
k^{\prime}
-group. Let
d=[k^{\prime}\colon k]
. Write
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d}X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})
and
W=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d}W^{\prime}
, with
\sigma
-action given by
(v_{1},\dots,v_{d})\mapsto(\sigma^{\prime}(v_{d}),v_{1},\dots,v_{d-1})
on both objects, where
\sigma^{\prime}
is the
\breve{k}/k^{\prime}
-Frobenius for
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
. If
c,c_{1}\in W
are
\sigma
-conjugate, the statement claimed in the proposition holds for
c
if and only if it holds for
c_{1}
. Thus we may assume that
c=(c^{\prime},1,\dots,1)
, where
c^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
is a Coxeter element in
W^{\prime}
. Suppose we know the claim for
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime},c^{\prime}
;
i.e.
, for any
v^{\prime}\in C_{W^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime})
,
(\Ad v^{\prime}-\id)(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}))\subseteq(\sigma^{\prime}_{c^{\prime}}-\id)(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}))
. We have

\displaystyle\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{c}-\id)(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}))=\left\{\sigma^{\prime}_{c^{\prime}}(\alpha_{d})-\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{r-1}-\alpha_{d}\colon\alpha_{i}\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})\text{ for }1\leq i\leq d\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\sigma^{\prime}_{c^{\prime}}(\alpha_{r})-\alpha_{d}-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d-1}\beta_{i},\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{d-1}\right)\colon\beta_{i},\alpha_{d}\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})\text{ for }1\leq i\leq d-1\right\}

The inclusion
W^{\prime}\rightarrow W
into the first factor induces an isomorphism
C_{W^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime})\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,C_{W}(c\sigma)
,
v^{\prime}\mapsto(v^{\prime},\dots,v^{\prime})=v
. For
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
, we have
(\Ad v-\id)((e_{i})_{i})=((\Ad v^{\prime}-\id)(e_{i}))_{i=1}^{d}
. This lies in
\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
as, setting
\beta_{i}:=(\Ad v^{\prime}-\id)(e_{i+1})
for
1\leq i\leq d-1
, it suffices to find an
\alpha_{d}\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})
such that
\sigma^{\prime}_{c^{\prime}}(\alpha_{d})-\alpha_{d}=\Ad v^{\prime}(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d}e_{i})-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d}e_{i}
, which works by assumption for
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
.

It remains to check the claim for an absolutely almost simple group
{\mathbf{G}}
over
k
. Let
d>0
be the smallest positive integer such that
\sigma^{d}
acts trivially on the Dynkin diagram of
{\mathbf{G}}
. By
[
Spr74
, Theorem 7.6(v)]
,
C_{W}(c\sigma)
is the cyclic subgroup of
W
generated by
c^{\spl}=c\sigma(c)\dots\sigma^{d-1}(c)
. Thus, it suffices to check that
(\Ad c^{\spl}-\id)(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}))\subseteq\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}=\im(\sigma_{c}-\id\colon X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\rightarrow X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}))
. Observe that
\sigma_{c}(\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c})=\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
(as
\sigma_{c}
is an automorphism and for any
v\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
,
\sigma_{c}(\sigma_{c}(v)-v)=\sigma_{c}(\sigma_{c}(v))-\sigma_{c}(v)\in\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
).
Now
\sigma^{d}
acts trivially on
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
, so it suffices to check that for all
\tau\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
,
c^{\spl}\sigma^{d}(\tau)-\tau\in\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
, or equivalently,
\sigma_{c}^{d}(v)-v\in\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
. We now prove that
\sigma_{c}^{i}(v)-v\in\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
for all
i>0
. For
i=1
, this holds as
\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}=\im(\sigma_{c}-1)
. For
i>1
, we have
\sigma_{c}^{i}(v)-v=\sigma_{c}^{i}(v)-\sigma_{c}^{i-1}(v)+\sigma_{c}^{i-1}(v)-v=\sigma_{c}^{i-1}(\sigma_{c}(v)-v)+\sigma_{c}^{i-1}(v)-v
. Now we have
\sigma_{c}^{i-1}(v)-v\in\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
by induction on
i
, and
\sigma_{c}^{i-1}(\sigma_{c}(v)-v)\in\sigma_{c}^{i-1}(\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c})=\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
. ∎

Proposition
3.13
becomes false without any assumptions on
{\mathbf{G}}
,
b
, as the next example shows.

Example 3.14
.

Suppose
\charac k\neq 2
. Let
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}={\bf SL}_{2}\times{\bf SL}_{2}
and
{\mathbf{G}}={\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}/\mu_{2}
, embedded diagonally. Let
{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
be the product of diagonal tori in
{\bf SL}_{2}
and
{\mathbf{T}}
its image in
{\mathbf{G}}
. Let
\varepsilon_{1}=((1,-1),(0,0))
,
\varepsilon_{2}=((0,0),(1,-1))
be a
{\mathbb Z}
-basis of
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})
. We may identify
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})=\{\frac{a_{1}\varepsilon_{1}+a_{2}\varepsilon_{2}}{2}\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})_{\mathbb Q}\colon a_{1},a_{2}\in{\mathbb Z},a_{1}\equiv a_{2}\mod 2\}
. Let
s
be the image of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in the Weyl group of the diagonal torus in
{\bf SL}_{2}
, so that
c=(s,s)
represents a Coxeter element in
W
. Let
\dot{c}_{0}=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)\in F_{c}(\breve{k})
be a hyperspecial lift, and let
\bar{c}_{0}
be its image in
\overline{F}_{c}
. We have
C_{W}(c)=W\cong{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}\times{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
. Note that
\tau_{1}=(\varpi^{\frac{1}{2}},-\varpi^{-\frac{1}{2}}),(\varpi^{\frac{1}{2}},-\varpi^{-\frac{1}{2}})\in{\mathbf{G}}(k)
by Galois descent. The action of the Frobenius
\sigma
on all objects below is trivial, so we ignore it. We have
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})=X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\,/X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})\cong{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
, the non-trivial element being the class of
\frac{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}}{2}
. It is represented by the basic element
b_{1}=\dot{c}_{0}\tau_{1}
. We have
\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}={\mathbb Z}\cdot 2\varepsilon_{1}+{\mathbb Z}\cdot(\varepsilon_{2}-\varepsilon_{1})\subseteq X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
.

Case
b=1

We have
[1]\cap\overline{F}_{c}=X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})\cdot\bar{c}_{0}
. As
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}=\{\bar{0},\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\}\cong{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
, we have
