[1]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}=(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c})\cdot\bar{c}_{0}=\{\bar{0},\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\}\cdot\bar{c}_{0}
. Now it is immediate to check that the action of
C_{W}(c)
from Proposition
3.7
on this set is trivial. For example,
\Ad(s,1)
send
\varepsilon_{i}\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})
to
(-1)^{i}\varepsilon_{i}
(
i\in 1,2
), so
\Ad(s,1)(\varepsilon_{i})-\varepsilon_{i}\in\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
. This is in agreement with Proposition
3.13
and also with the Bruhat–Tits picture:
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}\cong{\bf PGL}_{2}\times{\bf PGL}_{2}
, so an alcove of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbf{G}},k}
is a square whose vertices are hyperspecial of type
(\alpha,\beta)
with
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
the parity of the valuation of the determinant of the lattice representing a vertex of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\bf PGL}_{2}}
. The action of
\tau_{1}\in{\mathbf{G}}(k)
identifies the vertices
(0,0)\leftrightarrow(1,1)
and
(0,1)\leftrightarrow(1,0)
, so that there are two
{\mathbf{G}}(k)
-orbits in hyperspecial vertices of a base alcove and hence (by
[
DeB06
]
) two rational conjugacy classes of Coxeter tori.

Case
b=b_{1}

We have
[b_{1}]\cap\overline{F}_{c}=(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}){\,\smallsetminus\,}X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}))\cdot\bar{c}_{0}
. As
(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}){\,\smallsetminus\,}X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}))\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}=\{\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}}{2},\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}+3\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}}{2}\}
, we have
[b_{1}]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}=\{\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}}{2},\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}+3\bar{\varepsilon}_{2}}{2}\}\cdot\dot{c}_{0}
. The action of
(s,1)\in C_{W}(c)
identifies the two elements in
[b_{1}]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
as
\frac{\varepsilon_{1}+3\varepsilon_{2}}{2}-\Ad(s,1)(\frac{\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}}{2})=\frac{\varepsilon_{1}+3\varepsilon_{2}}{2}-\frac{-\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}}{2}=\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}\in\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
;
i.e.
, the
C_{W}(c)
-action is non-trivial. This agrees with Bruhat–Tits theory as the adjoint building of
{\mathbf{G}}_{b_{1}}
over
k
is a point (it is the barycenter of an alcove of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbf{G}},k}
), so that there is just one
{\mathbf{G}}_{b_{1}}(k)
-conjugacy class of Coxeter tori.

4. The maps
\boldsymbol{\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)\rightarrow X_{w}(b)}

Here we review and extend some results from
[
Iva23
, Section 11]
.
By
[
Iva23
, Lemma 11.5]
the space
\dot{X}_{\dot{w}}(b)
depends—up to a
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)\times{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(k)
-equivariant isomorphism—only on the image of
\dot{w}
in the discrete set
LF_{w}\,/\ker\kappa_{w}
and not on
\dot{w}
. As the concrete choice of
\dot{w}
never plays an important role, we make the following simplifying definition. Recall from Lemma
3.5
that
LF_{w}\,/\ker\kappa_{w}=\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
.

Definition 4.1
.

For
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
and
\bar{w}\in\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
, we denote by
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)
the space
\dot{X}_{\dot{w}}(b)
for an arbitrary lift
\dot{w}\in F_{w}(\breve{k})
of
\bar{w}
.

Proposition 4.2

(
cf.

[
Iva23
, Propositions 11.4 and 11.9]
)
.

Let
w\in W
and
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
. There is a canonical map of arc-sheaves

\alpha_{w,b}\colon X_{w}(b)\longrightarrow\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\overline{\kappa}_{w}

defining a clopen decomposition
X_{w}(b)=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\bar{w}\in\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\overline{\kappa}_{w}}X_{w}(b)_{\bar{w}}
, where
X_{w}(b)_{\bar{w}}:=\alpha_{w,b}^{-1}(\{\bar{w}\})
. For
\bar{w}\in\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
,
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)\rightarrow X_{w}(b)_{\bar{w}}
is a pro-étale
{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(k)
-torsor. In particular,
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)\neq\varnothing
if and only if
\bar{w}\in\im(\alpha_{w,b})
.

Proof.

Combine
[
Iva23
, Propositions 11.4 and 11.9]
with Lemma
3.5
.
∎

We have the following emptyness criterion for
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)
,
i.e.
, a constraint on the image of
\alpha_{w,b}
. Recall the map
\bar{\kappa}^{w}\colon\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
from (
3.5
).

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
and
\bar{w}\in\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
. If
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)\neq\varnothing
, then
\kappa_{{\mathbf{G}}}(b)=\bar{\kappa}^{w}(\bar{w})
. In particular,
\alpha_{w,b}
factors through a map
\alpha_{w,b}\colon X_{w}(b)\rightarrow(\bar{\kappa}^{w})^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b))
.

Proof.

Let
\dot{w}\in F_{w}(\breve{k})
be a lift of
\bar{w}
. As
\dot{X}_{\dot{w}}(b)\neq\varnothing
, there is some algebraically closed field
{\mathfrak f}\in\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
such that
\dot{X}_{\dot{w}}(b)({\mathfrak f})\neq\varnothing
. Let
\bar{g}\in\dot{X}_{\dot{w}}(b)({\mathfrak f})
, and let
g\in{\mathbf{G}}(L)
be a lift of
\bar{g}
. Then
g^{-1}b\sigma(g)\in{\mathbf{U}}(L)\dot{w}{\mathbf{U}}(L)
. We clearly have
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(g^{-1}b\sigma(g))=\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b)
. Furthermore, any element of
{\mathbf{U}}(L)
can be conjugated to some Iwahori subgroup, and the Kottwitz map is trivial on Iwahori subgroups. Thus
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}({\mathbf{U}})=1
.
Thus
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}({\mathbf{U}}(L)\dot{w}{\mathbf{U}}(L))=\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(\dot{w})
, and the first claim follows. The last claim follows from the first and Proposition
4.2
.
∎

Recall the map
\beta_{w}\colon X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}\rightarrow X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}
from (
2.1
). We have the following diagram with compatible simply transitive actions:

(4.1)

\textstyle{(\bar{\kappa}^{w})^{-1}(\kappa_{{\mathbf{G}}}(b))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\kappa}^{w}}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}}
\textstyle{\im(\beta_{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowleft}
\textstyle{X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\langle\sigma_{w}\rangle}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowleft}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowleft}

where the actions are induced by the left multiplication action of
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
on
\overline{F}_{w}
.

Corollary 4.4
.

If
\beta_{w}=0
, then
\alpha_{w,b}
factors through a point, i.e., there is at most one
\bar{w}\in\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}
such that
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)\neq\varnothing
. In this case,
X_{w}(b)\neq\varnothing
if and only if
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)\neq\varnothing
and
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)\rightarrow X_{w}(b)
is a pro-étale
{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(k)
-torsor.

Corollary 4.5
.

If
{\mathbf{G}}
is of adjoint type and
w
is Coxeter, the conclusions of Corollary
4.4
hold. Moreover, in this case, the map
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)\rightarrow X_{w}(b)
is quasi-compact.

Proof.

The first claim follows from Corollary
4.4
and Remark
2.5
.
The last claim follows as
\dot{X}_{\bar{w}}(b)\rightarrow X_{w}(b)
is a torsor under the profinite group
{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(k)
.
∎

In the case that
b
is basic and
w=c
is Coxeter, the situation simplifies as follows.

Corollary 4.6
.

Suppose
b
is basic and
c
is Coxeter. For any
\bar{c}\in\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
such that
X_{\bar{c}}(b)\neq\varnothing
, there exists a
b_{1}\in[b]\cap F_{c}(\breve{k})
lying over
\bar{c}
, and there is a
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)\times{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)
-equivariant isomorphism
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)\cong\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b_{1})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.3
,
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)\neq\varnothing
forces
\bar{c}\in(\bar{\kappa}^{c})^{-1}(\bar{\kappa}_{{\mathbf{G}}}(b))=[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
, where the equality holds by Proposition
3.10
. This implies the existence of
b_{1}
. For any
g\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
such that
g^{-1}b\sigma(g)=b_{1}
,
x\mapsto gx
induces an equivariant isomorphism
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)\cong\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b_{1})
by
[
Iva23
, Remark 8.7]
.
∎

Let us also record the following consequence.

Corollary 4.7
.

Suppose
b
is basic and
c
is Coxeter. Then
\alpha_{c,b}
factors through a surjection

\alpha_{c,b}\colon X_{c}(b)\longrightarrow[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}.

There is a canonical surjection from the set of different non-empty pieces
X_{c}(b)_{\bar{c}}\subseteq X_{c}(b)
to the set
{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}}_{b},c)\,/\Ad{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
of rational conjugacy classes of unramified Coxeter tori in
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}
.

In particular, if
{\mathbf{G}},b
satisfy the assumptions of Proposition
3.13
, then this surjection is a
1
-to-
1
correspondence.

Proof.

The surjectivity of the map
\alpha_{c,b}
in the corollary follows (for example) from Theorem
1.1
. The rest immediately follows from Propositions
3.7
,
3.10
and
3.13

∎

We finish this section with the following remark on the non-basic case.

Remark 4.8
.

If
[b]
is non-basic and
c
Coxeter, then
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}=\varnothing
by Lemma
3.11
. Hence—although we still have
\im(\beta_{c})_{{\rm tors}}=\im(\beta_{c})
just as in Proposition
3.10
—we cannot conclude that
[b]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\kappa_{c}=(\bar{\kappa}^{c})^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b))
. In particular, our arguments do not imply that
X_{c}(b)
(equivalently, all of its covering spaces
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
with
\bar{c}\in\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
) is empty. In agreement with this,
X_{c}(b)
indeed can be non-empty;
cf.

[
Iva23
, Theorem 13.2]
.

5. A variant of Steinberg’s cross section

By the
Dynkin diagram

\Dyn({\mathbf{G}})
of the unramified group
{\mathbf{G}}
, we mean the Dynkin diagram of the split group
{\mathbf{G}}_{\breve{k}}
endowed with the action induced by the Frobenius
\sigma
. There is a unique decomposition into
\sigma
-stable subdiagrams
\Dyn({\mathbf{G}})=\coprodop\displaylimits_{i}\Gamma_{i}
such that
\sigma
cyclically permutes
\pi_{0}(\Gamma_{i})
for each
i
. For each
i
, let
\Gamma_{i,0}
be a connected component of
\Gamma_{i}
, so that
\Gamma_{i}=\coprodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{d_{i}}\sigma^{j}(\Gamma_{i,0})
. Then
(\Gamma_{i,0},\sigma^{d_{i}})
is a
connected
Dynkin diagram of a group over
k_{d_{i}}
, an unramified extension of
k
of degree
d_{i}
.

Definition 5.1
.

We say that the unramified group
{\mathbf{G}}
is
of classical type
if each connected component
(\Gamma_{i,0},\sigma^{d_{i}})
as above of
\Dyn({\mathbf{G}})
is of one of the following types:
A_{n-1}
(
n\geq 2
),
B_{m}
(
m\geq 2
),
C_{m}
(
m\geq 2
),
D_{m}
(
m\geq 4
),
{}^{2}A_{n-1}
(
n\geq 3
) or
{}^{2}D_{m}
(
m\geq 4
).

Although Theorem
1.1
and its corollaries essentially hold for all Coxeter elements (
cf.
the discussion preceding Theorem
1.1
), it will be convenient to work with particular ones. Therefore, we introduce the following auxiliary notion.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{G}}
be of classical type. Let
c\in W
be a Coxeter element. We call
c

special
in the following cases:

•

If
{\mathbf{G}}
is absolutely almost simple,
c
is special if it is of the form
(5)
(5)

(5)

The explicit shape of these elements only becomes important when everything boils down to explicit calculations in Section
7
. For the sake of better readability, we postpone the explicit description until the relevant notation is introduced.
given in (
7.5
) (resp. (
7.7
), (
7.9
), (
7.13
), (
7.18
), (
7.26
)) if
{\mathbf{G}}
is of type
A_{n-1}
(resp.
C_{m}
,
B_{m}
,
D_{m}
,
{}^{2}A_{n-1}
,
{}^{2}D_{m}
).

•

If
{\mathbf{G}}=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
for a finite unramified extension
k^{\prime}/k
and an absolutely almost simple
k^{\prime}
-group
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
, we may identify the Weyl group
W
of
{\mathbf{G}}
with
\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{[k^{\prime}:k]}W^{\prime}
, where
W^{\prime}
is the Weyl group of
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
and
\sigma
acts cyclically. Then
c
is special if it is of the form
(c^{\prime},1,\dots,1)
, where
c^{\prime}
is a special Coxeter element of
W^{\prime}
.

•

If
{\mathbf{G}}
is isogeneous to a product
\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}{\mathbf{G}}_{i}
of almost simple
k
-groups
{\mathbf{G}}_{i}
, we may identify
W=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}W_{i}
, where
W_{i}
is the Weyl group of
{\mathbf{G}}_{i}
. Then
c=(c_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
is special if all
c_{i}
are special.

5.1. A loop version of Steinberg’s cross section

For classical groups and special Coxeter elements, we now prove the existence of certain Steinberg cross sections in our setup. This is a Frobenius-twisted loop version of
[
Ste65
, Proposition 8.9]
and
[
HL12
, Theorem 3.6 and Section 3.14]
, which will be an important ingredient in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Let
c\in W
be a Coxeter element. If
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
is any lift of
c
, then
b\sigma
has a unique fixed point
{\mathbf{x}}={\mathbf{x}}_{b}
on
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},\breve{k}}
. Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
denote the corresponding parahoric
{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}
-model of
{\mathbf{G}}
.
We have the twisted Frobenius
\sigma_{b}=\Ad(b)\circ\sigma
on
L{\mathbf{G}},L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{x}}
.
Let
({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}})_{{\mathbf{x}}}
be the closure of
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}
in
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
. Similarly, we have the closures
({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}
and
({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})_{{\mathbf{x}}}
of
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-}
and
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}
in
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
{\mathbf{G}}
be of classical type and
c\in W
a special Coxeter element. Then

\alpha_{b}\colon L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}})\times L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\longrightarrow L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}),\quad(x,y)\longmapsto x^{-1}y\sigma_{b}(x)

is an isomorphism.
The injectivity of
\alpha_{b}
holds for arbitrary elements
c\in W
which are of minimal length in an elliptic
\sigma
-conjugacy class
(
cf.
[
HL12
]
)
.

Furthermore,
\alpha_{b}
restricts to an isomorphism

\alpha_{b,{\mathbf{x}}}\colon L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}})_{{\mathbf{x}}}\times L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}\longrightarrow L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})_{{\mathbf{x}}}.

Remark 5.4
.

It looks very plausible that Proposition
5.3
holds for arbitrary Coxeter elements
c
. It should be possible to prove this by adapting
[
HL12
, Section 3.5, Theorem 3.6 and Section 3.14]
to the present setting. Let us make the relation of Proposition
5.3
with
[
HL12
]
more clear. With obvious notation (in particular,
c
is Coxeter and
b
any lift of it),
[
HL12
, Theorem 3.6]
says that
{\mathbf{U}}\times({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{b}{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\rightarrow{\mathbf{U}}b{\mathbf{U}}
,
(u,z)\mapsto uz\sigma(u)^{-1}
is an isomorphism if
{\mathbf{G}}
is reductive over a finite field with Frobenius morphism
\sigma
(and, in fact, in various other setups). As an easy corollary of this,
[
HL12
, Section 3.14]
states that
\alpha_{b}^{\prime}\colon({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{\sigma^{-1}(c)^{-1}}{\mathbf{U}})\times({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\rightarrow{\mathbf{U}}
,
(x,y)\mapsto xy\sigma_{b}(x)^{-1}
is an isomorphism in the same setup. Now the finite field version (without the loop functors) of the map
\alpha_{b}
of Proposition
5.3
is obtained from
\alpha_{b}^{\prime}
by a series of changes of variables (
cf.
the first paragraph of the proof of Proposition
5.3
), so that
\alpha_{b}
is an isomorphism if and only if
\alpha_{b}^{\prime}
is.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

We work with the pro-étale topology on
\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
and regard
\alpha_{b}
as a map of pro-étale sheaves. The injectivity of
\alpha_{b}
may be derived from
[
HL12
]
. First, change the variable by setting
y=\sigma_{b}(y_{1})
. It remains to show that
L({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})\times L({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{\sigma^{-1}(c^{-1})}{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\rightarrow L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})
,
x,y_{1}\mapsto x^{-1}\sigma_{b}(y_{1}x)
is injective. Compose this map with the isomorphism
(\cdot)^{-1}\circ\sigma^{-1}\circ\Ad(b)\colon L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})\rightarrow L{\mathbf{U}}
. It remains to show that this composition, given by
x,y_{1}\mapsto x^{-1}y_{1}^{-1}\sigma^{-1}(b^{-1}xb)
, is injective. Finally, change the variables by putting
x=x_{2}^{-1}
and
y_{1}=y_{2}^{-1}
, and set
\tilde{b}:=\sigma^{-1}(b)^{-1}
with image
\tilde{c}
in
W
. We are reduced to showing that
L({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{\sigma(\tilde{c})^{-1}}{\mathbf{U}})\times L({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{\tilde{c}}{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\rightarrow L{\mathbf{U}}
,
x_{2},y_{2}\mapsto x_{2}y_{2}\tilde{b}\sigma^{-1}(x_{2})^{-1}\tilde{b}^{-1}
is injective. This follows from
[
HL12
, Section 3.14(b)]
applied to
A={\mathbb W}(R)[1/\varpi]
(for any
R\in\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
) and
\chi=\phi_{R}^{-1}
.

We now show the surjectivity of
\alpha_{b}
. We may assume that
{\mathbf{G}}
is of adjoint type. Then
{\mathbf{G}}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{r}{\mathbf{G}}_{i}
with
{\mathbf{G}}_{i}
almost simple
k
-groups, and we are immediately reduced to the case that
{\mathbf{G}}
is almost simple over
k
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
k_{d}/k
be an unramified extension of degree
d\geq 1
, and suppose that
{\mathbf{G}}=\Res_{k_{d}/k}{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
for a
k_{d}
-group
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
. If the surjectivity claim for
\alpha_{b}
of Proposition
5.3
holds for
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
, then it holds for
{\mathbf{G}}
.

Proof.

Identify
{\mathbf{G}}_{\breve{k}}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d}{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}_{\breve{k}}
and similarly for the Weyl groups
W
,
W^{\prime}
and unipotent radicals
{\mathbf{U}},{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}
of Borels of
{\mathbf{G}},{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
, so that the Frobenius
\sigma
permutes cyclically the components. As
c
is special,
c=(c^{\prime},1,\dots,1)
for a special Coxeter element
c^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
.
We then have
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}=({}^{c^{\prime}}{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime})\times{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}\times\dots\times{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}
and
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-}=({}^{c^{\prime}}{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime-})\times 1\times\dots\times 1
. The lift
b
of
c
is then given by
(b^{\prime},\tau_{2},\dots,\tau_{d})
for a lift
b^{\prime}
of
c^{\prime}
to
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}(\breve{k})
and some
\tau_{i}\in{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}(\breve{k})
. Then the map
\alpha_{b}
from Proposition
5.3
is given by

(x_{i})_{i=1}^{d},y_{1}\longmapsto(x_{1}^{-1}y_{1}\Ad(b^{\prime})(\sigma^{\prime}(x_{d})),x_{2}^{-1}\Ad(\tau_{2})(x_{1}),\dots x_{d}^{-1}\Ad(\tau_{2})(x_{d-1})).

From this, surjectivity of
\alpha_{b}
for
{\mathbf{G}}
immediately reduces to surjectivity of
\alpha_{b^{\prime}}
for
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

If
\widetilde{\alpha}_{b}\colon L({\mathbf{U}}\,\cap\,{}^{c^{-1}}{\mathbf{U}})\times L({\mathbf{U}}\,\cap\,{}^{c^{-1}}{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\rightarrow L{\mathbf{U}}
denotes the map
\widetilde{\alpha}_{b}(x,y)=x^{-1}y\sigma(bxb^{-1})
, then
\Ad(b^{-1})(\alpha_{b}(x,y))=\widetilde{\alpha}_{b}(\Ad(b^{-1})(x),\Ad(b^{-1})(y))
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from a computation.
∎

As
{\mathbf{G}}
splits over
\breve{k}
, Lemma
5.5
reduces us to the case that
{\mathbf{G}}
is absolutely almost simple over
k
.
It suffices to show that the map
\widetilde{\alpha}_{b}
from Lemma
5.6
is surjective. Write
A=L({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{c^{-1}}{\mathbf{U}})
,
B=L({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{c^{-1}}{\mathbf{U}}^{-})
,
C=L{\mathbf{U}}
. For
\star\in\{A,B,C\}
, write
\Phi_{\star}=\{\alpha\in\Phi\colon L{\mathbf{U}}_{\alpha}\subseteq\star\}
, where
{\mathbf{U}}_{\alpha}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}
denotes the root subgroup corresponding to
\alpha
.
We call a subset
\Psi\subseteq\Phi

closed under addition
if for any
\alpha,\beta\in\Psi
with
\alpha+\beta\in\Phi
, we have
\alpha+\beta\in\Psi
.

Lemma 5.7
.

There exist a positive integer
r
and subsets

\Phi_{C}={}_{1}\supseteq{}_{2}\supseteq\dots\supseteq{}_{r}=\Phi_{B}

satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

For all
1\leq i\leq r
,
i
and
{}_{i}{\,\smallsetminus\,}{}_{r}
are closed under addition.

(ii)

For all
1\leq i<r
and all
\alpha,\beta\in{}_{i}
, the implication
\alpha+\beta\in\Phi\Rightarrow\alpha+\beta\in{}_{i+1}
holds.

(iii)

For all
1\leq i\leq r
,
\sigma(c({}_{i}{\,\smallsetminus\,}{}_{r}))\subseteq{}_{i}
.

Proof.

We give the desired partitions in Section
7.1.4
(type
A_{n-1}
), Section
7.2.4
(types
B_{m},C_{m}
), Section
7.4.9
(type
D_{m}
), Section
7.5.7
(type
{}^{2}A_{n-1}
) and Section
7.6.4
(type
{}^{2}D_{m}
) after the relevant notation is introduced. In each case, it is straightforward to check that the conditions of the lemma hold for the given partition; we omit this checking.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
I
be a finite set not containing
0
. Let
\lambda\colon I\rightarrow I\cup\{0\}
be a map such that
\lambda^{-1}(i)
has at most one element if
i\in I
. For any
i\in I\cap\lambda(I)
, let
\varepsilon_{i}\in\breve{k}^{\times}
. Consider the map
\delta\colon\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in I}L{\mathbb G}_{a}\rightarrow\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in I}L{\mathbb G}_{a}
given by
\delta((a_{i})_{i\in I})=(b_{i})_{i\in I}
, where
b_{i}=0
if
\lambda^{-1}(i)=\varnothing
and
b_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}\phi(a_{\lambda^{-1}(i)})
otherwise. Then the map
x\mapsto x-\delta(x)
is surjective for the pro-étale topology.

Proof.

There are some integers
s\geq r\geq 0
and a disjoint decomposition
I=\coprodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{r}I_{i}\dot{\cup}\coprodop\displaylimits_{i=r+1}^{s}I_{i}
such that for
1\leq i\leq r
,
\lambda
restricts to a map
I_{i}\rightarrow I_{i}
which is a full cycle of length
\#I_{i}
(first type), and for
r+1\leq i\leq s
,
\lambda
restricts to a map
I_{i}\rightarrow I_{i}\dot{\cup}\{0\}
, which is, after choosing an isomorphism
I_{i}\cong\{1,\dots,m\}
, given by
\lambda(j)=j-1
for all
j\in I_{i}
(second type). It suffices to show that the restriction of
\delta
to
\prodop\displaylimits_{j\in I_{i}}L{\mathbb G}_{a}
is surjective. Thus we may assume that
I
is one of the
I_{i}
. If
I=\{1,\dots,m\}
is of the second type, one has the filtration
G^{\bullet}
of
G=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}L{\mathbb G}_{a}
by closed subschemes (ind-group schemes)
G_{j}=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{j}L{\mathbb G}_{a}
, stable by
\delta
, such that
x\mapsto x-\delta(x)
induces the identity on the associated graded object
\gr\,G^{\bullet}
. From this, the result easily follows. If
I
is of the first type, we may assume
I={\mathbb Z}/m{\mathbb Z}
and
\lambda(i)=i+1
. Replacing
\phi
with
\phi^{m}
, one is then immediately reduced to the case that
m=1
and
\delta\colon L{\mathbb G}_{a}\rightarrow L{\mathbb G}_{a}
,
\delta(x)=\varepsilon\phi(x)
for some
\varepsilon\in\breve{k}^{\times}
. Then Lemma
5.9
finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\breve{k}
. Then the map
\id-\varepsilon\phi\colon L{\mathbb G}_{a}\rightarrow L{\mathbb G}_{a}
,
x\mapsto x-\varepsilon\phi(x)
is surjective for the pro-étale topology.

Proof.

If
\varepsilon\notin{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}^{\times}
, then
\id-\varepsilon\phi
is an isomorphism. Indeed, if
\ord_{\varpi}(\varepsilon)>0
,
\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{\infty}(\varepsilon\phi)^{i}
is a meaningful endomorphism of
L{\mathbb G}_{a}
(as
{\mathbb W}(R)
is
\varpi
-adically complete for each
R\in\Perf
) and the inverse of
\id-\varepsilon\phi
. If
\ord_{\varpi}(\varepsilon)<0
, a similar argument goes through using
\id-\varepsilon\phi=-\varepsilon\phi(\id-(\varepsilon\phi)^{-1})
. Now assume
\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}^{\times}
. We have
L{\mathbb G}_{a}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\rightarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{m\rightarrow-\infty}\varpi^{m}L^{+}{\mathbb G}_{a}
, and it suffices to show that
\id-\varepsilon\phi
is surjective on
L^{+}{\mathbb G}_{a}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle lim$\hfil\cr\kern 1.5pt\cr\leftarrow\textstyle\cr\kern-1.0pt\cr\cr}}}}\displaylimits_{r\rightarrow\infty}L_{r}^{+}{\mathbb G}_{a}
. This follows from a pro-version of Lang’s theorem. ∎

We now prove the surjectivity of
\alpha_{b}
. For a subset
\Psi\subseteq\Phi^{+}
closed under addition, let
{\mathbf{U}}
denote the unique closed subgroup of
{\mathbf{U}}
whose
\breve{k}
-points are generated by
\{{\mathbf{U}}_{\alpha}(\breve{k})\}_{\alpha\in\Psi}
. As a
\breve{k}
-scheme,
{\mathbf{U}}
is isomorphic to
\prodop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in\Psi}{\mathbf{U}}_{\alpha}
. Write
U=L{\mathbf{U}}
. Let
\{{}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
be a filtration as in Lemma
5.7
. For
1\leq i\leq r
, we have the closed subschemes (ind-group schemes)
C_{i}:=U_{{}_{i}}
of
L{\mathbf{U}}
, with
C_{1}=C
,
C_{r}=B
. Moreover, condition (
ii
) from Lemma
5.7
guarantees that
C_{i+1}\subseteq C_{i}
is a closed normal subgroup, with abelian quotient. As
H^{1}(k,{\mathbf{H}})=0
for a split unipotent
k
-group
{\mathbf{H}}
, we have
C_{i}/C_{i+1}\cong\prodop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in{}_{i}{\,\smallsetminus\,}{}_{i+1}}L{\mathbf{U}}_{\alpha}
. Moreover, by condition (
i
), for
1\leq i\leq r-1
,
C_{i}
has the closed subgroup
A_{i}=U_{{}_{i}{\,\smallsetminus\,}{}_{r}}
, and condition (
ii
) implies that
A_{i+1}
is normal in
A_{i}
and that the inclusion
A_{i}\hookrightarrow C_{i}
induces an isomorphism
\gamma_{i}\colon A_{i}/A_{i+1}\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,C_{i}/C_{i+1}
. Moreover, by condition (
iii
), the map
\sigma\circ\Ad(b)\colon A_{i}\rightarrow C_{i}
induces a map
\delta_{i}\colon A_{i}/A_{i+1}\rightarrow C_{i}/C_{i+1}
. The composition
\gamma_{i}^{-1}\delta_{i}
is an endomorphism of
A_{i}/A_{i+1}\cong\prodop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in{}_{i}{\,\smallsetminus\,}{}_{i+1}}L{\mathbf{U}}_{\alpha}
, which is of the form given in Lemma
5.8
. From Lemma
5.8
it follows that
a\mapsto a\cdot(\gamma_{i}^{-1}\delta_{i})(a)^{-1}
is a surjective endomorphism of
A_{i}/A_{i+1}
.

Let
R\in\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
. Let
x_{i}\in C_{i}(R)
, and let
\bar{x}_{i}
be its image in
(C_{i}/C_{i+1})(R)
. By the above, after replacing
R
with a pro-étale cover, we may find some
a_{i}\in A_{i}(R)
with image
\bar{a}_{i}\in(A_{i}/A_{i+1})(R)
such that
\bar{a}_{i}\cdot(\gamma_{i}^{-1}\delta_{i})(\bar{a}_{i}^{-1})=\gamma_{i}^{-1}(\bar{x}_{i})
. Applying
\gamma_{i}
, we deduce the equation
\gamma_{i}(\bar{a}_{i})^{-1}\bar{x}_{i}\delta_{i}(\bar{a}_{i})=1
in
(C_{i}/C_{i+1})(R)
. The left-hand side is precisely the image of
\widetilde{\alpha}_{b}(a_{i},x_{i})\in C_{i}(R)
in
(C_{i}/C_{i+1})(R)
. In other words, for each
x_{i}\in C_{i}(R)
, we may find (after possibly enlarging
R
) some
a_{i}\in A_{i}
such that
\widetilde{\alpha}_{b}(a_{i},x_{i})\in C_{i+1}
. Starting with an arbitrary
x=x_{1}\in C(R)
and applying this procedure consequently for
i=1,2,\dots,r-1
, we deduce that after possibly enlarging
R
, there are some
a_{1},\dots,a_{r-1}\in A(R)
such that for
a=a_{r-1}a_{r-2}\dots a_{1}
, we have
\widetilde{\alpha}_{b}(a,x)\in C_{r}(R)=B(R)
. (Here we use that
\widetilde{\alpha}_{b}(b,\widetilde{\alpha}_{b}(a,x))=\widetilde{\alpha}_{b}(ab,x)
.) This finishes the proof that
\alpha_{b}
is an isomorphism.

Next we turn to
\alpha_{b,{\mathbf{x}}}
. Note that it is well defined as for
x\in L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}})_{{\mathbf{x}}}
,
\sigma_{b}(x)\in L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})\cap L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}=L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}_{{\mathbf{x}}})
, the intersection taken in
L{\mathbf{G}}
. Being the restriction of
\alpha_{b}
, it is injective, as
\alpha_{b}
is, and it remains to prove its surjectivity. Applying
\Ad(b^{-1})
, which conjugates
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
to
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}
, where
{\mathbf{y}}=b^{-1}({\mathbf{x}})
, and the analogue of Lemma
5.6
, it suffices to show that (with the obvious notation) the map
\widetilde{\alpha}_{b,{\mathbf{x}}}\colon L^{+}({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{c^{-1}}{\mathbf{U}})_{{\mathbf{y}}}\times L^{+}({\mathbf{U}}\cap^{c^{-1}}{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{y}}}\rightarrow L^{+}{\mathbf{U}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}
,
\widetilde{\alpha}_{b}(x,y)=x^{-1}y\sigma(bxb^{-1})
is surjective. By the same reasoning as in Lemma
5.5
(and the paragraph preceding it), we are reduced to the case that
{\mathbf{G}}
is absolutely almost simple over
k
. For
\Psi\subseteq\Phi^{+}
, let
{\mathbf{U}}_{\Psi,{\mathbf{y}}}
denote the closure of
{\mathbf{U}}
in
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{y}}}
. We have
{\mathbf{U}}_{\Psi,{\mathbf{y}}}\cong\prodop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in\Psi}{\mathbf{U}}_{\alpha,{\mathbf{y}}}
. Replacing
C_{i},B,A_{i}
above with
C_{i}^{+}=L^{+}{\mathbf{U}}_{{}_{i},{\mathbf{y}}}
,
B^{+}=L^{+}({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{c^{-1}}{\mathbf{U}}^{-})
,
A_{i}^{+}=L^{+}{\mathbf{U}}_{{}_{i}{\,\smallsetminus\,}{}_{r},{\mathbf{y}}}
and using the integral version of Lemma
5.8
(with essentially the same proof), we can run the above inductive argument for the quotients
A_{i}^{+}/A_{i+1}^{+}
, deducing the surjectivity of
\alpha_{b,{\mathbf{x}}}
.
∎

5.2. A variant of a Deligne–Lusztig space

Throughout this section we work with the arc-topology on
\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
. Steinberg’s cross section allows us to give a different presentation of the Deligne–Lusztig spaces
X_{c}(b)
,
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
. We have the following general definition.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
w\in W
,
\dot{w}\in F_{w}(\breve{k})
and
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
. Define
\dot{X}_{\dot{w},b}
and
\tilde{X}_{w,b}
by the following cartesian diagrams:

\textstyle{\dot{X}_{\dot{w},b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L({}^{w}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\cdot\dot{w}b^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L{\mathbf{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g\mapsto g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)}
\textstyle{L{\mathbf{G}}}

and

\textstyle{\tilde{X}_{w,b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L({}^{w}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\cdot L{\mathbf{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L{\mathbf{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g\mapsto g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)}
\textstyle{L{\mathbf{G}}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

and let
X_{w,b}:=\tilde{X}_{w,b}/L{\mathbf{T}}
.

Remark 5.11
.

The map
g\mapsto g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)\colon L{\mathbf{G}}\rightarrow L{\mathbf{G}}
factors through
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b))\subseteq L{\mathbf{G}}
. Thus the map
\tilde{X}_{c,b}\rightarrow({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\cdot L{\mathbf{T}}
factors through
({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\cdot\kappa_{{\mathbf{G}}}|_{L{\mathbf{T}}}^{-1}(\kappa_{{\mathbf{G}}}(b))
. So, in the upper right entry of the right diagram of Definition
5.10
, we could have written
({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\cdot\kappa_{{\mathbf{G}}}|_{L{\mathbf{T}}}^{-1}(\kappa_{{\mathbf{G}}}(b))
without changing the definition.

The group
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)\times{\mathbf{T}}_{w}(k)
acts on
\dot{X}_{\dot{w},b}
by
g,t\colon x\mapsto gxt
, and
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
acts on
X_{w,b}
by
g\colon x\mapsto gx
. We only use these spaces in the case that
w=c
is a (special) Coxeter element.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
. Let
c\in W
be a Coxeter element and
\dot{c}\in F_{c}(\breve{k})
. Whenever the conclusion of Proposition
5.3
holds
(
e.g.,
{\mathbf{G}}
classical,
c
special Coxeter
)
, the following hold:

(i)

There is a
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)\times{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)
-equivariant isomorphism
\dot{X}_{\dot{c}}(b)\cong\dot{X}_{\dot{c},b}
.

(ii)

If
b\in F_{c}(\breve{k})
,
then there is a
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
-equivariant isomorphism
X_{c}(b)\cong X_{c,b}
.

Proof.

(
i
)
Consider the sheafification
\dot{X}^{\prime}
of the functor on
\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
,

X_{1}\colon R\longmapsto\left\{g\in L{\mathbf{G}}(R)\colon g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)\in L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})(R)\dot{c}b^{-1}=\dot{c}L{\mathbf{U}}(R)b^{-1}\right\}/L({\mathbf{U}}\cap{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})(R).

By
[
Iva23
, Proposition 12.1]

X_{\dot{c}}(b)
and
\dot{X}^{\prime}
are equivariantly isomorphic.
It remains to show that
\dot{X}^{\prime}\cong\dot{X}_{\dot{c},b}
equivariantly. Note that
\dot{X}_{\dot{c},b}
is the sheafification of

X_{2}\colon R\longmapsto\left\{g\in L{\mathbf{G}}(R)\colon g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)\in L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(R)\cdot\dot{c}b^{-1}\right\}.

There is an obvious equivariant map
X_{2}\rightarrow X_{1}
, of which we claim that it is an isomorphism. This follows once we check that

L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}})\times L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})\cdot\dot{c}b^{-1}\longrightarrow\dot{c}L{\mathbf{U}}b^{-1}=L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})\cdot\dot{c}b^{-1},\quad x,y^{\prime}\longmapsto x^{-1}y^{\prime}\sigma_{b}(x)

is an isomorphism. Substituting
y^{\prime}=y\dot{c}b^{-1}
, with
y
ranging in
L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})
, this map becomes
(x,y)\mapsto\left(x^{-1}y\dot{c}\sigma(x)\dot{c}^{-1}\right)\cdot\dot{c}b^{-1}
, which is an isomorphism by Proposition
5.3
. The proof of (
ii
) is completely analogous.
∎

Proposition 5.13
.

Suppose
{\mathbf{G}}
is of adjoint type. Let
c\in W
be as in Proposition
5.12
and
b\in F_{c}(\breve{k})
. For any
\dot{c}\in F_{c}(\breve{k})
satisfying
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b)=\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(\dot{c})
, there are
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
-equivariant isomorphisms
X_{c}(b)\cong X_{c,b}\cong\dot{X}_{\dot{c},b}\,/\,{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.12
, it suffices to show that
\dot{X}_{\dot{c},b}\,/{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)\cong X_{c,b}
. Writing

\tau:=\dot{c}^{-1}b\in(L{\mathbf{T}}\cap\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b)))(\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}),

these sheaves are the sheafifications of the quotient presheaves

\displaystyle Y_{1}\colon R

\displaystyle\longmapsto\{g\in L{\mathbf{G}}(R)\colon g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)\in L({\mathbf{U}}^{-}\cap{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})(R)\cdot\tau\}\,/\,{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)(R)\qquad\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle Y_{2}\colon R

\displaystyle\longmapsto\{g\in L{\mathbf{G}}(R)\colon g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)\in L({\mathbf{U}}^{-}\cap{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})(R)\cdot L{\mathbf{T}}(R)\}\,/L{\mathbf{T}}(R),

respectively. We have an obvious map
Y_{1}\rightarrow Y_{2}
, and we claim its sheafification is an isomorphism. First, the map between presheaves itself is injective (which already implies that its sheafification is injective, as quotient presheaves are separated). Indeed, let
g,h\in Y_{1}(R)
. Then
\sigma_{b}(g)=gu_{g}\tau
,
\sigma_{b}(h)=hu_{h}\tau
for some
u_{g},u_{h}\in L({\mathbf{U}}^{-}\cap{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})(R)
. Suppose their images in
Y_{2}(R)
agree,
i.e.
, there is some
t\in L{\mathbf{T}}(R)
such that
gt=h
. Then
\sigma_{b}(g)\sigma_{b}(t)=\sigma_{b}(h)
, and hence
gu_{g}\tau\sigma_{b}(t)=hu_{h}\tau
;
i.e.
,

\sigma_{c}(t)=\sigma_{b}(t)=\tau^{-1}u_{g}^{-1}tu_{h}\tau

(the first equality holds as
b\in F_{c}(\breve{k})
). Thus,
\sigma_{c}(t)=tu
for some
u\in L({\mathbf{U}}^{-}\cap{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}})(R)
, which implies
u=1
, and hence
t\in(L{\mathbf{T}})^{\sigma_{c}}(R)={\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)(R)
.

Secondly, let
g\in L{\mathbf{G}}(R)
satisfy
\sigma_{b}(g)=gu_{g}\tau^{\prime}
for some
u\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(R)
and
\tau^{\prime}\in L{\mathbf{T}}(R)
. By Remark
5.11
,
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(\tau^{\prime})=\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b)
. Changing
g
to
gt
for some
t\in L{\mathbf{T}}(R)
has no effect on the image of
g
in
Y_{2}(R)
but amounts to replacing
\tau^{\prime}
with
\tau^{\prime}t^{-1}\sigma(t)
. Thus, to show that
Y_{1}\rightarrow Y_{2}
is surjective when passing to sheafification, it remains to check that the
\sigma_{c}
-twisted action morphism

L{\mathbf{T}}\times(L{\mathbf{T}}\cap\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b)))\longrightarrow L{\mathbf{T}}\cap\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b)),\quad t,\tau^{\prime}\longmapsto t^{-1}\tau^{\prime}\sigma_{c}(t)

is surjective for the arc-topology. In fact, it is even surjective for the pro-étale topology. Indeed, the space
L{\mathbf{T}}\cap\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b))
is fibered over
(\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}|_{X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})})^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b))
, all fibers being isomorphic to
L^{+}{\mathcal{T}}
. Using pro-Lang for the
\sigma_{c}
-stable connected subscheme
L^{+}{\mathcal{T}}\subseteq L{\mathbf{T}}
, we are reduced to showing that the map of discrete abelian groups
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\times(\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}|_{X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})})^{-1}(\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(b))\rightarrow(\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}|_{X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})})^{-1}(\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}(b))
given
\mu,\chi\mapsto\chi+\sigma_{c}(\mu)-\mu
is surjective,
i.e.
, that the fibers of
\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}|_{X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})}
are torsors under
\im(\sigma_{c}-\id\colon X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\rightarrow X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}))=\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
. This holds by Remark
2.5
as
{\mathbf{G}}
is adjoint and
c
Coxeter.
∎

6. Decomposition into integral
\boldsymbol{p}
-adic spaces

In this section we begin with the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Before that, we show that this theorem is indeed sufficiently general to describe
all
spaces
X_{c}(b)
and
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
for an unramified classical group
{\mathbf{G}}
,
c
Coxeter and
b
basic.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
b\in{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
be basic,
c\in W
Coxeter and
\bar{c}\in\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
. Suppose
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)\neq\varnothing
.
Then there exist

•

a special Coxeter element
c_{1}\in W

(
cf. Definition
5.2

)
,

•

a lift
\bar{c}_{1}\in\overline{F}_{c_{1}}/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c_{1}}
and

•

an element
b_{1}\in[b]\cap F_{c}(\breve{k})
which lies over
\bar{c}_{1}

such that
X_{c}(b)\cong X_{c_{1}}(b_{1})
and
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)\cong\dot{X}_{\bar{c}_{1}}(b_{1})
,
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
-
(
resp.
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)\times{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)
-
)
equivariantly.

Proof.

By
[
Iva23
, Corollary 8.18]
there exists a special Coxeter element
c_{1}
such that
X_{c}(b)\cong X_{c_{1}}(b)
equivariantly. Moreover, we may find some
\bar{c}_{1}
over
c_{1}
such that
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(b)
is equivariantly isomorphic to
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}_{1}}(b)
;
cf.

[
Iva23
, Lemma 8.16]
. By Corollary
4.6
we may pick
b_{1}\in[b]\cap F_{c_{1}}(\breve{k})
lying over
\bar{c}_{1}
.
∎

The rest of the article is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Thus we start with

•

a special Coxeter element
c\in W
and
\bar{c}\in\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
,

•

a basic element
b
which lies in
F_{c}(\breve{k})
and lifts
\bar{c}
.

In Section
6.1
we reduce (
1.3
) to the case that
{\mathbf{G}}
is absolutely almost simple and adjoint. In Section
6.2
we then prove (
1.3
) by a
v
-descent argument, assuming the technical Proposition
6.3
(which essentially is (
1.4
) for absolutely almost simple adjoint groups,
cf.
Corollary
6.4
). Then it remains to deduce (
1.4
) from (
1.3
). This is done in Section
6.3
. Finally, in Section
7
we prove the remaining Proposition
6.3
, which is the most technical part of the proof. We also point out that specialness of
c
is only used in the proof of Proposition
6.3
and nowhere else in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

6.1. Reduction of (
1.3
) to the adjoint absolutely almost simple case

First, note that both sides of the equality claimed in the theorem only depend on
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
(for the left-hand side, use that
{\mathbf{G}}/{\mathbf{B}}={\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}/{\mathbf{B}}^{\ad}
). Hence we may assume that
{\mathbf{G}}
is of adjoint type. Then
{\mathbf{G}}
decomposes as a direct product of almost simple
k
-groups, and both sides of the claimed isomorphism (
1.3
) do so accordingly. Thus we are reduced to the case that
{\mathbf{G}}
is of adjoint type and almost simple.

If
{\mathbf{G}}
is adjoint and almost simple, then
{\mathbf{G}}=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
for some finite unramified extension
k^{\prime}/k
and some absolutely almost simple
k^{\prime}
-group
{\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}
. As
B({\mathbf{G}})=B({\mathbf{G}}^{\prime})
,
cf.

[
Kot85
, 1.10]
, (which allows us to change
b
appropriately inside its
\sigma
-conjugacy class), Lemma
6.2
reduces us to the case that
{\mathbf{G}}
is adjoint absolutely almost simple.

We state and prove Lemma
6.2
in bigger generality than just for adjoint absolutely almost simple groups and Coxeter elements. Let us fix notation first. Let
k^{\prime}/k
be a finite unramified extension of degree
d
, let
{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}
be an unramified reductive group over
k^{\prime}
, with maximal quasi-split torus
{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}
. Let
{\mathbf{H}}=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}
. Let
W^{\prime}
and
W\cong\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d}W^{\prime}
be the Weyl groups of
({\mathbf{H}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime})
and
({\mathbf{H}},\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}{\bf{\mathbf{T}}})
. Identifying the buildings of
{\mathbf{H}}
over
k
and of
{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}
over
k^{\prime}
, any point
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},\breve{k}}
defines a parahoric model
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
of
{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}
, and we have
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bf x}=\Res_{{\mathcal{O}}_{k^{\prime}}/{\mathcal{O}}_{k}}{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
;
cf.

[
HR20
, Proposition 4.7]
. Note that for definition (
1.1
) to work, we only need that the affine transformation
b\sigma
fixes
{\mathbf{x}}
(assumptions on
c
to be Coxeter and on
b
to be basic are redundant), so part (
ii
) of the following lemma makes sense.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
w^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
,
b^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(\breve{k})
, and let
w=(w^{\prime},1,\dots,1)\in W
,
b=(b^{\prime},1,\dots,1)\in{\mathbf{H}}(\breve{k})
.

(i)

We have
X_{w}^{{\mathbf{H}}}(b)\cong X_{w^{\prime}}^{{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}}(b^{\prime})
.

(ii)

If
b^{\prime}\in F_{w^{\prime}}(\breve{k})
is such that
b^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}
fixes
{\mathbf{x}}
, then
\dot{X}_{\bar{w},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{x}}\cong\dot{X}_{\bar{w}^{\prime},b^{\prime}}^{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}_{x}}
and
X_{w,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{x}}\cong X_{w^{\prime},b^{\prime}}^{{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}_{x}}
, where
\bar{w}

(
resp.
\bar{w}^{\prime})
is the image of
b

(
resp.
b^{\prime})
in
\overline{F}_{w}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w}

(
resp.
\overline{F}_{w^{\prime}}/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{w^{\prime}})
,

These natural isomorphisms are compatible with the natural identifications
{\mathbf{H}}(k)={\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}(k^{\prime})
and
{\mathcal{H}}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})={\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}}_{k^{\prime}})
.

Proof.

(
i
) Let
{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
be a
k^{\prime}
-rational Borel subgroup of
{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}
which contains
{\mathbf{T}}^{\prime}
, and let
{\mathbf{B}}=\Res_{k^{\prime}/k}{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
be the corresponding Borel subgroup of
{\mathbf{H}}
. As
\breve{k}
-groups, we have
{\mathbf{H}}\cong\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d}{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}
, with Frobenius permuting the components cyclically. As
L(\cdot)
commutes with products, we have
L({\mathbf{H}}/{\mathbf{B}})=\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d}L({\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}/{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})
. Let
g=(g_{1},\dots,g_{d})\in L({\mathbf{G}}/{\mathbf{B}})
. Then
g\in X_{w}(b)
if and only if the relative position of
(g_{1},\dots,g_{d})
and
(\sigma^{\prime}(g_{d}),g_{1},\dots,g_{d-1})
is
(w^{\prime},1,\dots,1)
, where
\sigma^{\prime}
is the geometric Frobenius on
L({\mathbf{G}}^{\prime}/{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime})
. This hold if and only if
g_{1}=g_{2}=\dots=g_{d}
and
g_{1}\in X_{w^{\prime}}^{{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}}(b)
. Thus
g\mapsto g_{1}
defines the claimed isomorphism.

(
ii
) Just as above, we have
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\cong\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{d}{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\prime}
. The same holds after applying
L^{+}(\cdot)
. Moreover,
{}^{w}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-}=({}^{w^{\prime}}{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime-},1,\dots,1)
. Thus,
g=(g_{1},\dots,g_{d})\in L^{+}{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
lies in
\dot{X}_{w,b}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
if and only if

g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)=(g_{1}^{-1}\Ad(b^{\prime})(\sigma^{\prime}(g_{d})),g_{2}^{-1}g_{1},\dots,g_{d}^{-1}g_{d-1}))\in(L^{+}({}^{w^{\prime}}{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime-}),1,\dots,1),

which is the case if and only if
g_{1}\in\dot{X}_{w,b}^{{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\prime}}
and the
g_{i}
(
i=2,\dots,d
) are determined by appropriate formulas in terms of
g_{1}
. Thus
g\mapsto g_{1}
defines the required isomorphism. The last isomorphism follows from the first.
∎

Altogether, in the proof of (
1.3
) we may assume
{\mathbf{G}}
to be absolutely almost simple and adjoint.

6.2. Disjoint decomposition

Here we reduce (
1.3
) to Proposition
6.3
.
Using Proposition
5.12
we may replace the left-hand side in (
1.3
), resp. (
1.4
), by
X_{c,b}
, resp.
\dot{X}_{b,b}
(as
b
lies over
\bar{c}
). Consider the following (auxiliary) subsheaf of
\dot{X}_{b,b}
:

\textstyle{\dot{X}_{b,b,{\mathcal{O}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L{\mathbf{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g\mapsto g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)}
\textstyle{L{\mathbf{G}}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

We then have the following result, whose proof occupies Section
7
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose
{\mathbf{G}}
is absolutely almost simple and adjoint. We have
\dot{X}_{b,b}=\dot{X}_{b,b,{\mathcal{O}}}
.

Corollary 6.4
.

Suppose
{\mathbf{G}}
is absolutely almost simple and adjoint.
We have a
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)\times{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)
-equivariant isomorphism

\dot{X}_{b,b}=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}\gamma\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}},

where
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
is as in (
1.1
).

Proof.

Consider the composed map

(6.1)

\dot{X}_{b,b}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow L{\mathbf{G}}\longrightarrow L{\mathbf{G}}/L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}},

where the second map is the natural projection. By Proposition
6.3
, for any
R\in\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
and any
g\in\dot{X}_{b,b}(R)
, we have
\sigma_{b}(g)\in gL^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}})_{{\mathbf{x}}}(R)\subseteq gL^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}(R)
. Now
L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\subseteq L{\mathbf{G}}
is a
\sigma_{b}
-stable subsheaf; thus
\sigma_{b}
acts on
L{\mathbf{G}}/L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
. It follows that (
6.1
) factors through
(L{\mathbf{G}}/L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}})^{\sigma_{b}}={\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
, which is a discrete set. The preimage of
1\cdot{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
is precisely
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
.
∎

We now prove (
1.3
) for
{\mathbf{G}}
as in Corollary
6.4
(this suffices by Section
6.1
). We need to descend the disjoint union decomposition of Corollary
6.4
to
X_{c,b}
. Recall that by
[
Ryd10
, Corollary 2.9]
, a quasi-compact morphism of schemes is universally subtrusive if and only if it satisfies the condition in
[
BS17
, Definition 2.1]
,
i.e.
, is a
v
-cover. We need the following version of
[
Ryd10
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
be a quasi-compact universally subtrusive map between schemes. Let
E\subseteq S
be a subset,
E^{\prime}=f^{-1}(E)
. Then
E
is closed
(
resp. open
)
if and only if
E^{\prime}
is closed
(
resp. open
)
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the claim for closed sets. The “only if” part is clear. For the other direction, it suffices by
[
Ryd10
, Corollary 1.5]
to show that
E
is closed in the constructible topology and stable under specializations if the same holds for
E^{\prime}
. As
f
is quasi-compact,
f
is closed in the constructible topology;
cf.

[
Ryd10
, Proposition 1.2]
. Hence
E=f(E^{\prime})
is closed in the constructible topology. Let
x_{0}\rightsquigarrow x_{1}
be a specialization relation in
S
with
x_{0}\in E
. As
f
is universally subtrusive,
x_{0}\rightsquigarrow x_{1}
lifts to a specialization relation
y_{0}\rightsquigarrow y_{1}
. Then
y_{0}\in E^{\prime}
and as
E^{\prime}
is closed, also
y_{1}\in E^{\prime}
. Hence
x_{1}=f(y_{1})\in f(E^{\prime})=E
. ∎

The space
X_{c,b}
is an ind-scheme by Proposition
5.12
and
[
Iva23
, Theorem C]
. Thus for any map
T\rightarrow X_{c,b}
with
T
a quasi-compact scheme, the fiber product
T\times_{X_{c,b}}\dot{X}_{b,b}
with the scheme
\dot{X}_{b,b}
is again a scheme. Moreover, the map
T\times_{X_{c,b}}\dot{X}_{b,b}\rightarrow T
is quasi-compact and a
v
-cover by Corollary
4.5
. Hence this map is universally subtrusive,
cf.

[
Ryd10
, Corollary 2.9]
, and by Lemma
6.5
and Corollary
6.4
,
T
admits a disjoint union decomposition
T=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in G_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}T_{\gamma}
which is functorial in
T
. Doing this for all maps
T\rightarrow X_{c,b}
from a quasi-compact scheme
T
into
X_{c,b}
, we deduce a disjoint union decomposition of
X_{c,b}
indexed by the same set. The subscheme (ind-scheme) corresponding to
\gamma=1
is the quotient of
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
by
{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)
, which equals
X_{c,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
by definition. This proves (
1.3
).

6.3. Proof of (
1.4
)

Using Proposition
5.12
we may replace the left-hand side in (
1.4
) by
\dot{X}_{b,b}
(as
b
lies over
\bar{c}
). If
{\mathbf{G}}
is of adjoint type, (
1.4
) follows from Corollary
6.4
and the discussion in Section
6.1
. We now proceed in two steps.

6.3.1. Special case: The center
{\mathbf{Z}}
of
{\mathbf{G}}
is an unramified induced torus

Lemma 6.6
.

The natural map
p\colon{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})\rightarrow{\mathbf{G}}_{b}^{\ad}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}^{\ad}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
is surjective, and its fibers are
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
-torsors.

Proof.

As
{\mathbf{Z}}
is an induced torus,
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathbf{Z}})=0
. Hence
{\mathbf{Z}}(k)\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}_{b}(k)
is exact. Let
{\mathcal{Z}}
denote the reductive
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
-model of
{\mathbf{Z}}
. Using
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}))=0
(by the pro-version of Lang’s theorem), we deduce the exact sequence
{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}^{\ad}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
. Factoring this sequence out from the first one, and using
{\mathbf{Z}}(k)/{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})\cong X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
(which again follows from
H^{1}(\sigma,{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}))=0
), we obtain the lemma.
∎

Let
\pi\colon L{\mathbf{G}}\rightarrow L{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
be the natural map. It is surjective as
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})
is. For
g\in{\mathbf{G}}
, write
\bar{g}
for
\pi(g)
. For any
\tau\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})
, choose a lift
\tilde{\tau}\in{\mathbf{Z}}(\breve{k})
subject to the condition that
\tilde{\tau}\in{\mathbf{Z}}(k)
if
z\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
. We have
\pi^{-1}(L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad})=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\tau\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})}\tilde{\tau}L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{x}}
(indeed,
L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\subseteq\pi^{-1}(L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad})
, and the quotient is the discrete scheme
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})
). Consequently, if
\bar{\gamma}\in{\mathbf{G}}_{b}^{\ad}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}^{\ad}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
with a fixed lift
\gamma\in{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
, then

(6.2)

\pi^{-1}(\bar{\gamma}\dot{X}_{b,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}})=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\tau\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})}\gamma\tilde{\tau}Y,\quad\text{ where }Y\mathop{\overset{\,\mathrm{def}\,}{\scalebox{2.2}[1]{=}}}\left\{g\in L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\colon\bar{g}^{-1}\sigma_{b}(\bar{g})\in L^{+}\left({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}^{\ad}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{\ad-}\right)_{{\mathbf{x}}}\right\}.

Lemma 6.7
.

If
\tau\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}}){\,\smallsetminus\,}X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
, then
\dot{X}_{b,b}\cap\gamma\tilde{\tau}Y=\varnothing
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
(\dot{X}_{b,b}\cap\gamma\tilde{\tau}Y)({\mathfrak f})=\varnothing
for any algebraically closed field
{\mathfrak f}\in\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
. Let
g\in L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathfrak f})
be such that
\gamma\tilde{\tau}g\in(\dot{X}_{b,b}\cap\gamma\tilde{\tau}Y)({\mathfrak f})
. Then
g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)\in\pi^{-1}(L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}^{\ad}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{\ad-})_{{\mathbf{x}}})({\mathfrak f})=
(L^{+}{\mathcal{Z}})({\mathfrak f})\cdot L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathfrak f})
, where
{\mathcal{Z}}
is the reductive
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
-model of
{\mathbf{Z}}
. Write
g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)=\zeta u
with
\zeta\in(L^{+}{\mathcal{Z}})({\mathfrak f})
and
u\in L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathfrak f})
. Then

(\gamma\tilde{\tau}g)^{-1}\sigma_{b}(\gamma\tilde{\tau}g)=\tilde{\tau}^{-1}\sigma(\tilde{\tau})g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)=\tilde{\tau}^{-1}\sigma(\tilde{\tau})\zeta u,

where we have
\sigma_{b}(\tilde{\tau})=\sigma(\tilde{\tau})
as
\tilde{\tau}
is central. This cannot lie in
L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})({\mathfrak f})
as the image of
\zeta\in L^{+}{\mathcal{Z}}({\mathfrak f})\subseteq L{\mathbf{Z}}({\mathfrak f})
in
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})
is
0
but the image of
\tilde{\tau}^{-1}\sigma(\tilde{\tau})
in
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})
is non-zero by assumption.
∎

As
\dot{X}_{b,b}\subseteq\pi^{-1}(\dot{X}_{b,b}^{\ad})
, the disjoint decomposition for
\dot{X}_{b,b}^{\ad}
(
cf.
beginning of Section
6.3
) implies, together with (
6.2
) and Lemmas
6.6
and
6.7
, that
\dot{X}_{b,b}
is the disjoint union of all
\gamma\dot{X}_{b,b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
, where
\gamma
varies through
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
, which is precisely (
1.4
).

6.3.2. General case

An arbitrary
{\mathbf{G}}
admits a derived embedding
{\mathbf{G}}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
such that
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
is unramified reductive and the center of
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
is an unramified induced torus. (This was pointed out to us by M. Borovoi;
cf.

[
Bor21
]
.) Now the result follows from Section
6.3.1
and the next proposition.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
{\mathbf{G}}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
be a derived embedding. If (
1.4
) holds for
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
, then it holds for
{\mathbf{G}}
.

Proof.

We identify
{\mathbf{G}}
with a subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
. Knowing
\dot{X}_{b,b}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}\gamma{\dot{X}}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
as subsheaves of
L\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
, we have to show that
\dot{X}_{b,b}=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}\gamma\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
{\mathfrak f}\in\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
be an algebraically closed field. Let
L:={\mathbb W}({\mathfrak f})[1/\varpi]
. We have
{\mathbf{G}}(L)\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})={\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
.

Proof.

By
[
HR08
, Proposition 3]
we have
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})=\Fix_{{\mathbf{G}}(L)}({\mathbf{x}})\cap\ker\tilde{\kappa}_{{\mathbf{G}}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})=\Fix_{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}(L)}({\mathbf{x}})\cap\ker\tilde{\kappa}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}
, where
\Fix({\mathbf{x}})
denotes the stabilizer of the vertex
{\mathbf{x}}
in the respective group (we identify the adjoint groups of
{\mathbf{G}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
). As
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{G}})
is injective,
cf.

[
Bor98
, Lemma 1.5]
, we have
\ker\tilde{\kappa}_{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\cap{\mathbf{G}}(L)=\ker\tilde{\kappa}_{{\mathbf{G}}}
. As the actions on the adjoint building are compatible with the inclusion
{\mathbf{G}}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
, we have
\Fix_{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}(L)}({\mathbf{x}})\cap{\mathbf{G}}(L)=\Fix_{{\mathbf{G}}(L)}({\mathbf{x}})
. Putting all these together, we deduce
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})\cap{\mathbf{G}}(L)={\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
.
∎

Taking
\sigma_{b}
-invariants in Lemma
6.9
, we deduce that
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})\cap{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)={\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
. In particular, the natural map
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
is injective. Pick some
\bar{\gamma}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
, represented by
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
.

Lemma 6.10
.

If
\gamma\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\cap L{\mathbf{G}}\neq\varnothing
, then
\bar{\gamma}\in{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
.

Proof.

By assumption, the sheaf
\gamma\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\cap L{\mathbf{G}}
admits a geometric point. Let
{\mathfrak f}\in\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
be algebraically closed, such that there exists some
x\in\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}({\mathfrak f})
with
\gamma x\in L{\mathbf{G}}({\mathfrak f})
. Write
L={\mathbb W}({\mathfrak f})[1/\varpi]
. By the definition of
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
, we have

x^{-1}\sigma_{b}(x)\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})\subseteq{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})

(here, we identify
{\mathbf{U}}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}
with the unipotent radical of a Borel of
\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}
). As
H^{1}(\sigma_{b},L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}})=0
,
we find some
\zeta\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
such that
x^{-1}\sigma_{b}(x)=\zeta^{-1}\sigma_{b}(\zeta)
. In particular,
x\zeta^{-1}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})^{\sigma_{b}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
. From this and
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
, it follows that
\gamma x\zeta^{-1}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)
. Moreover,
\gamma x\zeta^{-1}\in{\mathbf{G}}(L)
as
\gamma x\in{\mathbf{G}}(L)
and
\zeta\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}(L)
. Combining these observations, we deduce
\gamma x\zeta^{-1}\in{\mathbf{G}}(L)^{\sigma_{b}}={\mathbf{G}}_{b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
. Finally,
x\zeta^{-1}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
; thus
\gamma
and
\gamma x\zeta^{-1}
represent the same class in
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
as
{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
is injective. The lemma is proved.
∎

Next we claim that

(6.3)

\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\cap L{\mathbf{G}}=\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
X
be an ind-(reduced scheme) over
\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}
and let
Y,Z
be two closed subfunctors, which are themselves ind-(reduced schemes). If
Y({\mathfrak f})=Z({\mathfrak f})
for any algebraically closed field
{\mathfrak f}/\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}
, then
Y=Z
.

Proof.

Write
X=\mathop{\,\underrightarrow{\lim\,}}_{i}X_{i}
for qcqs reduced schemes
X_{i}
and an index set
I
. Then
Y\cap X_{i},Z\cap X_{i}
are closed reduced subschemes of
X_{i}
, which agree on geometric points. Thus
Y\cap X_{i}=Z\cap X_{i}
for each
i
, and hence also
Y=Z
as the
X_{n}
exhaust
X
.
∎

By combining Lemma
6.11
with Lemma
6.9
we see that
L{\mathbf{G}}\cap L^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}=L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
(note that any ind-(perfect scheme) is automatically an ind-(reduced scheme)). As
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\subseteq L^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
by definition, we deduce
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\cap L{\mathbf{G}}=\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\cap L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
. Now,
\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\cap L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}=\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}
is immediate, proving (
6.3
).

Finally, observe that
\dot{X}_{b,b}=\dot{X}_{b,b}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\cap L{\mathbf{G}}
. Now we compute

\displaystyle\dot{X}_{b,b}

\displaystyle=\dot{X}_{b,b}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}}\cap L{\mathbf{G}}=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}\left(\gamma\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\cap L{\mathbf{G}}\right)=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}\left(\gamma\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\cap L{\mathbf{G}}\right)

\displaystyle=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}\gamma\left(\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}\cap L{\mathbf{G}}\right)=\coprodop\displaylimits_{\gamma\in{\mathbf{G}}_{b}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},b}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})}\gamma\dot{X}_{\bar{c},b}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}},

where the second equality is by the assumption of the proposition, the third is by Lemma
6.10
and the fifth holds by (
6.3
). This proves the proposition.
∎

6.4. Quasi-split case

We work in the setup of Section
1.3
. In particular, we have the Coxeter element
c
, and we have
\bar{c}\in[1]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}
(if
\bar{c}\in\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}{\,\smallsetminus\,}[1]
, then
\dot{X}_{\bar{c}}(1)=\varnothing
by Corollary
4.6
). First, we justify that a choice of
\dot{c}
as claimed in Section
1.3
is indeed possible. It is well known (in the quasi-split case) that
{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}},c)\,/\Ad{\mathbf{G}}(k)
is in natural bijection with
{\mathbf{G}}(k)
-orbits in the set of hyperspecial vertices of
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbf{G}},k}={\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbf{G}},\breve{k}}^{\langle\sigma\rangle}
(
cf.

[
DeB06
]
or
[
DI20
, Lemma 2.6.2]
). Let
[{\mathbf{T}}_{\bar{c}}]\in{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}},c)\,/\Ad{\mathbf{G}}(k)
be the class corresponding to
\bar{c}
under the surjection
[1]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}},c)\,/\Ad{\mathbf{G}}(k)
from Proposition
3.7
. Then let
{\mathbf{x}}
be any hyperspecial vertex in
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbf{G}},k}
corresponding to
[{\mathbf{T}}_{\bar{c}}]
under the above bijection. As
{\mathbf{x}}
is hyperspecial,
c
admits a lift
\dot{c}\in F_{c}(\breve{k})\cap{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{x}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})
. As
c
is Coxeter, the affine transformation
\dot{c}\sigma
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},\breve{k}}
admits a unique fixed point, which necessarily equals
{\mathbf{x}}
. Comparing the two parametrizations of
{\mathfrak T}({\mathbf{G}},c)\,/\Ad{\mathbf{G}}(k)
above, we see that the image of
\dot{c}
equals the image of
\bar{c}
in
([1]\cap\overline{F}_{c}\,/\ker\bar{\kappa}_{c})\,/C_{W}(c\sigma)
. Replacing
\dot{c}
with
\gamma\dot{c}
for an appropriate
\gamma\in C_{W}(c\sigma)
, we have that
\dot{c}
lifts
\bar{c}
.

Now we prove Corollary
1.3
. We only prove the first isomorphism (the second is completely analogous). We may assume that
{\mathbf{G}}
is adjoint. By the pro-version of Lang’s theorem for
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
, we may find an
h\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})
such that
h^{-1}\dot{c}\sigma(h)=1
. By
[
Iva23
, Remark 8.7]
,
g\mapsto hg\colon L{\mathbf{G}}\rightarrow L{\mathbf{G}}
induces an isomorphism
X_{c}(\dot{c})\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,X_{c}(1)
which is equivariant with respect to
\Ad(h)\colon{\mathbf{G}}(k)\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,{\mathbf{G}}_{\dot{c}}(k)
. Moreover,
\Ad(h)
restricts to an isomorphism
{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},\dot{c}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})
, thus inducing
{\mathbf{G}}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,{\mathbf{G}}_{\dot{c}}(k)/{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}},\dot{c}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}})
. Exploiting Theorem
1.1
for
X_{c}(\dot{c})
, we are reduced to showing that
g\mapsto hg\colon L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\rightarrow L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}
induces an isomorphism
X_{c,\dot{c}}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,X_{c}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}(1)
. We compute (quotients are taken in the category of arc-sheaves)

\displaystyle X_{c,\dot{c}}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}^{\ad}}

\displaystyle=\{g\in L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\colon g^{-1}\sigma_{\dot{c}}(g)\in L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}\}/{\mathbf{T}}_{c}(k)

\displaystyle\cong\{g\in L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\colon g^{-1}\sigma_{\dot{c}}(g)\in L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}\cdot L^{+}{\mathcal{T}}\}/L^{+}{\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle\cong\{g\in L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\colon g^{-1}\sigma(g)\in L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}\cdot L^{+}{\mathcal{T}}\cdot\dot{c}\}/L^{+}{\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle\cong\{g\in L^{+}{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\colon g^{-1}\sigma(g)\in\dot{c}\cdot L^{+}{\mathbf{B}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\}/L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}})_{{\mathbf{x}}}L^{+}{\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle\cong\{g\in L^{+}({\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}/{\mathbf{B}}_{{\mathbf{x}}})\colon g^{-1}\sigma(g)\in L^{+}{\mathbf{B}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\cdot\dot{c}\cdot L^{+}{\mathbf{B}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\}/L^{+}{\mathbf{B}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\cong X_{c}^{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}}}(1),

where the third isomorphism is
g\mapsto hg
and in the fourth isomorphism, we exploit the last statement of Proposition
5.3
.

7. Estimates via Newton polygons

In this section we prove Proposition
6.3
for an absolutely almost simple classical group
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
of adjoint type (so, compared to Section
6.2
, we change our notation, and the group which was denoted by
{\mathbf{G}}
there is
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
here). It will be more convenient to work with a certain central extension
p\colon{\mathbf{G}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
, whose kernel
{\mathbf{Z}}=\ker(p)
is an unramified torus. (Which central extension we take is specified in each particular case of our case-by-case study below; see Sections
7.1.1
,
7.2.1
,
7.3.1
,
7.4.1
,
7.5.1
,
7.6.1
.) Then we have the exact sequence

(7.1)

X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\longrightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\longrightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\longrightarrow 0.

Remark 7.1
.

In our case-by-case study below, the left map will be injective, except in type
{}^{2}A_{n-1}
with
n
even. (This is clear in the split cases
A_{n-1},B_{m},C_{m},D_{m}
; see Sections
7.5.2
and
7.6.2
for the cases
{}^{2}A_{n-1}
(
n
odd) and
{}^{2}D_{m}
.) In type
{}^{2}A_{n-1}
with
n
even, the left map will be the zero map
{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}\rightarrow{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
(see Section
7.5.2
).

We let
\bar{b}\in F_{c}^{\ad}(\breve{k})\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}(\breve{k})
be a basic element lying over a Coxeter element
c\in W
, so that we have to show that
X_{\bar{b},\bar{b}}^{\ad}=X_{\bar{b},\bar{b},{\mathcal{O}}}^{\ad}
within
L{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
.
It suffices to show that the Lang map
g\mapsto g^{-1}\sigma_{\bar{b}}(g)\colon X_{\bar{b},\bar{b}}^{\ad}\rightarrow L({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}})
factors through the closed subscheme
L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}})_{{\mathbf{x}}}
. As all rings in
\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
are reduced,
it suffices to do so on geometric points. Let
{\mathfrak f}
denote a fixed algebraically closed field in
\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
and put
L:={\mathbb W}({\mathfrak f})[1/\varpi]
.

As
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
is surjective,
\bar{b}
lifts to a basic element
b\in F_{c}(\breve{k})\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
. For any element
\bar{\zeta}\in\ker\left(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\right)
, fix a representative
\zeta\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})
of
\bar{\zeta}
. For each such
\zeta
, we have the commutative diagram

(7.2)

\textstyle{\dot{X}_{b\varpi^{\zeta},b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{b,\zeta}}
\textstyle{\dot{X}_{\bar{b},\bar{b}}^{\ad}}
\textstyle{\dot{X}_{b\varpi^{\zeta},b,{\mathcal{O}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dot{X}_{\bar{b},\bar{b},{\mathcal{O}}}^{\ad}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the objects on the left (resp. right) are contained in
L{\mathbf{G}}
(resp.
L{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}
), and where

\dot{X}_{b\varpi^{\zeta},b,{\mathcal{O}}}\mathop{\overset{\,\mathrm{def}\,}{\scalebox{2.2}[1]{=}}}\text{preimage of $L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}\varpi^{\zeta}$ under $g\mapsto g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)\colon L{\mathbf{G}}\rightarrow L{\mathbf{G}}$}

is a subsheaf of
\dot{X}_{b\varpi^{\zeta},b}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

We have

\bigcupop\displaylimits_{\bar{\zeta}\in\ker\left(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\right)}q_{b,\zeta}(\dot{X}_{b\varpi^{\zeta},b}({\mathfrak f}))=\dot{X}_{\bar{b},\bar{b}}({\mathfrak f}).

Proof.

Let
\bar{g}\in\dot{X}_{\bar{b},\bar{b}}^{\ad}({\mathfrak f})
. The map
L{\mathbf{G}}({\mathfrak f})\rightarrow L{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}({\mathfrak f})
is surjective (as
H^{1}(L,{\mathbf{Z}})=0
). Thus we may lift
\bar{g}
to some
g\in L{\mathbf{G}}({\mathfrak f})
. Then
g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)=uz
with
u\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(L)
and
z\in{\mathbf{Z}}(L)
. On the other hand, it is clear that under
\kappa_{{\mathbf{G}}}\colon{\mathbf{G}}(L)\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
, the element
g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)
maps to
0
. As also
\kappa_{{\mathbf{G}}}(({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(L))=0
, we deduce that
\kappa_{{\mathbf{G}}}(z)=0
. Thus the image
\bar{\zeta}
of
z
in
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
lies in
\ker\left(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\right)
. The exactness of (
2.4
) implies that there exists some
\tau\in{\mathbf{Z}}(L)
such that
z=\tau^{-1}\sigma(\tau)\varpi^{\zeta}
. Replacing
g
with
g\tau^{-1}
and recalling that
{\mathbf{Z}}
is central in
{\mathbf{G}}
, we get
g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(L)\varpi^{\zeta}
,
i.e.
,
g\in\dot{X}_{b\varpi^{\zeta},b}({\mathfrak f})
.
∎

To show that the right arrow in (
7.2
) is surjective, it suffices (using Lemma
7.2
) to show that the left vertical arrow is surjective on
{\mathfrak f}
-points for all
\bar{\zeta}
. Thus, finally, we are reduced to showing that the inclusion
\dot{X}_{b\varpi^{\zeta},b,{\mathcal{O}}}({\mathfrak f})\hookrightarrow\dot{X}_{b\varpi^{\zeta},b}({\mathfrak f})
is surjective,
i.e.
, that

(7.3)

\forall g\in\dot{X}_{b\varpi^{\zeta},b}({\mathfrak f}),\quad\text{ we have }\quad g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)\in L^{+}({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}}({\mathfrak f})\varpi^{\zeta};

we will do this in the following subsections case by case. As mentioned in Remark
7.1
, we have
\#\ker\left(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\right)=1
, and it will suffice to prove only the surjectivity of
\dot{X}_{b,b,{\mathcal{O}}}({\mathfrak f})\hookrightarrow\dot{X}_{b,b}({\mathfrak f})
, with the only exception (treated in Section
7.5.6
) in the case
{}^{2}A_{n-1}
with
n
even, when
\#\ker\left(X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\right)=2
. We will use the following well-known property of isocrystals.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
(V,\varphi)
be an isocrystal over
{\mathfrak f}
which is isoclinic of slope
\lambda
and dimension
n\geq 1
. Let
v
be a
cyclic vector
of
(V,\varphi)
, that is, an element
v\in V
such that the set
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{n-1}
is a basis of
V
.
Write
\varphi^{n}(v)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{n-1}A_{i}\varphi^{i}(v)
with
A_{i}\in L
. Then
\ord_{\varpi}(A_{i})\geq(n-i)\lambda
.

Proof.

The Newton polygon of
(V,\varphi)
is the straight line segment connecting the points
(0,0)
and
(n,\lambda n)
in the plane. It coincides with the Newton polygon of the polynomial
\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{n}A_{i}X^{i}\in L[X]
with
A_{n}=1
, which is by definition the convex hull of the points
(i,\ord_{\varpi}(A_{n-i}))
;
cf.

[
Bea12
, Sections 3.1 and 3.2]
or
[
Ked04
, Proposition 5.7]
.
∎

Finally, note that if
g\in\dot{X}_{b,b}({\mathfrak f})
, then
g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)=y\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(L)
,
i.e.
, we have

(7.4)

b\sigma(g)=gyb.

We will now proceed case by case. Below we will denote the fixed point
{\mathbf{x}}
of
b\sigma
by
{\mathbf{x}}_{b}
(to make the notation non-ambiguous). Also recall the notation from Section
2
, especially Section
2.1.2
.

7.1. Type
\boldsymbol{A_{n-1}}

This case is treated in
[
CI21a
,
CI23
]
. We include it for the sake of completeness.

7.1.1.

Let
n\geq 2
, and let
V_{0}
be an
n
-dimensional
k
-vector space with an (ordered) basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
. Whenever convenient, identify
V_{0}
with
k^{n}
via this basis. Let
{\mathbf{G}}={\bf GL}_{n}(V_{0})
.

7.1.2.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}\subseteq{\mathbf{B}}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}
be the diagonal torus and the upper triangular Borel subgroup. Then
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\cong\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb Z}\beta_{i}
, with
\beta_{i}\colon t\mapsto(1^{\oplus i-1},t,1^{\oplus n-i})
. Then
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})={\mathbb Z}\Phi^{\vee}=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n-1}{\mathbb Z}\cdot\left(\beta_{i+1}-\beta_{i}\right)
,
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})={\mathbb Z}\cdot\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i}
.
Then
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})=X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbb Z}\Phi^{\vee}+X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})\cong{\mathbb Z}/n{\mathbb Z}
, the isomorphism given by sending
1
to the class
\bar{\beta}_{i}
of
\beta_{i}
for any
i
.

We have
\Phi=\{\alpha_{i-j}\colon 1\leq i<j\leq n\}
, where
\alpha_{i-j}\colon(t_{\lambda})_{\lambda=1}^{n}\mapsto t_{i}t_{j}^{-1}
and
\Delta=\{\alpha_{i-(i+1)}\colon 1\leq i\leq n-1\}
. We have the Coxeter element

(7.5)

c=s_{\alpha_{1-2}}s_{\alpha_{2-3}}\dots s_{\alpha_{(n-1)-n}}=(1,2,\dots,n).

For
0\leq\kappa<n
, consider the lift
\dot{c}_{\kappa}
of
c
to
{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
given by
e_{i}\mapsto e_{i+1}
for
1\leq i<n
and
e_{n}\mapsto\varpi^{\kappa}e_{1}
. Then
\dot{c}_{\kappa}
maps to
\kappa\bar{\beta}_{1}\in\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})
, and
{\mathbf{G}}_{\dot{c}_{\kappa}}
run through all inner forms of
{\mathbf{G}}
.

We identify
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}}^{\ad},\breve{k}}
with
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\ad})_{{\mathbb R}}
by sending
0\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{{\mathbb R}}
to the
\sigma
-stable hyperspecial point corresponding to
\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}e_{i}\subseteq V_{0}\otimes_{k}\breve{k}
.

The group
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-}
is generated by the root subgroups attached to
\alpha_{i-1}
(
1<i\leq n
). An element
y\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(R)
is given by
e_{1}\mapsto e_{1}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{n}a_{i-1}e_{i}
;
e_{i}\mapsto e_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq n
.

7.1.3.

Let
b=\dot{c}_{\kappa}
with
0\leq\kappa<n
. We compute
{\mathbf{x}}_{b}=\left(-\frac{(i-1)\kappa}{n}\right)_{i=1}^{n}
. Let
g\in\dot{X}_{b,b}({\mathfrak f})
. Then (
7.4
) holds with
y
as described in Section
7.1.2
. We have to show that
y\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}_{b}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
, or equivalently (see
e.g.

[
MP94
, Section 2.5]
)

(7.6)

\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq\langle\alpha_{(i+1)-1},{\bf x}_{b}\rangle=-\frac{\kappa i}{n}\qquad\forall 1\leq i\leq n-1.

Consider the
\phi
-linear isocrystal
(V,\varphi)=(V_{0}\otimes_{k}L,b\sigma)
over
{\mathfrak f}
. It is isoclinic of slope
\frac{\kappa}{n}
. For
1\leq i\leq n
, let
g_{i}=g(e_{i})
. Then
\sigma(g)(e_{i})=\sigma(g_{i})
for all
i
. Thus, (
7.4
) implies
\varphi(g_{i})=gyb(e_{i})
. Using this equation for all
i\neq n
and writing
v:=g_{1}
, we deduce
g_{i}=\varphi^{i-1}(v)
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
. In particular,
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{n-1}
is an
L
-basis of
V
. Using
\varphi(g_{i})=gyb(e_{i})
for
i=n
and inserting the values for
g_{i}
, we arrive at the equation

\varphi^{n}(v)=\varpi^{\kappa}v+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n-1}\varpi^{\kappa}a_{i}\varphi^{i}(v).

Now Lemma
7.3
shows that
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq\frac{\kappa}{n}(n-i)-\kappa=-\frac{\kappa i}{n}
. This gives (
7.6
), and we are done.

7.1.4. Proof of Lemma
5.7
for type
\boldsymbol{A_{n-1}}

The action of
c
on
\Phi^{+}
is as follows:
c(\alpha_{i-j})=\alpha_{(i+1)-(j+1)}
for
1\leq i<j<n
;
c(\alpha_{i-n})\in\Phi^{-}
for
1\leq i<n
. For
1\leq i\leq r=n-1
, put
{}_{i}=\{\alpha_{i^{\prime}-j^{\prime}}\in\Phi^{+}\colon j^{\prime}\geq i+1\}
.

7.2. Type
\boldsymbol{C_{m}}

7.2.1.

Let
m\geq 2
. Let
V_{0}
be a
2m
-dimensional
k
-vector space with an (ordered) basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{m},

e_{-m},\dots e_{-1}\}
. Whenever convenient, we will identify
V_{0}
with
k^{2m}
via this basis. Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
be the alternating bilinear form on
V_{0}
determined by
\langle e_{\pm i},e_{\pm j}\rangle=0
if
i\neq j
and
\langle e_{i},e_{-i}\rangle=1
for
1\leq i\leq m
. Let
{\mathbf{G}}
be the closed
k
-subgroup of
{\bf GL}(V_{0})
of elements preserving
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
up to a scalar;
i.e.
,

{\mathbf{G}}(R)=\{g\in{\bf GL}(V_{0})(R)\colon\langle gu,gv\rangle=\lambda(g)\langle u,v\rangle\text{ for all $u,v\in V_{0}\otimes_{k}R$}\},

where
\lambda(g)\in R^{\times}
is a unit only depending on
g
. The group
{\mathbf{G}}
is the
group of symplectic similitudes
, and the character
\lambda\colon{\mathbf{G}}\rightarrow{\mathbb G}_{m}
is called the
