similitude character
.

7.2.2.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}\subseteq{\mathbf{B}}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}
be the diagonal torus and the Borel subgroup consisting of upper triangular matrices in
{\mathbf{G}}
. Then
{\mathbf{T}}=\{(t_{0}t_{1},\dots,t_{0}t_{m},t_{m}^{-1},\dots,t_{1}^{-1})\colon t_{i}\in{\mathbb G}_{m}\}
and
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{m}{\mathbb Z}\varepsilon_{i}
, where

\varepsilon_{0}\colon t\longmapsto(t^{\oplus m},1^{\oplus m})\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon_{i}\colon t\longmapsto(1^{\oplus i-1},t,1^{\oplus 2(m-i-1)},t^{-1},1^{\oplus i-1})\text{ for $1\leq i\leq m$}

are cocharacters of
{\mathbf{T}}
. We have
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\ad})=X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbb Z}\cdot(2\varepsilon_{0}-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\varepsilon_{i})
and
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}{\mathbb Z}\varepsilon_{i}
. In particular,
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})=X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\ad})/X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})\cong{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
, generated by the class of
\varepsilon_{0}
.

We have
\Phi=\{\alpha_{\pm i\pm j}\colon(t_{i})_{i=1}^{m}\mapsto t_{i}^{\pm 1}t_{j}^{\pm 1}\colon 1\leq i<j\leq m\}\cup\{\alpha_{\pm 2\cdot i}\colon(t_{i})_{i=1}^{m}\mapsto t_{i}^{\pm 2}\colon 1\leq i\leq m\}
and
\Delta=\{\alpha_{i-(i+1)}\colon 1\leq i\leq m-1\}\cup\{\alpha_{2\cdot m}\}
. We have the Coxeter element

(7.7)

c=s_{\alpha_{1-2}}\cdot\dots\cdot s_{\alpha_{(m-1)-m}}s_{\alpha_{2m}}=(1,2,\dots,m-1,m,2m,2m-1,\dots,m+1).

We consider two lifts
\dot{c}_{0}
,
\dot{c}_{1}
of
c
to
{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
. The first is given by
\dot{c}_{0}(e_{\pm i})=e_{\pm(i+1)}
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
,
\dot{c}_{0}(e_{\pm m})=e_{\mp 1}
. The second is
\dot{c}_{1}=\dot{c}_{0}\varepsilon_{0}(\varpi)
. Then
\dot{c}_{0}
,
\dot{c}_{1}
are basic, and the image of
\dot{c}_{0}
, resp.
\dot{c}_{1}
, in
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}(\breve{k})
represents
0\in\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})
, resp.
\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\in\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})
. Thus the pairs
({\mathbf{G}},\sigma_{\dot{c}_{i}})
(
i=1,2
) represent the two inner forms of
{\mathbf{G}}
.

We identify
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}}^{\ad},\breve{k}}
with
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\ad})_{\mathbb R}
by sending
0\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\mathbb R}
to the
\sigma
-stable hyperspecial point (“the origin”) corresponding to the self-dual lattice
\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}e_{i}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}e_{-i}\right)\subseteq V_{0}\otimes_{k}\breve{k}
.

The group
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-}
is generated by root subgroups attached to
\alpha_{i-1}
(
2\leq i\leq m
) and
\alpha_{-2\cdot 1}
. Explicitly, any element
y\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(R)
is an
R
-linear map in
{\bf GL}(V_{0})(R)
given by
e_{1}\mapsto e_{1}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{m}a_{i-1}e_{i}+a_{m}e_{-m}
;
e_{i}\mapsto e_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq m
and
i=-1
;
e_{-i}\mapsto e_{-i}-a_{i-1}e_{-1}
for all
2\leq i\leq m
.

7.2.3.

Let
b=\dot{c}_{\kappa}
with
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
. We compute that
{\mathbf{x}}_{b}=0
if
\kappa=0
and that
{\mathbf{x}}_{b}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\varepsilon_{i}
with
\lambda_{i}=-\frac{m}{4}+\frac{i-1}{2}
for
1\leq i\leq m
if
\kappa=1
. Let
g\in\dot{X}_{b,b}({\mathfrak f})
. Then (
7.4
) holds with
y
as described in Section
7.2.2
, depending on some
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in L
.
We have to show that
y\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}_{b}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
, which is (as in Section
7.1.3
) equivalent to

(7.8)

\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq\frac{\kappa i}{2}\text{\quad$\forall\,1\leq i\leq m$}.

Consider the
\phi
-linear isocrystal
(V,\varphi)=(V_{0}\otimes_{k}L,b\sigma)
over
{\mathfrak f}
. It is isoclinic of slope
\frac{\kappa}{2}
. For
i\in\{\pm 1,\dots,\pm m\}
, let
g_{i}:=g(e_{i})
. Then
\sigma(g)(e_{i})=\sigma(g_{i})
for all
i
. Hence (
7.4
) implies
\varphi(g_{i})=gyb(e_{i})
. Using this equation for all
i\neq m+1
and writing
v:=g_{1}
, we deduce that

g_{i}=\varpi^{\kappa(1-i)}\varphi^{i-1}(v)
for
2\leq i\leq m
,
g_{-1}=\varpi^{-\kappa m}\varphi^{m}(v)
and

g_{-i}=\varpi^{-\kappa m}\varphi^{m+i-1}(v)+\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{i-1}\varpi^{-\kappa m}\phi^{i-1-j}(a_{j})\varphi^{m+i+1-j}(v)\quad\text{for $2\leq i\leq m$.}

In particular,
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m-1}
is a basis of
V
;
i.e.
,
v
is a cyclic vector for
V
. Finally, applying
\varphi(g_{i})=gyb(e_{i})
for
i=m+1
and inserting the above formulas for the
g_{i}
, we deduce

\varphi^{2m}(v)=\varpi^{\kappa m}v+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\varpi^{\kappa(m-i)}a_{i}\varphi^{i}(v)-\sumop\displaylimits_{m+1}^{2m}\phi^{i-m}(a_{2m-i})\varphi^{i}(v).

Now Lemma
7.3
shows that
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq 0
for all
1\leq i\leq m
if
j=0
and that
\ord_{\varpi}(\varpi^{m-i}a_{i})\geq\frac{1}{2}(2m-i)
,
i.e.
, that
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq\frac{i}{2}
for all
1\leq i\leq m
, if
j=1
. This shows (
7.8
), and we are done.

7.2.4. Proof of Lemma
5.6
for types
\boldsymbol{C_{m}}
and
\boldsymbol{B_{m}}

The action of
c
on
\Phi^{+}
is as follows: for
1\leq i<j<m
,
c(\alpha_{i\pm j})=\alpha_{(i+1)\pm(j+1)}
; for
1\leq i<m
,
c(\alpha_{i-m})=\alpha_{1+(i+1)}
; for
1\leq i\leq m-1
,
c(\alpha_{i+m})\in\Phi^{-}
; for
1\leq i\leq m-1
,
c(\alpha_{2\cdot i})=\alpha_{2\cdot(i+1)}
;
c(\alpha_{2\cdot m})\in\Phi^{-}
.

We set
r=m+1
;
{}_{m+1}=\{\alpha_{i+m}\}_{i=1}^{m-1}\cup\{\alpha_{2\cdot m}\}
;
{}_{m}=\{\alpha_{i+j}\colon 1\leq i<j\leq m\}\cup\{\alpha_{2\cdot i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
; and for
1\leq i_{0}\leq m-1
,
{}_{i_{0}}={}_{m}\cup\{\alpha_{i-j}\colon 1\leq i<j\leq m\text{ and $i_{0}<j$}\}
. Replacing all roots with the corresponding coroots, we obtain by duality the desired partition for type
B_{m}
(under the induced isomorphism of Weyl groups, the special Coxeter element for type
C_{m}
maps to the one in Section
7.3.2
).

7.3. Type
\boldsymbol{B_{m}}

7.3.1.

Let
m\geq 2
. Let
V_{0}
be a
(2m+1)
-dimensional
k
-vector space with an (ordered) basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{m},e_{m+1},e_{-m},\dots e_{-1}\}
. Whenever convenient, identify
V_{0}
with
k^{2m+1}
via this basis. Let
Q\colon V_{0}\rightarrow k
be the split quadratic form given by
Q\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}(a_{i}e_{i}+a_{-i}e_{i})+a_{m+1}e_{m+1}\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}a_{-i}+a_{m+1}^{2}
. Let
{\mathbf{G}}={\bf SO}(V_{0},Q)
be the split odd orthogonal group attached to
(V_{0},Q)
;
i.e.
,

{\mathbf{G}}(R)=\{g\in{\bf GL}(V_{0})(R)\colon\langle gu,gv\rangle=\langle u,v\rangle\text{ for all $u,v\in V_{0}\otimes_{k}R$, $\det(g)=1$}\}.

It is of adjoint type. (The computations below work independently of the characteristic and the residue characteristic of
k
.)

7.3.2.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}\subseteq{\mathbf{B}}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}
be the diagonal torus and the Borel subgroup consisting of upper triangular matrices in
{\mathbf{G}}
. Then
{\mathbf{T}}=\{(t_{1},\dots,t_{m},1,t_{m}^{-1},\dots,t_{1}^{-1})\colon t_{i}\in{\mathbb G}_{m}\}
and
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}{\mathbb Z}\varepsilon_{i}
, where

\varepsilon_{i}\colon t\longmapsto(1^{\oplus i-1},t,1^{\oplus 2(m-i-1)},t^{-1},1^{\oplus i-1})\text{ for $1\leq i\leq m$}

are cocharacters of
{\mathbf{T}}
. We have
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})=\{\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})\colon\sumop\displaylimits_{i}a_{i}\,\,\text{even}\}
. Thus
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})=X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})/X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})\cong{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
, generated by the class of
\varepsilon_{i}
for any
i
.

We have
\Phi=\{\alpha_{\pm i\pm j}\colon(t_{\lambda})_{\lambda=1}^{m}\mapsto t_{i}^{\pm 1}t_{j}^{\pm 1}\colon 1\leq i<j\leq m\}\cup\{\alpha_{i}\colon(t_{\lambda})_{\lambda=1}^{m}\mapsto t_{i}\colon 1\leq i\leq m\}
and
\Delta=\{\alpha_{i-(i+1)}\colon 1\leq i\leq m-1\}\cup\{\alpha_{m}\}
. We have the Coxeter element

(7.9)

c=s_{\alpha_{1-2}}\cdot\dots\cdot s_{\alpha_{(m-1)-m}}s_{\alpha_{m}}=(1,2,\dots,m,2m+1,2m,\dots,m+2).

We consider two lifts
\dot{c}_{0}
,
\dot{c}_{1}
of
c
to
{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
. The first is given by
\dot{c}_{0}(e_{\pm i})=e_{\pm(i+1)}
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
,
\dot{c}_{0}(e_{\pm m})=e_{\mp 1}
and
\dot{c}_{0}(e_{m+1})=-e_{m+1}
. The second is given by
\dot{c}_{1}(e_{\pm i})=e_{\pm(i+1)}
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
,
\dot{c}_{1}(e_{\pm m})=\varpi^{\pm 1}e_{\mp 1}
and
\dot{c}_{1}(e_{m+1})=-e_{m+1}
. Then
\dot{c}_{0}
,
\dot{c}_{1}
are basic, and the image of
\dot{c}_{0}
, resp.
\dot{c}_{1}
, in
{\mathbf{G}}^{\ad}(\breve{k})
represents
0\in\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
, resp.
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\in\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
. Thus the
{\mathbf{G}}_{\dot{c}_{i}}
(
i=1,2
) are the two inner forms of
{\mathbf{G}}
.

We identify
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}},\breve{k}}
with
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\mathbb R}
by sending
0\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\mathbb R}
to the
\sigma
-stable hyperspecial point (“the origin”) corresponding to the self-dual lattice
\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}e_{i}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}e_{-i}\right)\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}e_{m+1}\subseteq V_{0}\otimes_{k}\breve{k}
.

The group
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-}
is generated by root subgroups attached to
\alpha_{i-1}
(
2\leq i\leq m
) and
\alpha_{-1}
. Explicitly, any element
y\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(R)
is an
R
-linear map in
{\bf GL}(V_{0})(R)
given by
e_{1}\mapsto e_{1}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{m}a_{i-1}e_{i}+a_{m}e_{m+1}-a_{m}^{2}e_{-1}
;
e_{i}\mapsto e_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq m
and
i=-1
;
e_{m+1}\mapsto e_{m+1}-2a_{m}e_{-1}
;
e_{-i}\mapsto e_{-i}-a_{i-1}e_{-1}
for
2\leq i\leq m
.

7.3.3.

Let
b=\dot{c}_{\kappa}
with
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
. We have
{\mathbf{x}}_{b}=0
if
\kappa=0
and
{\mathbf{x}}_{b}=-\frac{1}{2}\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\varepsilon_{i}
if
\kappa=1
. Let
g\in\dot{X}_{b,b}({\mathfrak f})
. Then (
7.4
) holds with
y
as described in Section
7.3.2
, depending on some
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in L
. We have to show that
y\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}_{b}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
, which is (as in Section
7.1.3
) equivalent to

(7.10)

\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq 0\text{\quad$\forall\,1\leq i\leq m-1$}\quad\text{ and }\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{m})\geq\frac{\kappa}{2}.

Consider the
\phi
-linear isocrystal
(V,\varphi)=(V_{0}\otimes_{k}L,b\sigma)
over
{\mathfrak f}
. It is isoclinic of slope
0
. For
i\in\{\pm 1,\dots,\pm m,m+1\}
, let
g_{i}:=g(e_{i})
. Then (
7.4
) implies that
\varphi(g_{i})=gyb(e_{i})
for all
i
.
Using this equation for all
i\neq m+1,m+2
and writing
v:=g_{1}
and
u:=g_{m+1}
, we see
that
g_{i}=\varphi^{i-1}(v)
for
2\leq i\leq m
,
g_{-1}=\varpi^{-\kappa}\varphi^{m}(v)
and

g_{-i}=\varpi^{-\kappa}\left(\varphi^{m+i-1}(v)+\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{i-1}\phi^{i-1-j}(a_{j})\varphi^{m+i-1-j}(v)\right)\quad\text{for $2\leq i\leq m$.}

In particular, it follows that
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m-1}\cup\{u\}
are linearly independent over
L
. Now, evaluating the equation
\varphi(g_{i})=gyb(e_{i})
for
i=m+1
and
i=m+2
gives

(7.11)

\displaystyle 2a_{m}\varphi^{m}(v)

\displaystyle=u+\varphi(u),

(7.12)

\displaystyle\varphi^{2m}(v)

\displaystyle=v+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m-1}a_{i}\varphi^{i}(v)-a_{m}^{2}\varpi^{-\kappa}\varphi^{m}(v)-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=m+1}^{2m}\phi^{i-m}(a_{2m-i})\varphi^{i}(v)+a_{m}u.

First suppose
a_{m}=0
. Then (
7.12
) together with the linear independence of
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m-1}
shows that
v
spans a
2m
-dimensional sub-isocrystal of
V
and is a cyclic vector in it. Then Lemma
7.3
and (
7.12
) show that
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq 0
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
.

Now suppose
a_{m}\neq 0
. Then
a_{m}\in L^{\times}
, and
\lambda:=\frac{a_{m}}{\phi(a_{m})}\in L^{\times}
satisfies
\ord_{\varpi}(\lambda)=0
. Apply
\lambda\varphi(\cdot)
to (
7.12
), and add the result to (
7.12
); then use (
7.11
) to eliminate
u
:

\displaystyle\lambda\varphi^{2m+1}(v)

\displaystyle=v+(a_{1}+\lambda)\varphi(v)+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{m-1}(a_{i}+\lambda\phi(a_{i-1}))\varphi^{i}(v)+\ast\cdot\varphi^{m}(v)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\phi(a_{m-1})-\lambda\phi(a_{m})^{2}\varpi^{-\kappa}\right)\varphi^{m+1}(v)+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=m+2}^{2m}\ast\cdot\varphi^{i}(v),

where the
\ast
denote some unspecified elements of
L
. Moreover,
v
is a cyclic vector for
V
. Indeed,
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m-1}\cup\{u\}
is a basis of
V
, and hence it follows from (
7.12
) and
a_{m}\neq 0
, that
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m}
also is. Using the cyclicity of
v
, the last equation, the fact that
\ord_{\varpi}(\lambda)=0
and Lemma
7.3
, we deduce by induction on
i
that
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq 0
for
i=1,\dots,m
and that, moreover,
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})\geq\frac{1}{2}
if
\kappa=1
. This agrees with what we had to show in (
7.10
).

7.4. Type
\boldsymbol{D_{m}}

7.4.1.

Let
m\geq 4
. Let
V_{0}
be a
2m
-dimensional
k
-vector space with an (ordered) basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{m},

e_{-m},\dots e_{-1}\}
. Whenever convenient, we use this basis to identify
V_{0}
with
k^{2m}
.
Consider the quadratic form
Q\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}(a_{i}e_{i}+a_{-i}e_{-i})\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}a_{-i}
on
V_{0}
. We have the orthogonal group
{\mathbf{O}}(V_{0},Q)\subseteq{\bf GL}(V_{0})
. Via the Clifford algebra attached to
Q
, we have the surjective Dickson morphism
D_{Q}\colon{\mathbf{O}}(V_{0},Q)\rightarrow{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
;
cf.

[
Con14
, Proposition C.2.2]
.
Its kernel is the special orthogonal group
{\bf SO}(V_{0},Q)
of
Q
. Finally, let
{\mathbf{G}}={\bf GSO}(V_{0},Q)
be the closed subgroup of
{\bf GL}(V_{0})
of elements which up to a scalar lie in
{\bf SO}(V_{0},Q)
.
(6)
(6)

(6)

If
\charac k\neq 2
, then the Dickson morphism is not necessary to describe
{\mathbf{G}}
. For any
k
-algebra
R
, we have

{\mathbf{G}}(R)=\left\{g\in{\bf GL}(V_{0})(R)\colon Q(gv)=\lambda(g)Q(v)\text{ for all $u,v\in V_{0}\otimes_{k}R$\, and \,$\frac{\det(g)}{\lambda(g)^{m}}=1$}\right\},

where
\lambda(g)\in R^{\times}
is a unit only depending on
g
.

7.4.2.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}\subseteq{\mathbf{B}}\subseteq{\mathbf{G}}
be the diagonal torus and the Borel subgroup consisting of upper triangular matrices in
{\mathbf{G}}
. Then
{\mathbf{T}}=\{(t_{0}t_{1},\dots,t_{0}t_{m},t_{m}^{-1},\dots,t_{1}^{-1})\colon t_{i}\in{\mathbb G}_{m}\}
and
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{m}{\mathbb Z}\varepsilon_{i}
, where

\varepsilon_{0}\colon t\longmapsto(t^{\oplus m},1^{\oplus m})\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon_{i}\colon t\longmapsto(1^{\oplus i-1},t,1^{\oplus 2(m-i)},t^{-1},1^{\oplus i-1})\text{ for $1\leq i\leq m$}

are cocharacters of
{\mathbf{T}}
. We have
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm sc}})={\mathbb Z}\Phi^{\vee}=\{\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\colon\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\lambda_{i}\text{\, even}\}
,
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\mathop{\rm der}})=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}{\mathbb Z}\varepsilon_{i}
and
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})={\mathbb Z}(2\varepsilon_{0}-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\varepsilon_{i})
. We have
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})=X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})/{\mathbb Z}\Phi^{\vee}+X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})
. If
m
is even,
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})\cong{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}\times{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
, generated by the classes of
\varepsilon_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{i}
for an arbitrary
1\leq i\leq m
. If
m
is odd,
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})\cong{\mathbb Z}/4{\mathbb Z}
, generated by the class of
\varepsilon_{0}
. In any case,
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})
has the distinguished subgroup
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\mathop{\rm der}})\cong{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
, generated by the class of some (
i.e.
, any)
\varepsilon_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq m
).

We have
\Phi=\{\alpha_{\pm i\pm j}\colon(t_{\lambda})_{\lambda=1}^{m}\mapsto t_{i}^{\pm 1}t_{j}^{\pm 1}\colon 1\leq i<j\leq m\}
and
\Delta=\{\alpha_{i-(i+1)}\colon 1\leq i\leq m-1\}\cup\{\alpha_{(m-1)+m}\}
. We have the Coxeter element

(7.13)

c=s_{\alpha_{1-2}}\cdot\dots\cdot s_{\alpha_{(m-1)-m}}s_{\alpha_{(m-1)+m}}=(1,2,\dots,m-2,m-1,2m,2m-1,\dots,m+1)(m,m+1).

We consider three lifts
\dot{c}_{0}
,
\dot{c}_{1}
,
\dot{c}_{2}
of
c
to
{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
. For
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
,
\dot{c}_{\kappa}
is given by
\dot{c}_{\kappa}(e_{\pm i})=e_{\pm(i+1)}
for
1\leq i\leq m-2
,
\dot{c}_{\kappa}(e_{\pm(m-1)})=e_{\pm 1}
,
\dot{c}_{\kappa}(e_{\pm m})=\varpi^{\mp\kappa}e_{\mp m}
. The third is given by
\dot{c}_{2}=\dot{c}_{0}\varpi^{\varepsilon_{0}}
. Then
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}(\dot{c}_{0})=0
,
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}(\dot{c}_{1})
is the non-trivial class in
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\mathop{\rm der}})
and
\tilde{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}(\dot{c}_{2})=\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\in\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}){\,\smallsetminus\,}\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\mathop{\rm der}})
. Thus the
{\mathbf{G}}_{\dot{c}_{\kappa}}
(
\kappa\in\{0,1,2\}
) are three of the four inner forms of
{\mathbf{G}}
. The fourth inner form is conjugate to
({\mathbf{G}},\dot{c}_{2})
by the outer automorphism of
{\mathbf{G}}
induced by an inner automorphism of the orthogonal group
{\bf GO}(V_{0})
; hence we need not to consider it separately.

We identify
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}}^{\ad},\breve{k}}
with
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\ad})_{\mathbb R}
by sending
0\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\mathbb R}
to the
\sigma
-stable hyperspecial point (“the origin”) corresponding to the self-dual lattice
\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\left({\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}e_{i}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}e_{-i}\right)\subseteq V_{0}\otimes_{k}\breve{k}
.

The group
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-}
is generated by root subgroups attached to
\alpha_{i-1}
(
2\leq i\leq m
) and
\alpha_{-1-m}
. Explicitly, any element
n\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(R)
is an
R
-linear map in
{\bf GL}(V_{0})(R)
given by
e_{1}\mapsto e_{1}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{m}a_{i-1}e_{i}+a_{m}e_{-m}-a_{m-1}a_{m}e_{-1}
;
e_{i}\mapsto e_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq m-1
and
i=-1
;
e_{m}\mapsto e_{m}-a_{m}e_{-1}
;
e_{-i}\mapsto e_{-i}-a_{i-1}e_{-1}
for all
2\leq i\leq m
.

7.4.3.

Let
b=\dot{c}_{\kappa}
with
\kappa\in\{0,1,2\}
. We compute
{\mathbf{x}}_{b}=0
if
\kappa=0
;
{\mathbf{x}}_{b}=-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{m}
if
\kappa=1
;
{\mathbf{x}}_{b}=-\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{m}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m-1}(-\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{2}(i-1))\varepsilon_{i}
if
\kappa=2
. Let
g\in\dot{X}_{b,b}({\mathfrak f})
. Then (
7.4
) holds, with
n
as described in Section
7.4.2
, depending on some
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in L
. We have to show that
y\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}_{b}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
, which (as in Section
7.1.3
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq 0\,\text{ for }1\leq i\leq m

if
\kappa=0
,

(7.14)

\displaystyle\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq 0\,\text{ for }1\leq i\leq m-1\;\text{ and }\;\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{m})\geq 1

if
\kappa=1
,

\displaystyle\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq\frac{i}{2}\,\text{ for }1\leq i\leq m-2,\;\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{m-1})\geq\frac{m-2}{4}\;\text{ and }\;\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{m})\geq\frac{m}{4}

if
\kappa=2
.

Consider the
\phi
-linear isocrystal
(V,\varphi)=(V_{0}\otimes_{k}L,b\sigma)
over
{\mathfrak f}
. It is isoclinic of slope
0
if
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
and slope
\frac{1}{2}
if
\kappa=2
. For
i\in\{\pm 1,\dots,\pm m\}
, put
g_{i}:=\varphi(e_{i})
. Then (
7.4
) implies that
\varphi(g_{i})=gyb(e_{i})
for all
i
. Using this equation for all
i\neq m+1,m+2
and writing
v:=g_{1}
and
u:=g_{m}
, we compute that
g_{i}=\varpi^{A_{i}}\varphi^{i-1}(v)
for
2\leq i\leq m
,
g_{-1}=\varpi^{A_{-1}}\varphi^{m}(v)
,
g_{-m}=\varpi^{A_{-m}}\varphi(u)+\varpi^{A^{\prime}_{-m}}a_{m-1}\varphi^{m-1}(v)
and

g_{-i}=\varpi^{A_{-i}}\left(\varphi^{m+i-2}(v)+\sumop\displaylimits_{d=m-1}^{m-3+i}\phi^{d-m+1}(a_{m-d+i-2})\varphi^{d}(v)\right)\quad\text{for $2\leq i\leq m-1$,}

where
A_{i}=A^{\prime}_{-m}=0
for all
i\in\{\pm 1,\dots,\pm m\}
if
\kappa=0
;
A_{i}=A^{\prime}_{-m}=0
for all
i\neq-m
and
A_{-m}=-1
if
\kappa=1
;
A_{i}=1-i
for
2\leq i\leq m-1
,
A_{-1}=A^{\prime}_{-m}=A_{-i}=1-m
for
2\leq i\leq m-1
and
A_{-m}=-1
if
\kappa=2
.

Lemma 7.4
.

The vectors
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m-3}
,
u,\varphi(u)
form a basis of
V
.

Proof.

From the formulas for the
g_{\pm i}
above, it follows that the vectors are linearly independent, hence form a basis by dimension reasons.
∎

Now, evaluating the equation
\varphi(g_{i})=gyb(e_{i})
for
i=m+1
and
i=m+2
gives the formulas

(7.15)

\displaystyle\varphi^{2m-2}(v)

\displaystyle=\varpi^{B_{0}}v+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m-2}\varpi^{B_{i}}a_{i}\varphi^{i}(v)-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=m}^{2m-3}\phi^{i-m+1}(a_{2m-2-i})\varphi^{i}(v)+\varpi^{B_{0}^{\prime}}a_{m-1}u+\varpi^{B_{1}^{\prime}}a_{m}\varphi(u),

(7.16)

\displaystyle\varphi^{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\varpi^{C_{0}}u-\varpi^{C_{1}}\phi(a_{m-1})\varphi^{m}(v)-\varpi^{C_{2}}a_{m}\varphi^{m-1}(v),

where all
B_{i},B_{i}^{\prime},C_{i}
are
0
if
\kappa=0
;
B_{1}^{\prime}=-1
,
C_{1}=1
and all other
B_{i},B_{i}^{\prime},C_{i}
are
0
if
\kappa=1
;
B_{i}=m-1-i
for
0\leq i\leq m-2
,
B_{0}^{\prime}=B_{1}^{\prime}=m-2
,
C_{0}=1
and
C_{1}=C_{2}=2-m
if
\kappa=2
. Put

\mu:=\varpi^{D}a_{m}\phi(a_{m})-a_{m-1}\phi(a_{m-1})\in L,

where
D=0
if
\kappa=0
;
D=-2
if
\kappa=1
;
D=1
if
\kappa=2
. We have five cases, which we consider separately in the following subsections.

7.4.4. Case
\boldsymbol{a_{m}\neq 0}
,
\boldsymbol{a_{m-1}\neq 0}
,
\boldsymbol{\mu\neq 0}

Suppose
a_{m}a_{m-1}\mu\neq 0
. Apply
\frac{a_{m}}{\phi(a_{m-1})}\varphi(\cdot)
to (
7.15
), and subtract (
7.15
) from the resulting equation. This gives an expression of
\frac{a_{m}}{\phi(a_{m-1})}\varphi^{2m-1}(v)
as a linear combination of
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m-2}
,
\varphi^{2}(u)
and
u
. Substitute (
7.16
) into this expression to eliminate
\varphi^{2}(u)
. The coefficient of
u
in the resulting equation is
\mu\neq 0
, and it gives an expression of
u
as a linear combination of
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m-1}
:

\displaystyle\varpi^{E}u\ =\

\displaystyle\varpi^{E_{0}}\frac{\phi(a_{m-1})}{\mu}v-\frac{\varpi^{E_{1}}a_{m}-\varpi^{E_{1}^{\prime}}\phi(a_{m-1})a_{1}}{\mu}\varphi(v)-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{m-2}\frac{\varpi^{E_{i}}a_{m}\phi(a_{i-1})-\varpi^{E_{i}^{\prime}}\phi(a_{m-1})a_{i}}{\mu}\varphi^{i}(v)

(7.17)

\displaystyle-\frac{\varpi^{E_{m-1}}a_{m}\phi(a_{m-2})-\varpi^{E_{m-1}^{\prime}}a_{m}^{2}\phi(a_{m})}{\mu}\varphi^{m-1}(v)

\displaystyle-\frac{\phi(a_{m-1})\phi(a_{m-2})-\varpi^{E_{m}^{\prime}}\phi(a_{m-1})a_{m}\phi(a_{m})))}{\mu}\varphi^{m}(v)+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=m+1}^{2m-1}\ast\cdot\varphi^{i}(v)+\varpi^{E_{2m-1}}\frac{a_{m}}{\mu}\varphi^{2m-1}(v),

where the
\ast\in L
denote unspecified coefficients; all
E,E_{i},E^{\prime}_{i}=0
if
\kappa=0
;
E=E_{0}=E_{i}^{\prime}=0
(
1\leq i\leq m-2
),
E_{m}^{\prime}=E_{2m-1}=E_{i}=-1
(
1\leq i\leq m-1
),
E_{m-1}^{\prime}=-2
if
\kappa=1
;
E=m-2
,
E_{0}=m-1
,
E_{i}=m-i
and
E_{i}^{\prime}=m-1-i
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
,
E_{m}^{\prime}=E_{2m-1}=0
if
\kappa=2
.

As
a_{m}\neq 0
, the coefficient of
\varphi^{2m-1}(v)
in (
7.17
) is non-zero. Using this observation, as well as the version of it after applying
\varphi(\cdot)
, shows, together with Lemma
7.4
, that
v
is a cyclic vector for
V
. Now insert (
7.17
) into (
7.15
) to eliminate
u
there. This gives (after multiplication with an element in
L^{\times}
) an expression

\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}\varphi^{2m}(v)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{2m-1}\beta_{i}\varphi^{i}(v)

with
\beta_{i}\in L
satisfying
\ord_{\varpi}(\beta_{i})\geq(2m-i)\cdot\lambda
, where
\lambda
is the slope of
(V,\varphi)
, by Lemma
7.3
. Let
\gamma:=-\frac{a_{m-1}}{\phi(a_{m})}+\frac{\phi^{2}(a_{m-1})\mu}{\phi(a_{m})\phi(\mu)}
.

Lemma 7.5
.

We have
\ord_{\varpi}(\gamma)\geq 0
if
\kappa=0
,
\ord_{\varpi}(\gamma)\geq-1
if
\kappa=1
and
\ord_{\varpi}(\gamma)\geq 1
if
\kappa=2
.

Proof.

One checks that
\gamma=\varpi^{D}\cdot\frac{\phi^{2}(a_{m-1})a_{m}-\phi^{2}(a_{m})a_{m-1}}{\phi(\mu)}
. First assume
\kappa=0
. If
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1})>\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})
, then
\ord_{\varpi}(\phi^{2}(a_{m-1})a_{m}-\phi^{2}(a_{m})a_{m-1})\geq\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1})+\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})\geq 2\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})=\ord_{\varpi}(\phi(\mu))
, and we are done as
D=0
in this case. One concludes similarly in the case
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})<\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1})
. Finally, suppose
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1})=\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})=:\alpha
. Write
x=\varpi^{\alpha}a_{m}
,
y=\varpi^{\alpha}a_{m-1}
, so that
x,y\in{\mathcal{O}}_{L}^{\times}
. Write
A:=x\phi(x)-y\phi(y),B:=x\phi^{2}(y)-y\phi^{2}(x)\in{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
. It suffices to show that
\ord_{\varpi}(B)\geq\ord_{\varpi}(A)
. We now compute in
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}/\left(\varpi^{\ord_{\varpi}(A)}\right)
, where we surely have
A=0
,
i.e.
,
x\phi(x)=y\phi(y)
, or equivalently
\phi\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)=\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{-1}
. Exploiting this equation twice, we see
\phi^{2}\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)=\phi\bigl(\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{-1}\bigr)=\frac{x}{y}
,
which shows that
\phi^{2}(x)y=x\phi^{2}(y)
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}\,/\left(\varpi^{\ord_{\varpi}(A)}\right)
,
i.e.

B=0
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}\,/\left(\varpi^{\ord_{\varpi}(A)}\right)
,
i.e.
,
\ord_{\varpi}(B)\geq\ord_{\varpi}(A)
.

Suppose
\kappa=1
. Write
a_{m}=\varpi a_{m}^{\prime}
. Then
\gamma=\varpi^{-1}\cdot\frac{\phi^{2}(a_{m-1})a_{m}^{\prime}-\phi^{2}(a_{m}^{\prime})a_{m-1}}{\phi(a_{m}^{\prime}\phi(a_{m}^{\prime})-a_{m-1}\phi(a_{m-1}))}
, where the fraction is just
\gamma
from case
\kappa=0
for
a_{m-1}
and
a_{m}^{\prime}
, and we are done by applying the above.

Finally, suppose
\kappa=2
. Then
\mu=\varpi a_{m}\phi(a_{m})-a_{m-1}\phi(a_{m-1})
, and the claim is immediately checked in both cases,
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})<\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1})
and the opposite.
∎

Now we proceed case by case.

Case
b=\dot{c}_{0}

The slope is
\lambda=0
;
i.e.
,
\ord_{\varpi}(\beta_{i})\geq 0
for all
i
. One computes
\beta_{1}=a_{1}+\gamma
and
\beta_{2}=a_{2}+\phi(a_{1})\gamma-\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}
.
As
\ord_{\varpi}\left(\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}\right)=0
, Lemma
7.5
implies
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq 0
for
i=1,2
. Using this as the first step, and computing
\beta_{i}=a_{i}-\frac{\mu\phi^{2}(a_{i-2})}{\phi(\mu)}+\phi(a_{i-1})\gamma
for
3\leq i\leq m-2
, we conclude by induction (using Lemma
7.5
) that
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq 0
for all
1\leq i\leq m-2
. Next, we have
\beta_{m-1}=a_{m-1}a_{m}-\frac{\mu\phi^{2}(a_{m-3})}{\phi(\mu)}+\phi(a_{m-2})\gamma
, which (using the above) implies that
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1}a_{m})\geq 0
. Thus, if
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1})=\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})
, we deduce that this number is at least
0
, and we are done according to (
7.14
). Finally, suppose
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1})\neq\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})
. We have
\beta_{m}=-\left(1+\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}\right)\phi(a_{m-2})+a_{m-1}\phi(a_{m-1})+\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}\phi(a_{m})\phi^{2}(a_{m})
, which shows (as
\ord_{\varpi}(\beta_{m})\geq 0
) that
\ord_{\varpi}\left(a_{m-1}\phi(a_{m-1})+\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}\phi(a_{m})\phi^{2}(a_{m})\right)\geq 0
. As the two summands have different valuations by assumption, both valuations must be non-negative, which implies
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1}),\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})\geq 0
. We are done by (
7.14
).

Case
b=\dot{c}_{1}

The slope is again
\lambda=0
, so that
\beta_{i}\geq 0
for all
i
. We have
\beta_{1}=a_{1}+\varpi\gamma
,
\beta_{2}=a_{2}-\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}+\varpi\phi(a_{1})\gamma
and
\beta_{i}=a_{i}-\frac{\mu\phi^{2}(a_{i-2})}{\phi(\mu)}+\varpi\phi(a_{i-1})\gamma
for
3\leq i\leq m-2
. As in the case
b=\dot{c}_{0}
, we deduce
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq 0
for
1\leq i\leq m-2
. We have
\beta_{m-1}=a_{m-1}a_{m}+\varpi\phi(a_{m-2})\gamma
. Using Lemma
7.5
, we deduce that if
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1})=\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})-1
, then this number must be non-negative, and we are done according to (
7.14
). Assume
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1})\neq\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})-1
. We compute
\beta_{m}=-\left(1+\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}\right)\phi(a_{m-2})+\varpi a_{m-1}\phi(a_{m-1})+\varpi^{-1}\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}\phi(a_{m})\phi^{2}(a_{m})
, and it follows that
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m-1}),\ord_{\varpi}(a_{m})-1\geq 0
.

Case
b=\dot{c}_{2}

The slope is
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
, so that for any
i
,
\ord_{\varpi}(\beta_{i})\geq m-\frac{i}{2}
, or equivalently
\ord_{\varpi}(\beta_{i})\geq m-\lfloor\frac{i}{2}\rfloor
. We have
\beta_{1}=\varpi^{m-1}(a_{1}+\gamma)
,
\beta_{2}=\varpi^{m-2}(a_{2}+\gamma\phi(a_{1})-\varpi\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)})
,
\beta_{i}=\varpi^{m-i}(a_{i}+\gamma\phi(a_{i-1})-\varpi\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}\phi^{2}(a_{i-2}))
for
3\leq i\leq m-2
,
\beta_{m-1}=a_{m-1}a_{m}+\varpi\gamma\phi(a_{m-2})-\varpi^{2}\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}\phi^{2}(a_{m-3})
and
\beta_{m}=-\varpi(1+\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)})\phi(a_{m-2})+a_{m-1}\phi(a_{m-1})+\frac{\mu}{\phi(\mu)}\phi(a_{m})\phi^{2}(a_{m})
. As in the previous two cases, we deduce that (
7.14
) holds.

In the following cases, we do not give all details as the computations are similar to (and easier than) those in Section
7.4.4
.

7.4.5. Case
\boldsymbol{a_{m-1}\neq 0}
,
\boldsymbol{a_{m}=0}

In this case (
7.16
) gives
\varphi^{m}(v)=\frac{1}{\phi(a_{m-1})}(u-\varphi^{2}(u))
. By Lemma
7.4
this implies that
u
is a cyclic vector for
V
. Apply
\varphi^{m}(\cdot)
to (
7.15
), and insert the above expression of
\varphi^{m}(v)
in terms of
u
to eliminate
v
. This gives an expression
\varphi^{2m}(u)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{2m-1}\beta_{i}\varphi^{i}(u)
. Making all
\beta_{i}
explicit for
1\leq i\leq m
and using Lemma
7.3
, one immediately deduces (
7.14
).

7.4.6. Case
\boldsymbol{a_{m-1}=0}
,
\boldsymbol{a_{m}\neq 0}

This case is completely analogous to the one in Section
7.4.5
(one has
\varphi^{m-1}(v)=\frac{1}{a_{m-1}}(u-\varphi^{2}(u))
, from which the cyclicity of
u
and an explicit formula
\varphi^{2m}(u)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{2m-1}\beta_{i}\varphi^{i}(u)
follow).

7.4.7. Case
\boldsymbol{a_{m-1}=0}
,
\boldsymbol{a_{m}=0}

By Lemma
7.4

v
is a cyclic vector for the
(2m-2)
-dimensional subisocrystal with
L
-basis
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m-3}
. The result follows easily from Lemma
7.3
by looking at the coefficients of (
7.15
).

7.4.8. Case
\boldsymbol{a_{m-1}\neq 0}
,
\boldsymbol{a_{m}\neq 0}
,
\boldsymbol{\mu=0}

(Note that
\kappa\neq 2
in this case.) As
a_{m-1}\neq 0
, (
7.15
) along with Lemma
7.4
show that the vectors
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m-2}
are linearly independent. Further, following the same steps as in the first lines of Section
7.4.4
and exploiting
\mu=0
, we arrive at an expression
\frac{a_{m}}{\phi(a_{m-1})}\varphi^{2m-1}(v)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{2m-2}\beta_{i}\varphi^{i}(v)
, thus showing that
v
is a cyclic vector for the
(2m-1)
-dimensional subisocrystal generated by it. Proceeding as in the previous cases, one verifies (
7.14
). This finishes the proof in type
D_{m}
.

7.4.9. Proof of Lemma
5.6
for type
\boldsymbol{D_{m}}

The action of
c
on
\Phi^{+}
is as follows: for
1\leq i<j<m-1
,
c(\alpha_{i\pm j})=\alpha_{(i+1)\pm(j+1)}
; for
1\leq i\leq m-2
,
c(\alpha_{i-(m-1)})=\alpha_{1+(i+1)}
; for
1\leq i\leq m-2
,
c(\alpha_{i\pm m})=\alpha_{(i+1)\mp m}
; for
1\leq i\leq m-2
,
c(\alpha_{i+(m-1)})\in\Phi^{-}
,
c(\alpha_{(m-1)\pm m})\in\Phi^{-}
.

Set
r=m+1
;
{}_{m+1}=\{\alpha_{i+(m-1)}\}_{i=1}^{m-2}\cup\{\alpha_{(m-1)-m},\alpha_{(m-1)+m}\}
;
{}_{m}=\{\alpha_{i+j}\colon 1\leq i<j\leq m\}\cup\{\alpha_{(m-1)-m},\alpha_{(m-1)+m}\}
;
{}_{m-1}={}_{m}\cup\{\alpha_{i-m}\}_{i=1}^{m-2}\cup\{\alpha_{i+m}\}_{i=1}^{m-2}
; for
1\leq i_{0}\leq m-2
,
{}_{i_{0}}={}_{m-1}\cup\{\alpha_{i-j}\colon 1\leq i<j\leq m-1\text{ and }j>i_{0}\}
.

7.5. Type
\boldsymbol{{}^{2}A_{n-1}}

7.5.1.

Let
n\geq 2
. Let
m=\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor
, so that
n=2m+1
if
n
odd and
n=2m
if
n
is even. Let
V_{0},e_{i}
be as in Section
7.1.1
.
Let
{\mathbf{G}}
be the unitary group attached to the standard hermitian form on
V_{0}\otimes_{k}k_{2}
, relative to the unramified extension
k_{2}/k
of degree
2
.
We may identify
{\mathbf{G}}\otimes_{k}k_{2}={\bf GL}_{n}(V_{0}\otimes_{k}k_{2})
, so that for any
{\mathbb F}_{q}
-algebra
R_{0}
with
\widetilde{R}={\mathbb W}(R_{0}\otimes_{{\mathbb F}_{q}}\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q})[1/\varpi]
, the Frobenius
\sigma
on
{\mathbf{G}}(\widetilde{R})={\bf GL}_{n}(V_{0})(\widetilde{R})
is given by

g\longmapsto\sigma(g)=J\sigma_{0}(g)^{-1,T}J^{-1},

where
\sigma_{0}
is the Frobenius on
{\bf GL}(V_{0})(\widetilde{R})
corresponding to the natural
k
-structure on
{\bf GL}(V_{0})
,
(\cdot)^{T}
is transposition and
J\in{\bf GL}_{n}(V_{0})(k)
is given by
e_{i}\mapsto e_{n+1-i}
(
1\leq i\leq n
).

7.5.2.

Let
{\mathbf{T}},{\mathbf{B}},\beta_{i},\alpha_{i-j}
be as in Section
7.1.2
. Note that
{\mathbf{T}},{\mathbf{B}}
are both
k
-rational (with respect to
\sigma
). Moreover, the action of
\sigma
on
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})
is by multiplication with
-1
. Thus,
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}=1
if
n
is odd, and
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\cong{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
, with the non-trivial element represented by
\bar{\beta}_{i}
for any
i
, when
n
is even. We have an isomorphism
{\mathbb Z}\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
,
1\mapsto\text{class of $\beta_{1}$}
, under which
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})\subseteq\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
corresponds to
n{\mathbb Z}
. The action of
\sigma
corresponds to multiplication by
-1
. Justifying Remark
7.1
in this case, we deduce from this that the induced map
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\cong{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}\rightarrow{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}\cong\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
is an isomorphism when
n
odd and is
0
when
n
even.

The sets
\Phi
and
\Delta
are the same as in Section
7.1.2
. The action of
\sigma
on
\Delta
is given by
\sigma\colon\alpha_{i-(i+1)}\mapsto\alpha_{(n-i)-(n-i+1)}
.
We have the Coxeter element

(7.18)

c=s_{\alpha_{1-2}}\cdot\dots\cdot s_{\alpha_{m-(m+1)}}=(1,2,\dots,m,m+1).

We consider the lift
\dot{c}_{0}
of
c
to
{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
, given by
\dot{c}_{0}(e_{i})=e_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq m
,
\dot{c}_{0}(e_{m+1})=e_{1}
and
\dot{c}_{0}(e_{i})=e_{i}
for the remaining
i
. If
n=2m
is even, we also consider the lift
\dot{c}_{1}
given by
\dot{c}_{1}(e_{i})=\dot{c}_{0}(e_{i})
for
i\neq m+1
,
\dot{c}_{1}(e_{m+1})=\varpi e_{1}
. Then
\dot{c}_{0}
,
\dot{c}_{1}
are basic, and if
n
is even,
\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}(\dot{c}_{0})=0
,
\bar{\kappa}_{\mathbf{G}}(\dot{c}_{1})\neq 0
in
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
. Thus, if
n=2m+1
is odd, the quasi-split group
{\mathbf{G}}
(represented by the pair
({\mathbf{G}},\dot{c}_{0})
) has no non-trivial inner forms; if
n=2m
is even, there are two inner forms, represented by the pairs
({\mathbf{G}},\dot{c}_{\kappa})
with
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
.

We identify
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}}^{\ad},\breve{k}}
with
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\ad})_{\mathbb R}
by sending
0
to the
\sigma
-stable hyperspecial point (“the origin”) corresponding to the standard lattice
\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}e_{i}
.

The group
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-}
is generated by the root subgroups attached to
\alpha_{i-1}
(
2\leq i\leq m+1
). For a
\breve{k}
-algebra
R
, any element
y\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(R)
is an element of
{\bf GL}(V_{0})(R)
, determined by
e_{1}\mapsto e_{1}+\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda=1}^{m}a_{\lambda}e_{\lambda+1}
,
e_{i}\mapsto e_{i}
(
2\leq i\leq n
) for some
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in R
.

7.5.3.

Let
b=\dot{c}_{\kappa}
with
\kappa=0
if
n
odd and
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
if
n
even. We compute that
{\bf x}_{b}=0
if
\kappa=0
and that
{\bf x}_{b}
is the image of
\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda=1}^{m}\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\lambda}-\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda=m+2}^{n}\frac{1}{2}\beta_{\lambda}
under
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})_{\mathbb R}\twoheadrightarrow X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}}^{\ad})_{\mathbb R}\cong{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}}^{\ad},\breve{k}}
if
\kappa=1
(and
n=2m
even). Let
g\in\dot{X}_{b,b}({\mathfrak f})
. Then (
7.4
) holds with
y
as described in Section
7.5.2
, depending on some
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in L
. We have to show that
y\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\bf x}_{b}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
, which is (as in Section
7.1.3
) equivalent to

(7.19)

\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq 0\quad\forall\,1\leq i\leq m

(independently of what
\kappa
is).

Let
c^{\spl}=c\sigma(c)
, and let
\dot{c}^{\spl}_{\kappa}=\dot{c}_{\kappa}\sigma(\dot{c}_{\kappa})
. We write
b^{\spl}
for
\dot{c}^{\spl}
if
b=\dot{c}_{\kappa}
. We have the automorphism
b\sigma\colon g\mapsto\dot{b}\sigma(g)
of
L{\mathbf{G}}
(regarded as a sheaf on
\Perf_{\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q}}
). As
\sigma^{2}=\sigma_{0}^{2}
(recall the identification in Section
7.5.1
), we have

(7.20)

(b\sigma)^{2}=b^{\spl}\sigma^{2}=b^{\spl}\sigma_{0}^{2}.

Let
V=V_{0}\otimes_{k}L
, and denote by
\sigma_{0}\colon V\rightarrow V
the Frobenius automorphism
v\otimes\lambda\mapsto v\otimes\phi(\lambda)
. Then
\varphi:=b^{\spl}\sigma_{0}^{2}\colon V\rightarrow V
is a
\phi^{2}
-linear automorphism of
V
. Moreover,
(V,\varphi)
is an isocrystal over
{\mathfrak f}
(relative to
{\mathbb F}_{q^{2}}
) of slope
0
.

For
1\leq i\leq n
, let
g_{i}:=g(e_{i})
. Then
\sigma_{0}^{2}(g)(e_{i})=\sigma_{0}^{2}(g_{i})
. Using (
7.20
), (
7.4
) applied twice gives the equation

(7.21)

b^{\spl}\sigma_{0}^{2}(g)=g\cdot\left(y\cdot{}^{b}\sigma(y)\right)\cdot b^{\spl}.

Applying both sides of this to
e_{i}\in V
and using the above, for
1\leq i\leq n
, we get

(7.22)

\varphi(g_{i})=g\cdot\left(y\cdot{}^{b}\sigma(y)\right)\cdot b^{\spl}(e_{i}).

7.5.4.

Assume that
n=2m+1
is odd (and
b=\dot{c}_{0}
). Then

c^{\spl}=(1,2,\dots,m,m+1,2m+1,2m,\dots,m+3,m+2),

b^{\spl}
is its lift with all entries
1
or
0
, and
y\cdot{}^{b}\sigma(y)
is given by
e_{1}\mapsto e_{1}+\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda=1}^{m}a_{i}e_{i+1}-\phi(a_{m})e_{2m+1}
;
e_{i}\mapsto e_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq m+1
and
i=2m+1
;
e_{i}\mapsto e_{i}-\phi(a_{2m+1-i})e_{2m+1}
for
m+2\leq i\leq 2m
. Put
v:=g_{1}
. Applying (
7.22
) for all
i\neq m+2
, we deduce that
g_{i}=\varphi^{i-1}(v)
for
1\leq i\leq m+1
,
g_{i}=\varphi^{3m+2-i}(v)+\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda=1}^{2m+1-i}\phi^{1+2(2m-i-\lambda+1)}(a_{\lambda})\phi^{3m+2-i-\lambda}(v)
for
m+2\leq i\leq 2m+1
. As
g\in{\bf GL}(V_{0})(\breve{k})
, the set
\{g_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2m+1}
is a basis of
V
. This easily implies that the set
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m}
is a basis of
V
;
i.e.
,
v
is a cyclic vector for
(V,\varphi)
. Now, applying (
7.22
) with
i=m+2
and inserting the values of
g_{i}
, we deduce

\varphi^{2m+1}(v)=v+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\varphi^{i}(v)-\phi(a_{m})\varphi^{m+1}(v)-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=m+2}^{2m}\phi^{1+2(i-m-1)}(a_{2m-i+1})\varphi^{i}(v).

Applying Lemma
7.3
, we deduce (
7.19
).

7.5.5.

Assume that
n=2m
is even. Then

c^{\spl}=(1,2,\dots,m-1,m,2m,2m-1,\dots,m+3,m+2),

c^{\spl}_{0}
is its lift with all entries
1
or
0
, and
c^{\spl}_{1}
is given by
c^{\spl}_{1}(e_{m})=\varpi^{-1}e_{2m}
,
c^{\spl}_{1}(e_{m+2})=\varpi e_{1}
,
c^{\spl}_{1}(e_{i})=c^{\spl}_{0}(e_{i})
for all
i\neq m,m+2
. Next,
y\cdot{}^{b}\sigma(y)
is given by
e_{1}\mapsto e_{1}+\sumop\displaylimits_{\lambda=1}^{m}a_{i}e_{i+1}-\varpi^{-\kappa}\phi(a_{m-1})e_{2m}
;
e_{i}\mapsto e_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq m
and
i=2m
;
e_{m+1}\mapsto e_{m+1}-\phi(a_{m})e_{2m}
;
e_{i}\mapsto e_{i}-\phi(a_{2m-i})e_{2m}
for
m+2\leq i\leq 2m-1
. Put
v:=g_{1}
and
u:=g_{m+1}
. Applying (
7.22
) for all
i\neq m+1,m+2
, we deduce
g_{i}=\varphi^{i-1}(v)
for
1\leq i\leq m
;
g_{i}=\varpi^{\kappa}\varpi^{3m-i}(v)+\varpi^{\kappa}\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{2m-i}\phi^{1+2(2m-i-j)}(a_{j})\varphi^{3m-i-j}(v)
for
m+2\leq i\leq 2m
. As in the previous cases, from this description we deduce the following.

Lemma 7.6
.

The set
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m-2}\cup\{u\}
is a basis of
V
.

Inserting the above values for
g_{i}
into (
7.22
) for
i=m+1
and
i=m+2
, we deduce

(7.23)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=u-\varphi(u)-\varpi^{\kappa}\phi(a_{m})\varphi^{m}(v),

(7.24)

\displaystyle\varphi^{2m-1}(v)

\displaystyle=v+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m-1}a_{i}\varphi^{i}(v)-\phi(a_{m-1})\varphi^{m}(v)-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=m+1}^{2m-2}\phi^{1+2(i-m)}(a_{2m-1-i})\varphi^{i}(v)+a_{m}u.

If
a_{m}=0
, Lemma
7.6
shows that
v
is a cyclic vector for the
(2m-1)
-dimensional isocrystal generated by it. Then (
7.24
) and Lemma
7.3
finish the proof by showing formulas (
7.19
).

We may now assume
a_{m}\neq 0
. Dividing (
7.24
) by
a_{m}
, we obtain an expression of
u
as a linear combination of
\{\varphi^{i}(v)\}_{i=0}^{2m-1}
, with
\varphi^{2m-1}(v)
appearing with non-zero coefficient. Then Lemma
7.6
shows that
v
is a cyclic vector for
V
. Moreover, inserting this expression of
u
into (
7.23
) and multiplying with
a_{m}
, we eliminate
u
and obtain an expression of the form
\varphi^{2m}(v)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{2m-1}\gamma_{i}\varphi^{i}(v)
with fixed
\gamma_{i}\in L
. By Lemma
7.3
we have
\ord_{\varpi}(\gamma_{i})\geq 0
for all
i
. We then compute that
\gamma_{2m-1}=1-\frac{a_{m}}{\phi^{2}(a_{m})}\phi^{1+2(m-1)}(a_{1})
,
\gamma_{i}=\phi^{1+2(i-m)}(a_{2m-1-i})-\frac{a_{m}}{\phi^{2}(a_{m})}\phi^{1+2(i-m)}a_{2m-i}
for
m+1\leq i\leq 2m-2
and
\gamma_{m}=-\varpi^{\kappa}a_{m}\phi(a_{m})+\phi(a_{m-1})-\frac{a_{m}}{\phi^{2}(a_{m})}\phi^{2}(a_{m-1})
. Using induction, it follows from these equations that
\ord_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq 0
for
1\leq i\leq m
. This shows (
7.19
).

7.5.6.

Suppose that
n=2m
is even and
b=\dot{c}_{\kappa}
(
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
) as in Section
7.5.3
. We have the central cocharacter
\zeta=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\beta_{i}\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})
, whose image in
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\cong{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
is non-trivial but which maps to
0\in\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
. To finish the proof in the
{}^{2}A_{n-1}
-case (
cf.
the discussion after Lemma
7.2
), we have to show that for all
g\in X_{b\varpi^{\zeta},b}({\mathfrak f})
, we have
g^{-1}\sigma_{b}(g)\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})_{{\mathbf{x}}_{b}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})\varpi^{\zeta}
. As in (
7.4
), we can write

(7.25)

b\sigma(g)=gy\varpi^{\zeta}b

with
y\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(L)
depending on
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in L
as described in Section
7.5.2
. We thus have to show that (
7.19
) holds for these
a_{i}
. However, doing the same computation as in Section
7.5.3
(with
b^{\spl}
and other notation as there), we see that

b^{\spl}\sigma_{0}^{2}(g)=(b\sigma)^{2}(g)=gy\varpi^{\zeta+\sigma(\zeta)}{}^{b}\sigma(y)b^{\spl}

(where we used that
\varpi^{\zeta}
and
\varpi^{\sigma(\zeta)}
are central). As
\zeta+\sigma(\zeta)=0
, we arrive simply at (
7.21
) and may proceed as there (and in Section
7.5.5
) to deduce (
7.19
).

7.5.7. Proof of Lemma
5.6
for type
\boldsymbol{{}^{2}A_{n-1}}

The action of
c
on
\Phi^{+}
is as follows: for
1\leq i<j\leq m
,
c(\alpha_{i-j})=\alpha_{(i+1)-(j+1)}
; for
1\leq i\leq m
and
m+2\leq j\leq n
,
c(\alpha_{i-j})=\alpha_{(i+1)-j}
and
c(\alpha_{(m+1)-j})=\alpha_{1-j}
; for
m+2\leq i<j\leq n
,
c(\alpha_{i-j})=\alpha_{i-j}
; for
1\leq j\leq m
,
c(\alpha_{(m+1)-j})\in\Phi^{-}
. The action of
\sigma
on positive roots is given by
\sigma(\alpha_{i-j})=\alpha_{(n+1-j)-(n+1-i)}
.

We set
r=m+2
;
{}_{m+2}=\{\alpha_{(m+1)-j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
;
{}_{m+1}={}_{m+2}\cup\{\alpha_{i-j}\colon 1\leq i\leq m\text{ and }m+2\leq j\leq n\}
; for
m\geq i_{0}\geq 2
,
{}_{i_{0}}={}_{i_{0}+1}\cup\{\alpha_{i-i_{0}}\}_{i=1}^{i_{0}-1}\cup\{\alpha_{(n-i_{0})-j}\}_{j=n-i_{0}+1}^{n}
;
{}_{1}=\Phi^{+}
.

7.6. Type
\boldsymbol{{}^{2}D_{m}}

7.6.1.

Let
m\geq 4
,
V_{0}
,
Q
be as in Section
7.4.1
. Consider the closed subgroup
{\bf GSO}(V_{0},Q)\subseteq{\bf GL}(V_{0})
, which was denoted by
{\mathbf{G}}
in Section
7.4.1
. It is
k
-split, and its derived group is of type
D_{m}
. First, suppose
\charac k\neq 2
. Fix some
\Delta\in{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times}
which is not a square in
k
, and fix a square root
\sqrt{\Delta}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}^{\times}
. Let
h_{0}\in{\bf GL}_{2m}(V_{0})(k)
be given by
h_{0}(e_{i})=e_{i}
for all
i\neq\pm m
and
h_{0}(e_{\pm m})=(-2\Delta)^{\pm 1}e_{\mp m}
. Now suppose
\charac k=2
. Then let
h_{0}\in{\bf GL}_{2m}(V_{0})(k)
be given by
h_{0}(e_{i})=e_{i}
for all
i\neq\pm m
and
h_{0}(e_{\pm m})=e_{\mp m}
. In this section we let
{\mathbf{G}}
be the
k
-group
{\bf GSO}(V_{0},Q)_{h_{0}}
in the sense of Section
2.1.3
. In particular, for any
{\mathbb F}_{q}
-algebra
R_{0}
with
\widetilde{R}={\mathbb W}(R_{0}\otimes_{{\mathbb F}_{q}}\overline{{\mathbb F}}_{q})[1/\varpi]
, the geometric Frobenius on
{\mathbf{G}}(\widetilde{R})={\bf GSO}(V_{0},Q)(\widetilde{R})\subseteq{\bf GL}(V_{0})(\widetilde{R})
is given by

g\longmapsto\sigma(g)=h_{0}\sigma_{0}(g)h_{0}^{-1},

where
\sigma_{0}
is the Frobenius on
{\bf GL}(V_{0})(\widetilde{R})
corresponding to the natural
k
-structure on
{\bf GL}(V_{0})
.

Lemma 7.7
.

The derived group of the
k
-group
{\mathbf{G}}
is of type
{}^{2}D_{m}
.

Proof.

First suppose
\charac k\neq 2
. Let
Q^{\prime}\colon V_{0}\rightarrow k
be the quadratic form
Q^{\prime}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}(a_{i}e_{i}+a_{-i}e_{-i})\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m-1}a_{i}a_{-i}+a_{m}^{2}-a_{-m}^{2}\Delta
. It is non-split of Witt index
m-1
; hence
{\bf SO}(V_{0},Q^{\prime})
is of type
{}^{2}D_{m}
. It suffices to construct a
k
-isomorphism
{\bf GSO}(V_{0},Q^{\prime})\cong{\mathbf{G}}
. Let
h_{1}\in{\bf GL}(V_{0})(\breve{k})
be the element given by
h_{1}(e_{\pm i})=e_{\pm i}
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
and
h_{1}(e_{m})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\Delta}}e_{m}+\frac{1}{2}e_{-m}
,
h_{1}(e_{-m})=\sqrt{\Delta}e_{m}-\Delta e_{-m}
. Then
h_{1}^{T}\sigma_{0}(h_{1})^{-1,T}=h_{0}
. If
Q
(resp.
Q^{\prime}
) denotes the matrix representing
Q
(resp.
Q^{\prime}
) with respect to the fixed basis of
V_{0}
(so that
Q(v)=v^{T}Qv
for all
v\in V
), one immediately checks that
h_{1}Qh_{1}^{T}=Q^{\prime}
. This means that
v\mapsto h_{1}^{T}v\colon(V,Q^{\prime})\rightarrow(V,Q)
is a
\breve{k}
-isometry (
i.e.
,
Q^{\prime}(v)=Q(h_{1}^{T}v)
) and that
\Int(h_{1}^{T,-1})\colon{\bf GL}(V_{0})_{\breve{k}}\rightarrow{\bf GL}(V_{0})_{\breve{k}}
restricts to a
\breve{k}
-rational isomorphism
\Int(h_{1}^{T,-1})\colon{\bf GSO}(V_{0},Q)_{\breve{k}}\rightarrow{\bf GSO}(V_{0},Q^{\prime})_{\breve{k}}
. (Indeed, if
g\in{\bf GSO}(V_{0},Q)
, then
g^{t}Qg=Q
and one computes using
h_{1}Qh_{1}^{T}=Q^{\prime}
that
\Int(h_{1}^{T,-1})(g)^{T}\,Q^{\prime}\,\Int(h_{1}^{T,-1})(g)=Q^{\prime}
.) For
\widetilde{R}
as above, this isomorphism transfers the geometric Frobenius
\sigma_{0}
of
{\bf GSO}(V_{0},Q^{\prime})(\widetilde{R})
to the Frobenius
\sigma
given by

\sigma(g)=\Int(h_{1}^{T,-1})\left(\sigma_{0}(\Int(h_{1}^{T,-1})(g))\right)=\Int(h_{0})\left(\sigma_{0}(g)\right).

Now suppose
\charac k=2
. Let
Q^{\prime}\colon V_{0}\rightarrow k
be the quadratic form
Q^{\prime}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}(a_{i}e_{i}+a_{-i}e_{-i})\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m-1}a_{i}a_{-i}+a_{m}^{2}+a_{m}a_{-m}+\Delta a_{-m}^{2}
, where
\Delta\in{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times}
is such that
X^{2}+X+\Delta\in k[X]
is irreducible. It is non-split of Witt index
m-1
; hence
{\bf SO}(V_{0},Q^{\prime})
is of type
{}^{2}D_{m}
. Let
\lambda\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\breve{k}}^{\times}
be a root of
X^{2}+X+\Delta
. Let
h_{1}\in{\bf GL}(V_{0})(\breve{k})
be given by
h_{1}(e_{i})=e_{i}
for
i\neq\pm m
,
h_{1}(e_{m})=e_{m}+\lambda e_{-m}
,
h_{1}(e_{-m})=e_{m}+\lambda^{-1}\Delta e_{-m}
. Then
h_{1}
induces a
\breve{k}
-isometry between
(V,Q^{\prime})
and
(V,Q)
. We may conclude as above, computing that
h_{1}^{T}\sigma_{0}(h_{1})^{-1,T}=h_{0}
. ∎

7.6.2.

Via the identification
{\mathbf{G}}\otimes_{k}\breve{k}\cong{\bf GSO}(V_{0},Q)_{\breve{k}}
, we can use
{\mathbf{T}},{\mathbf{B}}
,
\varepsilon_{i}\in X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
(
0\leq i\leq m
) from Section
7.4.2
. The induced action of
\sigma
on
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{T}})
is given by
\sigma(\varepsilon_{i})=\varepsilon_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
,
\sigma(\varepsilon_{m})=-\varepsilon_{m}
,
\sigma(\varepsilon_{0})=\varepsilon_{0}-\varepsilon_{m}
. It follows that
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}}^{\ad})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\cong{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}
, generated by the class of
\varepsilon_{0}
.

We have isomorphisms
{\mathbb Z}\oplus{\mathbb Z}/2{\mathbb Z}\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
,
(1,0)\mapsto\varepsilon_{0}
,
(0,1)\mapsto\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}
and
{\mathbb Z}\,\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vskip-2.4pt}}\,X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})
,
1\mapsto 2\varepsilon_{0}-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}\varepsilon_{i}
. The inclusion
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
corresponds to the map
1\mapsto(2,1)
if
m
is odd and to
1\mapsto(2,0)
if
m
is even. Moreover,
\sigma
acts on
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})
as the identity, and the action on
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})
is given by
(a,b)\mapsto(0,(a\,\modd\,2)+b)
. Justifying Remark
7.1
in this case, one easily deduces that the induced map
X_{\ast}({\mathbf{Z}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\cong{\mathbb Z}\rightarrow{\mathbb Z}\cong\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
is multiplication by
2
, hence injective.

We have the roots
\alpha_{\pm i\pm j}
of
{\mathbf{T}}
in
{\mathbf{G}}
as in Section
7.4.2
. The
\sigma
-action on the simple roots fixes all
\alpha_{i-(i+1)}
(
1\leq i\leq m-2
) and interchanges
\alpha_{(m-1)-m}
and
\alpha_{(m-1)+m}
. Thus we obtain a Coxeter element

(7.26)

c=s_{\alpha_{1-2}}\cdot\dots\cdot s_{\alpha_{(m-1)-m}}=(1,2,\dots,m-1,m)(2m,2m-1,\dots,m+2,m+1).

We consider two lifts
\dot{c}_{0}
,
\dot{c}_{1}
of
c
to
{\mathbf{G}}(\breve{k})
. For
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
,
\dot{c}_{\kappa}
is given by
\dot{c}_{\kappa}(e_{i})=\varpi^{\kappa}e_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
,;
\dot{c}_{\kappa}(e_{m})=\varpi^{\kappa}e_{1}
;
\dot{c}_{\kappa}(e_{-i})=e_{-(i+1)}
for
1\leq i\leq m-1
;
\dot{c}_{\kappa}(e_{-m})=e_{-1}
. Then
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(\dot{c}_{0})=0
,
\kappa_{\mathbf{G}}(\dot{c}_{1})=\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}
in
\pi_{1}({\mathbf{G}})_{\langle\sigma\rangle}
. Thus the
{\mathbf{G}}_{\dot{c}_{j}}
(
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
) are the two inner forms of
{\mathbf{G}}
.

We use the
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathbf{T}}^{\ad},\breve{k}}
as in Section
7.4.2
. The group
{}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-}
is generated by root subgroups attached to
\alpha_{i-1}
(
2\leq i\leq m
). Explicitly, any element
y\in({}^{c}{\mathbf{U}}\cap{\mathbf{U}}^{-})(R)
is an
R
-linear map in
{\bf GL}(V_{0})(R)
given by
e_{1}\mapsto e_{1}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=2}^{m}a_{i-1}e_{i}
;
e_{i}\mapsto e_{i}
for
2\leq i\leq m
and
i=-1
;
e_{-i}\mapsto e_{-i}-a_{i-1}e_{-1}
for all
2\leq i\leq m
.

7.6.3.

Let
b=\dot{c}_{\kappa}
with
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
. We compute
{\mathbf{x}}_{b}=0
if
\kappa=0
and
{\bf x}_{b}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}(-\frac{m}{4}+\frac{i}{2})\varepsilon_{i}
if
\kappa=1
. Let
g\in\dot{X}_{b,b}({\mathfrak f})
. Then (
7.4
) holds, with
n
as described in Section
7.6.2
, depending on some
a_{1},\dots,a_{m-1}\in L
. We have to show that
n\in{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathbf{x}}_{b}}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
, which is (as in Section
7.1.3
) equivalent to

(7.27)

\ord\nolimits_{\varpi}(a_{i})\geq\frac{\kappa i}{2}\quad\text{for $1\leq i\leq m-1$.}

Let
V=V_{0}\otimes_{k}L
. Let
\sigma_{0}
be the
\phi
-linear automorphism
x\otimes\lambda\mapsto x\otimes\phi(\lambda)
of
V
. Consider the
\phi
-linear automorphism
\varphi=bh_{0}\sigma_{0}
of
V
. Then
(V,\varphi)
is an isocrystal over
{\mathfrak f}
of slope
\frac{\kappa}{2}
.
Equation (
7.4
) gives
bh_{0}\sigma_{0}(g)=gybh_{0}
. Applying this to
e_{i}
for
1\leq\pm i\leq m
, we get
\varphi(g_{i})=gybh_{0}(e_{i})
, where we write
g_{i}=g(e_{i})
(and where we use
\sigma_{0}(g)(e_{i})=\sigma_{0}(g_{i})
). Writing
v:=g_{1}
, we deduce
g_{i}=\varpi^{1-i}\varphi^{i-1}(v)
and
g_{-i}=-(2\Delta)^{-1}\varpi^{\kappa(1-m)}\left(\varphi^{m-1+i}(v)+\sumop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{i-1}\phi^{i-j-1}(a_{j})\varphi^{m+i-j-1}(v)\right)
for
1\leq i\leq m
. Inserting this into the equation attached to
\varphi(g_{m+1})=gybh_{0}(e_{m+1})
gives

\varphi^{2m}(v)=\varpi^{\kappa m}v+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m-1}\varpi^{\kappa(m-i)}a_{i}\varphi^{i}(v)-\sumop\displaylimits_{i=m+1}^{2m-1}\phi^{i-m}(a_{2m-i})\varphi^{i}(v).

It is easy to see that
v
is cyclic for
V
. Using Lemma
7.3
, (
7.27
) follows.

7.6.4. Proof of Lemma
5.6
for type
\boldsymbol{{}^{2}D_{m}}

The action of
c
on
\Phi^{+}
is as follows: for
1\leq i<j<m
,
c(\alpha_{i\pm j})=\alpha_{(i+1)\pm(j+1)}
; for
1\leq i\leq m-1
,
c(\alpha_{i+m})=\alpha_{1+(i+1)}
; for
1\leq i\leq m-1
,
c(\alpha_{i-m})\in\Phi^{-}
. Moreover,
\sigma(\alpha_{i\pm j})=\alpha_{i\pm j}
if
j\neq m
and
\sigma(\alpha_{i\pm m})=\alpha_{i\mp m}
.

Set
r=m+1
;
{}_{m+1}=\{\alpha_{i-m}\}_{i=1}^{m-1}
;
{}_{m}={}_{m+1}\cup\{\alpha_{i+j}\colon 1\leq i<j<m\}
;
{}_{m-1}={}_{m}\cup\{\alpha_{i+m}\}_{i=1}^{m-1}
; for
1\leq i_{0}\leq m-2
,
{}_{i_{0}}={}_{i_{0}+1}\cup\{\alpha_{i-(i_{0}+1)}\colon 1\leq i<i_{0}+1\}
.
